## G: grazing paddock–gypsy kettle

### grazing paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a land area specifically set aside for grazing.

>  The green illustrated has been prepared by merely rolling and mowing an area of an ordinary grazing paddock.. <i>(Aug 15, 1939,NZ Journal of Agriculture,152</i>)

### grazing right
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the legal opportunity to graze Crown-owned land.

>  In Otago, DOC has only gained about 38% of the land that has been given to continued grazing rights over some 18% of this new conservation land, including mountain-tops and fragile alpine herbfields. <i>(May 2002,Conservation News,1</i>)

### grazing run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pastoral property [usually with a low carrying-capacity. ]

>  Two-thirds of this number are on small grazing-runs leased by men who winter their ewes on farms within a few miles from their runs. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,99</i>)

>  It is on the grazing-runs, where stock are seen only at irregular intervals, that difficulty [with cattle ticks] is experienced. <i>(Nov 21, 1927,NZ Journal of Agriculture,297</i>)

>  Included in this list are four small grazing-runs which carry a few hundred ewes for eight months of the year and are grazed in conjunction with plain lands. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,104</i>)

### grazing-ground
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) pastoral land.

>  Of all those stations possessing country above 3,000 ft, the grazing-ground is more or less sharply divided off into what is called 'summer country' and 'winter country.' <i>(Jun 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7</i>)

### grazing-value
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the extent to which land and cover supports stock. (See [feeding-value](../F#feeding-value))

>  It is however, only on the more fertile aspects of hill country in general that crested dogstail presents this early spring feed and adds so to the attractive appearance and grazing-value of the pasture at that period. <i>(Oct 20, 1926,NZ Journal of Agriculture,248</i>)

### grease boil
 
 <i>n.</i> a boil caused by contact with grease in a sheep's wool; cf mutton boil. [(? farming, ? freezing works)]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Honi Reki is pretty bad with grease boils. <i>(1926,Butcher Shop, The,73</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> [check quot.] <i>(1956,Wool Away!,52</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a localised infection on arms and legs from which shearers suffer when in contact with damp, greasy wool.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> If sheep's grease, being slightly poisonous, enters the pores of the skin, grease boils and skin troubles arise. The shearer, however, may contract grease boils, rheumatics and chills through shearing wet sheep. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,14</i>)

>  <b>WATERSON</b> Wet sheep could not be shorn as the shearers would develop grease boils on their arms and legs. <i>(1999,Some Buggers I've Seen,70</i>)

>  Showering immediately after work is important if the gang has to travel a long way, especially for those who are prone to grease boils. <i>(2002,Wool Grower,Spring 23</i>)

### grease up to
 
 <i>v.</i> act sycophantically towards

>  "I don't have the patience and I'm really bad at greasing up to people." <i>(Mar 2005,North & South,71</i>)

### grease-spot
 
 <i>n.</i> a disease of passion fruit.

>  <i>(Feb 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,179/2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bacterial disease of passion fruit.

>  Grease-spot (Phytomonas L passiflorae): Whilst brown-spot is apparent generally in the months of February and March, grease-spot is more noticeable in the winter months. <i>(Mar 15, 1943,NZ Journal of Agriculture,169</i>)

### greaser
 
 <i>n.</i> a term of abuse for a useless or crawling person. [1960 18C M B11 Nelson Boys C. 25 GREAZERS (FOR ABUSE-SCAB) -->1984 15 F E1 Pakuranga Coll. 10 GREASER,A [a useless person]]

### greasies
 
 <i>n.</i> shearing clothes

>  greasies: Shearing clothes. This is an appropriate name for clothing which has become impregnated with grease from the wool. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/24</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
 <i>n.</i> Takeaway fried food, esp. fish (etc.) and chips, cooked in fat [1984 16 F E7 Pakuranga Coll. 22B GREASIES -->1984 17 F E48 Pakuranga Coll. 22B GREASIES -->1984 16 F E3 Pakuranga Coll. 22B GREASIES (GREASYS) -->1984 17 M E99 Pakuranga Coll. 22A GREASIES (GREASYS) -->1984 17 M E97 Pakuranga Coll. 22B GREASY GRUB]

>  [Hdg] Taking the grease out of 'greasies'. Health experts are out to cut the grease from the Friday night "greasies". [They] are starting a national survey of chip cooking practices. <i>(Jun 11, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

>  We all know that a packet of greasies and a stubby or a tube are the perfect match. <i>(Jul 7, 2001,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Unpack the boot and head for the locally famous fish and chip shop, which is packed, but get a load of greasies that we eat in the car... <i>(Jan 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  Only with his design work, the results stand the test of time to be shown years later in an exhibition, rather than being used to wrap greasies. <i>(Aug 6, 2003,Dominion Post,A13</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> greasies: a derogatory name for breaded and deep-fried FISH AND FRENCH FRIES. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,48</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Across the road from Joe's [cafe] ... a gang of callow youths eat their greasies and swig rudely from bottles of beer, dining out a la country road. <i>(1979,Skin Deep,131</i>)

>  <i>(1980,Heinemann Dictionary of NZ Quotations,474</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> Fish and chips steaming, a bottle of sherry,... ...`Only, paid for the sherry. And the greasies.' <i>(1982,Other Halves,73</i>)

>  Greasies off menu in Elsdon [headline] Fish and chips were off the menu in Elsdon, Porirua, last night after Elsdon Takeaways caught fire. <i>(Apr 12, 1986,Evening Post,60</i>)

>  This delicacy of the English-speaking world is referred to in other circles as `shark and taties', `Maori roast', or `greasies' - understanding all depends on knowing the convention. Once the convention is established, it is just a matter of stating one's preference for two meat patties and a scoop, over a stuffed frankfurter. <i>(Aug 1986,Metro,194</i>) [NOTES: [quoting Imogen de la Bere, NZ Listener ?date]]

>  The Works closed at four, which meant at least two hours before Danny could expect them [the parents] - and the `kai' - greasies probably, and his stomach sent out signals. <i>(Dec 1986,Landfall,160/491</i>) [NOTES: (Smither)]

>  Kiwis go for greasies FISH AND CHIPS still rates tops as New Zealanders' most popular takeaway meal. <i>(Sep 4, 1987,Dominion,7</i>)

>  Last night Margie arrived at Celia and Rob's house with greasies, ... <i>(Dec 1989,Landfall,172/468</i>)

### greaso
 
 <i>n.</i> a `smooth' type , `city slicker'. [(? ephemeral slang) Cf bunny, Cuba Street Yank, slicker; cf greaser. -->ca1949]

### greasy
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep with a fleece full of grease.

>  greasy - sheep with wool with plenty of grease. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearer.

>  greasy - a shearer. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [-->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 16 GREASY, COME A]



 
 <i>n.</i> a fish and chip seller; cf greasies

>  ...a hurried meal of fish and chips, grabbed hastily from a greasey's shop on the main road. <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,129</i>) [NOTES: 1970]



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] unwashed with fleeces full of grease.

>  <b>ORMOND</b> Greasy ewes 3592, washed ewes 2976, paddock ewes 170. <i>(Jan 26, 1865,JD Ormond Stockbook 1864-1866</i>)

>  <b>ORMOND</b> A good shearing day - got through 1431 sheep - all greasy ewes with exception of stragglers from the wether flock. <i>(Jan 18, 1865,JD Ormond Stockbook 1864-1866</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) station cook.

>  <b>MILLS</b> When men loved dogs like women, and mustering the hills was more than signing on as a 'greasy' or scrubbing the cookhouse floor. <i>(1966,Huntaway,203</i>)

>  greasy - a station cook. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### greasy as a butcher's apron
 
 <i>phr.</i> phrases:(as) greasy as a butcher's apron, prick, pup. [ca1966 July Tirimoana Hotel, Marlborough: Harold `Horse' Prentice, Publican warned: The road to Picton's as greasy as a butcher's prick. ca1972 Wellington of a football field, `She's as greasy as a butcher's apron.]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Grimy as a sweep, greasy as a butcher's pup manhandling ammunition, but oh I feel fine, fit as a buck rat. <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,19</i>)

### greasy heel
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a condition of a horse's feet.

>  Greasy heel (a different complaint from "grease") does affect light horses, and can be treated by application of zinc ointment. <i>(Sep 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,249</i>)

>  I notice a reference to it [stamping] in the May journal (page 411) where the cause is deemed to be greasy heel. <i>(Sep 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,248</i>)

### greasy wool
 
 <i>n.</i> farming: unprocessed wool as shorn from the sheep, still retaining its natural greases and impurities; wool as shorn from the sheep and containing the natural impurities of wax, suint, and dirt [perhaps place at aj GREASY.] Cf in the grease. [(woolgrowers')]

>  Packed bale of greasy wool. put up washpool in Wedderburn. <i>(Nov 15, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  Greasy wool: Wool as shorn from the sheep and containing the natural impurities of wax, suint, and dirt. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)

>  greasy wool n. N.Z. & Austral. untreated wool, still retaining the lanolin, which is used for waterproof clothing. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,488</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 329; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 216]]

>  Greasy wool unscoured shorn wool. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) untreated or unscoured raw wool, containing approximately 25% grease.

>  <b>RENNIE</b> Greasy is the term for wool in the condition in which it is shorn from the sheep, when about 25% of the weight of the fleece is wool grease. New Zealand exports raw wool in three forms, (1) greasy (2) scoured and (3)slipe. <i>(1988,Agriculture in NZ,13</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> .. a store for greasy wool which was hoisted to the sorting shed. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,70</i>)

### great
 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in names of plants and animals see bulrush; buttercup, mountain; buttercup, white; grebe, great crested; groundsel, white; sedge, shark; shark, white; sow-thistle, spike-rush.

### Great Auks
 
 <i>n.</i> the Auckland rugby team

>  The score might be best erased from the memory but Horowhenua rugby people won't forget in a hurry the "buzz" created in Levin on Saturday by the Great Auks. <i>(Apr 5, 1993,Evening Post,1</i>)

### great blue shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [not NZ specific]

>  The heart of a foetal foal, the heart of a great blue shark recently mounted, and the alimentary canals of a skate and a sting-ray were also shown. <i>(Aug 13, 1886,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Mango-pounamu, Prionae glauca great blue shark.</i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,178</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GREAT BLUE SHARK ... <i> Other names: Prionace glauca </i> ; blue shark, blue whaler shark, blue pointer; mango-pounamu (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,178</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> ~ mango-pounamu <i> Prionace glauca </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> GREAT BLUE SHARK [<i> Prionace glauca </i> ] ... Other names include blue whaler shark, blue-pointer, and mango-pounamu (Maori) <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,10</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> GREAT BLUE SHARK <i> Carcharinus mackiei </i> : An uncommon species also known as Blue Whaler, rarely taken by game fishermen. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,215</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GREAT BLUE SHARK ... <i>Other names: Prionace glauca</i>; blue shark, blue whaler shark, blue pointer; mango-pounamu (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,178</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> GREAT BLUE SHARK [<i>Prionace glauca</i>] ... Other names include blue whaler shark, blue-pointer, and mango-pounamu (Maori) <i>(1987,Marine Fishes,10</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BLUE SHARK Prionace glauca</i> (Oceanic blue shark, blue pointer, blue whaler) <i>(1982,Collins Guide,59,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] blue shark .. [scientific name] <i>Prionace glauca </i>.. [other name(s)] blue whaler shark <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] whaler, blue 240, 253, 256 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### Great Chameleon
 
 <i>n.</i> a nickname for Winston Peters

>  Perhaps someone should tell the Great Helmsman that his mate, the Great Chameleon, is confusing us common folk. <i>(Jul 18, 1997,Dominion,8</i>)

### great colonial adjective
 
 <i>phr.</i> i.e. bloody

>  Two witnesses in the two-up cases at Wellington mentioned that `bastard' was a common expression among the habitue's of the `school'. The word is usually preceded by the great colonial adjective, which flourishes in the vocabulary of assorted sizes and degrees of luridity. <i>(Aug 24, 1907,NZ Truth,5</i>)

### Great Debater
 
 <i>n.</i> (ironic) nickname of Dr Don Brash, former National Party leader

>  We've had the Great Helmsman. Now we've got the Great Debater.It's the latest moniker attached to Opposition leader Don Brash who started his leadership as "24/7" - an ironic reference to his inaccessibility after promising reporters he would field calls at all hours of the day and night. The Great Debater is not meant any more kindly. It's Winston Peters' latest stratagem to call attention to Dr Brash's absences from Parliament. <i>(Nov 20, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### Great Divide
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> Great Divide common name for the Southern Alps which run down the middle of the South Island of New Zealand <i>(1984,Blackball 08,243</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary)]

### great doer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm animal that is economical to feed but performs extremely well in constitution and/or production of milk, meat or wool.

>  The blacks are great doers on the hills. <i>(Sep 1, 1925,NZ Farmer,1361</i>)

>  They [Gwavas cattle] are very strong in point of constitution and are great doers. <i>(Sep 1, 1925,NZ Farmer,1380</i>)

>  The calves are great doers, coming off their mothers at nine months topping 300 kg. <i>(Jan 24, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,11</i>)

### Great Easter Bunny Hunt
 
 <i>phr.</i> an annual South Island contest to reduce rabbit numbers

>  The Hare Raising Mutineers, of Southland, won the Great Easter Bunny Hunt after bagging 1119 rabbits at the weekend. <i>(Apr 13, 2004,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  The annual Great Easter Bunny Hunt in Central Otago showed how far rabbit numbers had fallen since the rabbit haemorrhagic disease was illegally introduced in Otago in August 1997. <i>(Apr 15, 2004,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

### Great Exodus
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAWSON</b> [1893] was to be a memorable [year] for all New Zealand. The country was slowly recovering from the dreadful days of unemployment which led to the Great Exodus - a name given to the migration of thousands of people from New Zealand to Australia - in the late '80's. <i>(1951,Gold in their Hearts,93</i>)

### Great Fleet
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Fleet](../F#fleet))

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The myth of the "great Fleet" has pervaded New Zealand pre-history since late last century. <i>(1983,Illustrated History of Taranaki,15</i>)

>  No, there was no Great Fleet, and no separate Moriori people preceding the Maori to New Zealand. These things were largely the creation of 19th-century-Pakeha scholars working from limited and flawed data. <i>(Mar 8, 2003,Dominion Post,F13</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Organized Maori migration to New Zealand began about 1250 and ended with the arrival of the `Great Fleet' of canoes one hundred years later. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),24</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> Then, about 1350, the `Great Fleet' arrived in New Zealand. <i>(1968,Early Fiordland,22</i>)

>  <b>SORRENSON</b> But as Anne Salmond has pointed out, at hui on maraes throughout the country, the Great Fleet lives on: ... <i>(1979,Maori Origins and Migrations,86</i>)

>  Also included is a somewhat tiresome reiteration of D R Simmon's case, now more than 20 years old, against the notion of a Great Fleet. <i>(Nov 12, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78</i>)

>  But I think a lot of mischief has been done by Percy Smith in giving the impression of a great fleet, that bore down on New Zealand in the fourteenth century. <i>(1988,Na To Hoa Aroha</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata) 1933 Mar 22 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 3/74]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The concept of a `Great Fleet' is a post-European romanticisation and without traditional warrant ... <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,1</i>)

### Great Helmsman
 
 <i>n.</i> a nickname for former PM Jim Bolger [supposedly bestowed on JB by political correspondents after a trip to China]

>  What I did not anticipate was that then Prime Minister Jim Bolger would take the proposal as some sort of personal affront - but then the Great Helmsman took most things I did personally in those days. <i>(Dec 3, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  On his way to the White House earlier this year, Bolger, nicknamed the Great Helmsman by his staff, called in to San Diego and was offered the 17th man spot for one of the Louis Vuitton races by Peter Blake. <i>(May 27, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  [Hdg] Helmsman Jim steers Kiwis along the path of stability... In his own, shrewd, King-Country-cocky way, the Prime Minister has quietly assimilated the first rule of Great Helmsmanship : "Don't rock the boat." <i>(Mar 1, 1996,Independent Herald (Wellington),15</i>)

>  The electorate is asked.. to accept that [these moves] are an example of the great helmsman at the wheel "steady as she goes". <i>(Mar 17, 1996,NZ Tablet,24</i>)

>  Even if the Great Helmsman and all the rest of caucus threw themselves under a Seatoun bus, he would not want the job... [Mr Bolger] has taken the role of Great Helmsman to heart and appears determined to lead National into the next election. <i>(May 6, 1997,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  His ability to steer past dangerous reefs and shoals earned him the satirical title of the Great Helmsman. <i>(Nov 9, 1997,Sunday Star Times,C4</i>)

>  [Hdg] The Great Helmsman Seeks Landfall in Unknown Seas. <i>(Sep 19, 1997,Independent (Auckland),12</i>)

>  We've had the Great Helmsman... The Great Helmsman was, of course, Jim Bolger - whose extremely high opinion of his own talents was not universally shared. <i>(Nov 20, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  It stripped the gloss away from the history-making mission of the Prime Minister to the U.S. No wonder the Great Helmsman was furious. <i>(Apr 2, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Members of the Port Nicholson Yacht Club were delighted to be able to rub shoulders with Team New Zealand at a special reception, also attended by the Great Helmsman himself, Jim Bolger. <i>(Jun 2, 1995,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Oh the smile on the face of Bolger... Clap Hands for the Helmsman and small cheers for the crew. <i>(Jun 4, 1995,NZ Tablet,20</i>)

>  In the unlikely event of the Great Helmsman being granted his heart's desire and New Zealand becoming a republic.. we could still get a holiday on the first weekend in June. <i>(Jun 12, 1995,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Sir Michael Fay was appearing before parliamentary select committee. Here, The Great Helmsman told that conclave of MPs.. that repeated attacks on his reputation would have left him jobless and unemployable, had he not been a self-employed, rich merchant banker. <i>(Aug 11, 1995,Independent (Auckland),32</i>) [NOTES: now transferred from Jim Bolger to any leader (have seen another example but forgot to note it)]

>  The External Assessments Bureau.. has told Jim Bolger that there is more background radiation in New Zealand than at Mururoa so what price the Great Helmsman's crocodile tears about Wednesday's explosion. <i>(Sep 10, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  The Great Helmsman proved himself the Great Provider at last week's South Pacific Forum meeting. Jim Bolger returned from an early morning fishing trip with a respectable-sized Spanish mackerel. <i>(Jul 19, 1995,Dominion,6</i>)

>  The surprise.. is this is happening under the leadership of Jim Bolger, the King Country farmer satirised by some as the Great Helmsman. <i>(Oct 29, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Confident in his new exalted status, the Great Helmsman was even prepared to be generous about the full employment strategy released by Labour earlier in the week. <i>(Nov 4, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  There is a strong rumour.. that the Great Helmsman is considering the possibility of forecasts for marginal electorates being read first. <i>(Nov 25, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  Why has Jim Bolger, mockingly known in Wellington as the Great Helmsman, for so long failed to fire the imagination of a nation? <i>(Oct 1995,Metro,84</i>)

### great Kiwi knocking machine
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <i>Country Calendar'</i>s exceptional success must not only be due to the fact that each programme is always interesting, but in a country with a thriving "knocking machine" it is always warm and positive. <i>(Apr 4, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  The great Kiwi knocking machine is quick to respond when performance is poor, but we are not always so good at congratulating ourselves on a job well done. <i>(Jan 13, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  The great kiwi knocking machine vented its spleen after it did not get an invitation but, as the philosopher of science, Thomas Kuhn said, you can't know about something unless you become a practitioner. <i>(Apr 13, 2000,City Voice,23</i>)

### Great Migration
 
 <i>n.</i> [Cf. Great Fleet]

>  <b>Buck</b> The great migration (heke) from Hawaiki is the most famous event in Maori history, because all the tribes trace their aristocratic lineages back to the chiefs of the voyaging canoes. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,36</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> It has proved (in my opinion) conclusively that the Maoris from whom the leading tribes claim descent, those ancestors said to have arrived in the Arawa, Tainui, Aotea, and other canoes of the Great Migration, were certainly not aborigines of New Zealand, even if there were other Maoris or other inhabitants resident on these islands when the Hawaiki canoes arrived. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,560</i>)

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> [Hdg] THE GREAT MIGRATION ABOUT A.D. 1350. <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),20</i>)

### great New Zealand novel, the
 
 <i>phr.</i> an outstanding work of fiction that many readers feel they are still waiting for, but for which there will probably never be agreement.

>  Should established writers leave a budding genius alone in his or her attic to write the Great New Zealand Novel or should we teach creative writing to anyone who wants it? <i>(Nov 28, 2008,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.berylfletcher.co.nz/2008/ii/28Is it possible to teach creative writing?]

>  Too busy trying to write the great New Zealand novel, not to mention the great, nutfree, perfectly balanced lunch box. <i>(Jul 2008,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.poppyshock.com/make/sarahsdirtylaundry.]

>  In the 1950s, he [Bill Pearson] wrote .. Coal Flat, which many people thought was the "great New Zealand novel" the country had been waiting for. <i>(Apr 2010,North & South,96</i>)

### Great Railway Pie
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The buffet's open, boasting Canterbury Lamburger, Goldminer's Lunch, a selection of New Zealand cheese and, of course, The Great New Zealand Railway Pie. <i>(Feb 1989,North & South,19</i>)

>  Railways people will feed travellers such delicacies as the Great Railways Pie "served with stuffed potato, coleslaw, tea or coffee" ($5.00)... <i>(Feb 13, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Geology)

>  <i>Phalacrocorax novae-hollandiae</i>, Stephens. (Great Sea-shag.) <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/196</i>)

### great shark
 
 <i>n.</i> great white shark (See [shark](../S#shark))

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Great S.- <i>Carcharias maso. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,412</i>)

### Great South Road
 
 <i>n.</i> the main road south from Auckland

>  By 1859, even the rough track through the Maori kainga (village) at Rangiriri and along the east bank of the river as far as Ngaruawahia was being dignified with the name "Great South Road." <i>(Jan 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 61/59</i>)

### great spotted kiwi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Among the Okarito and great spotted kiwi, the females play a much greater role in the incubation process, regularly taking turns on the egg. <i>(Jan 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 45/91</i>)

>  [Caption]... great spotted kiwi - at 45 cm the largest species - can present a formidable mien, and some birds will charge human intruders in their forest fiefdoms in north-west Nelson and the West Coast. <i>(Jan 2000,NZ Geographic,72</i>)

>  Great spotted kiwi is the first species to be reintroduced to the Rotoiti recovery area. <i>(Jul 2004,Conservation Action,12</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> GREAT SPOTTED KIWI <i>Apteryx haasti Maori name</i>: Roa. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,20</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> There are three species of kiwi, divided into five races: the North Island kiwi (<i>Apteryx australis mantelli </i>... ); the South Island tokoeka (<i>A.a. australis</i>); the Stewart Island Kiwi (<i>A.a. lawryi </i>... );the little spotted kiwi of west and southern South Island (<i>A. owenii</i>);and the great spotted kiwi from more northern parts of the South Island (<i>A. haastii</i>). <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,250</i>)

>  <i>3. Apteryx haastii</i> Potts, 1872 <i>Great Spotted Kiwi (Roa)</i> South Island, chiefly west of the divide ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,15,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Great Spotted Kiwi Apteryx haastii </i>POTTS, 1872 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Roa, large grey kiwi. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,39</i>)

>  A fourth species of kiwi had been discovered... Till now scientists had recognised three species - the great spotted kiwi or roa in the northwest of the South Island, the little spotted kiwi on Kapiti and a few other islands, and the brown kiwi, scattered from Northland to Stewart Island. <i>(Apr 23, 1993,Dominion,3</i>)

>  There is, evidently, a great spotted kiwi called Pete living on the West Coast that specialises in hit-and-runs on people who invade his patch. <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### great white shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [great white shark, aka white pointer - universal term, not NZ specific but NZ waters a 'hot spot' for the species]

>  <i>Carcharodon carcharias</i> Linnaeus. The great white shark ... is an occasional visitant. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/235</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Carcharodon carcharias</i> (Linnaeus). WHITE-POINTER or GREAT WHITE SHARK. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,9,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>GREAT WHITE SHARK Carcharodon carcharias </i>(White death, white pointer, man-eater) <i>(1982,Collins Guide,52,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>White shark Carcharodon carcharias</i> Alternatively white pointer, great white, maneater, and similar combinations. World wide in tropical to temperate waters ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,25</i>)

>  <i>Carcharadon carcharias</i>. On account of the pure white colouring of its under-surface, this species is usually referred to as `white pointer' or `white shark.' <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/269</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Carcharadon carcharias</i>. On account of the pure white colouring of its under-surface, this species is usually referred to as `white pointer' or `white shark.' <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,9,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Carcharodon carcharias</i>: White shark, white pointer. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> ...we have to record some of the voracious man eating types, including the notorious Great White Shark. <i>Ibid.</i> 192 Three sharks built for speed are the Mako, the Porbeagle and the Great White Shark. ... The Porbeagle (<i>Lamna nasus</i>) has long sharp teeth, each with a small cusp at the base on either side; the Mako (<i>Isurus glaucus</i>) has teeth of similar shape without the cusps, and the Great White Shark (<i>Carcharodon carcharias</i>) has large triangular teeth with serrated edges. - <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,191</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 191]

>  This delicacy of the English-speaking world is referred to in other circles as `shark and taties', `Maori roast', or `greasies' - understanding all depends on knowing the convention. Once the convention is established, it is just a matter of stating one's preference for two meat patties and a scoop, over a stuffed frankfurter. <i>(Aug 1986,Metro,194</i>) [NOTES: [quoting Imogen de la Bere, NZ Listener ?date]]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Mango taniwha, Carcharidin carcharias, white pointer shark</i>. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,378</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> <i>Other names: Carcharodon carcharias</i>; great white shark, white pointer, man-eater; mango-tuatini (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,185</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> WHITESHARK [<i>Carcharodon carcharias</i>] ... White pointer, great white shark, and maneater, are widely used alternative names, Mango-tuatini is Maori and not in regular use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,12</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> WHITE POINTER: see <i>Whiteshark</i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,225</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> WHITE POINTER: see <i>Whiteshark</i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,225</i>)

### grebe
 
 <i>n.</i> astral spirit

>  <b>MACRAE</b> `Real heavy stuff Someone was out to hex us. I saw the grebes they [the gays] sent hanging round our old flat.' <i>(1988,Awful Childhoods,136</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Podiceps</i> spp.

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> Seal killed a Paradise Flapper on a Lagoon - ... John killed a couple of Grebe which he got out ... <i>Podiceps</i> sp. <i>(Dec 27, 1856,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,60</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Dec 27 Phillips Rockwood Journal [TS] 60]

>  <b>HAAST</b> The water of the lake was perfectly clear ...; a few grebes (<i>Podiceps rufipectus</i>), were swimming upon it, and gave life to the otherwise solitary and tranquil scenery. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,133,?1866 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The Grebe [<i>Podiceps cristatus</i>] <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,241</i>) [NOTES: ca1899 (1957) Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 241 (Birds S. Westland)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Grebe, ... common English bird-name of the genus <i>Podiceps</i>. [1888 Buller] <i>(1898,Austral English,174</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> THE GREBE (<i>Podiceps rufipectus</i>) [title] Two and sometimes three pair of Grebe bred on Tutira Lake in the 'eighties and two ... pair still continue to do so ... . <i>(1921,Tutira,?19?? ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I never saw the rare shoveller duck except as a wee lad. My father called them shovel-bills ... I did not see the grebe. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,121</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Australian Little Grebe Tachybaptus novaehollandiae</i> (STEPHENS, 1826). NATIVE OTHER NAMES: <i>Black-throated grebe, Australasian little grebe, Australian dabchick. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,56</i>)

>  [<i>Tachybaptus novaehollandae novaehollandae</i> (Stephens, 1826)] <i>Australian Little grebe ... Now widely distributed in New Zealand but not yet common. </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,18,1980 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>P. cristatus</i>. Often initial caps; big diver, kamana. [1773 cit shows early application in similes of English bird names]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> About 6 a pinguin was seen. About 7 we saw a bird like a <i>crested Grebe</i> or like a <i>Loom</i>, Br. Zool: which made a noise very near like a Goose, ... [ed. fn] Not specifically identifiable. <i>(Feb 9, 1777,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/224</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> On the lakes ... we found the crested grebe (Podiceps cristatus?), of which only very little is known. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,138</i>)

>  A small dab-chick (<i>Podiceps rufipectus</i>)) is common in our freshwater lagoons; and a large crested grebe, hitherto undescribed, inhabits the lakes of the South. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/19</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  PODICEPS HECTORI, Buller. Crested Grebe. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/74</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 104. PODICEPS CRISTATUS. L. Crested Grebe. <i>P. Hectori</i>. BULLER <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,39</i>)

>  I have fully noted the habits of the Crested Grebe ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/209 (or 294)</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Grebe ..... Colymbidae; G. Podiceps; S. P. cristatus (?). A beautiful crested grebe is found about the lakes in the Middle Island. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/338</i>)

>  <i>Podiceps cristatus</i>. - Crested Grebe. South Island; Australia, S. Africa, Asia, North America, Europe. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/117</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> The Crested Grebe is generally-speaking a rare bird in both islands. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/285</i>) [NOTES: [Morris]]

>  ... the divers are represented by the almost cosmopolitan crested grebe and the dabchick. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,197</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kaha</i>, n. <i>Podicipes cristatus, crested grebe. </i> [so 1957/82 <i>Podiceps cristatus</i>] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,96</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The <i>tui</i> and the bell-bird make music here ...; and here too are to be seen wild ducks and that rare and beautiful bird the crested grebe. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 126</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Totokepio is the `little diver' of the settler to distinguish it from the `big diver' (the crested grebe). The Canterbury compiler spells it as given, but in Southland the name is Totokipio, and if taken in sections To-toki-pio it is easy to pronounce. The kamana or crested grebe is not mentioned. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Paateketeke</i>, n.<i>Podiceps cristatus australis, crested grebe. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,270</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> CRESTED GREBE <i>Podiceps australis Maori name</i>: Putekteke. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,27</i>)

>  <b>FRANCIS</b> ... the first thing I spotted swimming and diving near the shore line was a pair of crested grebes, dark- plumaged birds with silvery white underbodies, the characteristic crests on their heads and swimming low in the water. <i>(1983,Wildlife Ranger,80</i>)

>  <i>Southern Crested Grebe Podiceps cristatus australis</i> GOULD, 1844 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Crested grebe, puteketeke. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,57</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  15. <i>Podiceps cristatus </i>(Linnaeus, 1758) <i>P. cristatus australis</i> Gould, 1844 <i>Southern Crested Grebe (Puteketeke)</i> ... Australia and New Zealand. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,18,1980 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> PODICEPS CRISTATUS. (GREAT CRESTED GREBE.) ... [<i>Native name</i>.- Pateketeke. ... [284] The species ... is no doubt identical with that inhabiting Australia ...] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,352,{1888 2nd edn 2/283-284 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The solitude [near Lake Ida] is enlivened by a great number of water-fowl, ... of which the Great Crested Grebe (<i>Podiceps cristatus </i>Lin.) is the most worthy of notice. [Also 1866 Von Haast in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 470 ] <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,138</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Hoary-headed Grebe Podiceps poliocephalus </i>JARDINE & SELBY, 1827 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Hoary-headed dabchick, Tom Pudding</i>. ... Breeds mainly in southern Australia and Tasmania. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,56</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Their long white necks, distinctive ruff of chestnut with black edges about the neck, and double black crest on the top of their heads made them unlike any other bird we had seen. Once home we searched through our book of New Zealand birds and located only a single candidate: the southern (or Australasian) crested grebe, <i>Podiceps cristatus australis</i>, a subspecies of the great crested grebe found in Europe, Africa and Asia. <i>(Mar 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 50/82</i>)

>  <b>57</b> <i>Southern Crested Grebe Podiceps cristatus australis</i> GOULD, 1844 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Crested grebe, puteketeke. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### greek
 
 <i>n.</i> [(child marble-players')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### Greeline
 
 <i>n.</i> breed of sheep

>  The couple have developed the Greeline Composite sheep, a genetic package which at this stage comprises quarter East Friesian, three-eighths Texel and three-eighths Coopworth. ... Dunlop says driving the decision to develop the Greeline is the increasing emphasis being placed on and value being derived from lamb production. ... One of the results of the changing relativities between the returns from lamb meat and crossbred wool has been the development over the past decade of composite breed options such as the Greeline that followed the availability in New Zealand of the Texel and East Friesian. <i>(Oct 2005,Country-Wide,34</i>) [NOTES: Country-wide Stock~land]

>  <b>AYSON</b> Just when it was growing dusk and when Thomas was becoming a little anxious about his companion's ability to reach Galt's at Marairua, they breasted the top of a ridge and saw a beautiful stretch of sloping ridges covered with silver tussock and almond grass, facing the sun. <i>Ibid.</i> 101 All the country was very rough, with scrub, fern and tutu mixed with native grass, especially long green almond grass, which made splendid cattle feed. <i>(1937,Thomas,74</i>)

### green drought
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dry situation where grass appears green but has a low nutrient value.

>  The dire situation in Marlborough and a "green drought" in other parts of New Zealand - where grass has grown but has little nutrient value - has seen feed prices rocket. <i>(Apr 26, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

### green feed disease
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) milk fever or hypocalcaemia in sheep.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> A disease of pregnant or lactating ewes which may occur from six weeks before lambing to six weeks after. It is often associated with a sudden change to green feed, and is sometimes called 'Green Feed' disease. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,34</i>)

### green gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a general name applied to any member of the Naultinus genus of native gecko species each of which has also an individual, usually toponymic, name [<i>Naultinus</i> genus, as opposed to <i>Hoplodactylus</i>.] (See [Auckland green gecko](../A#auckland-green-gecko),[Marlborough green gecko](../M#marlborough-green-gecko),[Nelson green gecko](../N#nelson-green-gecko),[North Cape green gecko](../N#north-cape-green-gecko),[Northland green gecko](../N#northland-green-gecko),[Wellington green gecko](../W#wellington-green-gecko),[West Coast green gecko](../W#west-coast-green-gecko))

>  New Zealand's green geckoes are absolutely harmless. <i>(Dec 21, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>Green Gecko - moko-kakariki</i>, Naultinus elegans <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  Geckos may be various shades of brown or grey, but the North Island green gecko, or kakariki, is the commonest in the Urewera. Its Maori name comes from the chattering sound it makes, like that of its namesakes, the red and yellow-crowned parakeets. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,76</i>)

>  Following 10 years of rat eradication on Codfish Island / Whenua Hou, Rod Hitchmough, a scientific officer with the threatened species science unit, at the Department of Conservation, and a small band of researchers spent two weeks looking for green and cloudy gecko populations. They found just three individuals. <i>(Sep 2010,NZ Geographic,46</i>)

>  DESPITE THEIR vibrant colour schemes, green geckos are almost impossible to see in the wild. <i>(Sep 2010,NZ Geographic,46</i>)

>  NEW ZEALAND is home to two types of geckos, which have generally been described as green (<i>Naultinus</i> genus), and brown (<i>Hoplodctylus</i> genus) geckos. .. ### green-bird
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Stringops habroptilus</i> kakapo [(whalers' and sealers' obs)]

>  Milford [139] Haven is a wild romantic looking place - ... the woods are abundantly supplied with game, as woodhens, green birds, emus &c - these birds are of large size, ... and as they cannot fly, they are easily overtaken with dogs. <i>Ibid.,<i> c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 109 green-bird - ko'kabboo [kakapo] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 138]

>  There are [in West Southland] green parroquets, large hawks, and several kinds of wild ducks, besides Emus, green birds & woodhens, which are birds of an excellent flavour. <i>Ibid.</i> 282 (|= macron precedes) green-bird, ko|abboo [kakapo] <i>(1828,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1828 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 222]

>  Milford Haven [west coast sounds] is a wild romantic looking place, ... the woods are abundantly supplied with game, as woodhens, green birds, emus etc - these birds are of large size, they lay their eggs in holes in the ground ... and as they cannot fly, they are easily overtaken with dogs. <i>Ibid.</i> 104 The birds are not remarkably musical - the linnets are rather so; The Bell-Bird, is of a purple colour, with a white spot on the breast, shaped like a bell. The Saddle back, is a brown bird with a bright red spot on its back like a saddle, these two last species, are about the size of a thrush. There are green parroquets, large hawks, and several kinds of wild ducks, besides Emus, greenbirds and woodhens, which are birds of excellent flavour. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,37,1986 ed.</i>)

>  Another bird, called by the whalers the `green bird,' by the natives the kakapo, is abundant on the west coast. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 261]

 <i>n.</i> . [fish (Local mainly South Island.)] (See [butterfish](../B#butterfish))

An outside fish secured in the vicinity of kelp around the northern coasts during most months of the year. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/117</i>)

>  CORIDODAX PULLUS (Forster). <i>Butterfish; Marari</i>. Sold as `greenbone' or `kelp-salmon' in Dunedin. An outside fish secured in the vicinity of kelp around the northern coasts during most months of the year. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/117</i>)

>  35. <i>Coridodax pullus</i> Forster. Kelpfish, Butterfish, or Greenbone. Maori: Marari <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/145</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <i>Coridodax pullus</i>: Butter-fish (Wellington and Marlborough), green-bone or kelp-fish (Otago). <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <i>Coridodax pullus</i> (Forster). Greenbone (butterfish, kelp-fish, kelp-salmon). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/412</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Under present conditions, the Stewart Island grounds offer ... the usual catch ... blue cod, groper, trumpeter, moki and greenbone. <i>(1940,Rakiura,221</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> An alternative name [for butterfish] is `Greenbone' from the fact that the bones and the flesh in contact with them are stained bright bluish green. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,69</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Greenbone (Butterfish) <i>Coridodax pullus</i> (Bloch and Schn.) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,408</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 408]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GREENBONE, BUTTERFISH, KELP SALMON, or KELPIE (MARARI) <i>Coridodax pullus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) ... The name Greenbone is given on account of the bones being a bright green colour, which is so permanent as to with-stand boiling or bleaching. ... The name kelp-fish is given on account of its habit of feeding on kelp or seaweed, and also because it likes to make its home among kelp. In such a habitat it has both food and a favourite home. Kelp-salmon is given for two reasons. The first part, kelp, is explained in the last paragraph, while the second half, salmon, is because the fish is not unlike a Salmon in shape, so much so that new chums who caught this fish have been under the impression they had caught the protected introduced Salmon, but of that later in this chapter. But why this dark coloured fish should receive the name Butterfish I am unable to say. It is certainly not as some people suggest because it needs butter with it when cooked or eaten. In Auckland the fish known to South Islanders as Spotty (<i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>) is called Butterfish on account of its bright golden colouring on the lower parts of the body and fins fancifully resembling the colour of butter. The name Kelpie, of course, is derived from its habit of living amidst kelp and it is easily understood how the `ie' was tacked on by fishermen. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,261,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> It is unfortunate that the Butterfish [<i>Corridodax pullus</i>] has been given a variety of local names which have originated either from their habit of associating with kelp, their body form, or from the colour of their bones, as for example, Kelpfish, Kelp-salmon, Greenbone, and several other names not so easy to explain. Butterfish or Greenbone are possibly the more generally used names for this fish ... <i>Ibid.</i> 169 Butterfish <i>Corridodax pullus</i> Greenbone; Kelp Salmon; Kelpi; Kelpfish ... Kelpfish <i>Odax vittatus</i> Butterfish; Greenbone; Northern Kelpfish <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,130</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Greenbone .. .. koaea, marari .. .. <i>Corridodax pullus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BUTTERFISH ... <i>Other names: Coridodax pullus</i>; greenbone; marari, rari (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,256</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Parochialism runs strong in most of us, and many will note indignantly the absence of a familiar local name. The difficulty is that Stewart Island butterfish = Wellington spotty, while Wellington butterfish = Stewart Island greenbone. <i>Ibid.</i> 224 Family SCARIDAE includes <i>Odax vittatus</i>, one of many kinds of `kelp fish', and the greenbone (if I may use its apt southern name), otherwise <i>Corridodax pullus</i> ... , a handsome fish with its long graceful fins. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,207</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BUTTERFISH [<i>Corridodax pullus</i>] ... The term greenbone is widely used in southern waters referring to the bone colour. The Maori names marari, and rari are seldom used. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,28</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> BUTTERFISH (a) Scientific name: <i>Corridodax pullus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Butterfish; Greenbone. (c) Other common names: Southen Kelpfish. (d) Principal localities where taken: Wellington, Castlepoint, Makara, Paremata, Nelson, Picton, Bluff and Stewart Island. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,14</i>)

>  Tuamarina: for at east 20 years, the usual term used by Picton fishermen for what are usually in Wellington called `butterfish'. What G.R. would normally term `butterfish' is a fish like a large spottie (=? KELPFISH). (A `kelpie' is something quite different again) <i>(Oct 12, 1972,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: George Routledge,]

>  <b>AYLING</b> BUTTERFISH (Greenbone, marari) <i>Odax pullus (Coridodax pullus, Odax vittatus)</i> ... Small individuals have a markedly different colour pattern from that of the adults and until recently were thought to belong to a separate species named the kelpfish, [266] <i>Odax vittatus</i>. These young fish are yellow-brown with a line of white dashes running down each side ... [Butterfish] are an excellent eating fish, with delicate tasty flesh that prompted the name butterfish ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,265,1884 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] greenbone 1754-62 <i>(1987,Bibliography of NZ Fishes</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAXTER</b> And feel below their ledge's roof/ The tugging greenbone flock. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,158</i>) [NOTES: 1955]

>  [species name] butterfish .. [scientific name] <i>Odax pullus</i> .. [other name(s)] greenbone, southern kelpfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Butterfish Odax pullus</i> ... An alternative name is greenbone. Restricted to New Zealand ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,110</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHARPE</b> A uniform green in colour and the shape of a very deep trout, this tiddler was about a foot long .... but at any rate it was a fish. It soon became obvious that in the clear water below the rocks those little greenbone, if that was their name, were present in force. <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,72</i>)

### greenbottle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PERRY</b> ... there are at least fifteen species of maggot flies in New Zealand, but the chief damage [to sheep] is occasioned by two species, the golden-haired blow fly (<i>Pollenia stygia</i>) and the greenbottle (<i>Lucilia sericata</i>). <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,145</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(watersiders')]

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Blue drew himself up to his full five feet four and took a long, reflective swallow of beer. `Oh, the ones we've got left now aren't too bad. We got shot of all greenbottles earlier [in the Watersiders Union after the 1951 lockout] in the piece. Took a bit of working though. We blokes from the old union had to play it quiet for a while when they let us back on the wharf. And man, were we glad to get back.' <i>Ibid.</i> 82 `WHAT IS A GREENBOTTLE THEN?' I ask ... `Don't know can rightly give you a winning ticket on that one, `said Charley. Sort of like a blue bottle trying to fly on one wing. As Bill, he'll put you right;' Bill blew on his tea while he considered. `Our Clanger's a greenbottle sometimes - and then again sometimes he's not. Come to think of it most people down here act like bottles now and then. Except me of course. Tell what, ask Curly. He's a bit of an intellectual.' For the fourth time I repeated my question. But Curly just smiled and said, `You'll learn, boy. Take a look in the mirror for the starter ...' . <i>(1964,Watersiders,70</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DAVIS</b> These greenbottle new chums sure learn fast,' grunted curly ... . <i>(1964,Watersiders,124</i>)

>  In the South Island oats for greenfeed are mainly confined to sheepfarming areas which have severe winters and dry summers. <i>(Apr 15, 1944,NZ Journal of Agriculture,245</i>)

>  From chou moellier ewes are put on to greenfeed when they have lambed until new grass and lucerne are available. <i>(Jul 1959,NZ Journal of Agriculture,13</i>)

>  After the cereals we direct-drill greenfeed into the stubble. <i>(Sep 4, 1997,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [attrib]

 <i>n.</i> jade

>  <b>BUTLER</b> I have seen the rarest kind of mountain duck swimming within five yards of me ... as though I had no soul for roast duck seasoning. There are several ducks - the paradise duck is commonest. ... Then there are the grey duck and the mountain duck - rarer, less striking in their personal appearance, and better eating. <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,44,1960 ed.</i>)

>  I shot the ducks for Mrs F.G. They are very numerous both grey and paradice [sic], the latter is a very handsome bird. <i>(Apr 8, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,31,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> I don't think a minute passed without a brace of wild ducks flying past, - grey, blue, and Paradise. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,181,1870 ed.</i>)

>  Of aquatic birds I saw the Grey Duck, Brown Teal ... common in New Zealand. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/178</i>) [NOTES: (Travers)]

>  Grey ducks, Paradise ducks, (which are rather a species of geese) teal, and widgeon frequent the streams. ... Blue mountain-duck is found in the more elevated regions. <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,31</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 99. ANAS SUPERCILIOSA. Gml. Grey Duck. Parera. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,36</i>)

>  The following is a list of the birds shot ... <i>Querquedula gibberifrons</i>, little teal; <i>Anas superciliosa</i>, grey duck; ... <i>Graculus carbo</i>, cormorant or black shag; <i>Graculus varius</i>, pied shag; <i>Graculus brevirostris</i>, white throated shag; also another species of shag, locally called the `blue shag' ... pretty highly esteemed by the sailors as an article of food; <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>) [NOTES: (Black Stewart Island)]

>  The Grey Duck in the wild state, is ... less shy than the Paradise Duck ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/207</i>) [NOTES: (Travers NZ Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANAS SUPERCILIOSA. (GREY DUCK.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Parera [and Maunu (Taupo)]. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,245</i>) [NOTES: [1888 2nd edn 2/251]]

>  <b>HAAST</b> Of birds still belonging to the <i>Avifauna</i> of New Zealand, the following species were represented :- <i>Graculus punctatus</i>, spotted shag; <i>Eudyptula undina</i>, small blue penguin; <i>Anas superciliosa</i>, grey duck;<i> Graculus carbo</i>, black shag; <i>Graculus varius</i>, pied shag; <i>Graculus brevirostris</i>, white throated shag; <i>Ossifraga gigantea</i>, nelly; <i>Apteryx australis</i>, large kiwi, <i>Nestor meridionalis</i>, kaka; <i>Stringops habroptilus</i>, kakapo, and some other undetermined species. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,417</i>)

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> At the mouth of the river you might fill your boat with ... the blue, grey and paradise ducks, teal, ... and other birds ... <i>(1879,Spangles and Sawdust,25</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The species we have identified in the Kaipara and Hokianga are - the Putangi, or `paradise-duck' (<i>Casarca variegata</i>); the grey duck, or Parera (<i>Anas superciliosa</i>); the brown duck, an allied species or variety of the last; the Papanga, or `teal,' or `widgeon' (<i>Fuligula N.Z</>.), and some other varieties that may be imported birds, or crosses, or other native species. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/225</i>)

>  A few paradise ducks, usually in pairs, grey ducks and teal, and black swans, were seen on the tarns at Tarndale, and Lakes Guyon and Tennyson. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/355</i>) [NOTES: (Park)]

>  <b>18/117</b> <i>Anas superciliosa</i>. - Parera, Grey Duck. Both Islands; Chatham Islands; Australia, Tasmania, and Polynesia. <i>(1886,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  ANAS SUPERCILIOSA, <i>Gml.</i> - Grey Duck <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/127</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Karakahia ...grey duck. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the Maori Language,51</i>)

>  He has had a rough time of it surveying a block on the ranges up the Wai-o-hine in the Wairarapa ...[sic] Once ...[sic] they had nothing to eat but a tui and a more-pork. ... They were stewing the two birds when a kuku-pa (bush pigeon) flew up and perched. Bob shot it ... This is not exactly what the Britishers call `sport' - but there is more of nature about it than 30 gents in pink and tops ... waiting for a wretched little fox who, when he is killed, is not equal for culinary purposes to a more-pork. Once coming up the Waikato in 1851 your Uncle James got a pot-shot at a lot of parera (grey duck) ... <i>(Mar 22, 1894,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/597,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  As regards the duck family we are also well off, with our paradise ducks, brown ducks, grey ducks, white-winged ducks and blue mountain ducks - the last belonging to a genus (Hymenolaemus) peculiar to New Zealand. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,196</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The paradise duck (<i>putangi-tangi</i>: Casarca variegata) a bird of beautiful plumage, but not of such flavour as the grey duck or teal, was considered a prize. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,105</i>)

>  Of all these creatures ... the grey duck and waxeye seem to be the only survivors, and that in greatly reduced numbers. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,43,1978 ed.</i>)

>  The occurrence ... of a pure-white form of the common grey duck (<i>Anas superciliosa</i>) is of sufficient interest to justify its being put on record. <i>(1915,TrNZI,47/145</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Karakahia ... Nyroca australis, grey duck. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,115</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Parera, ...Anas superciliosa, grey duck. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,310</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Grey duck and putangitangi or paradise duck abound in the creeks, and it is amusing to watch the ... way they sail down over the rapids, ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 56</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Although the Grey Duck on Tutira barely holds its own as a New Zealand species it is safe. <i>(1927,Birds of New Zealand,13</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> On the west side, at the foot of the Pass, lay a little lake, the Lyndon. Paradise duck, grey duck, little grebe, and other water dwellers were enjoying life ... <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,22,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> On and around the [Central Otago] streams there were teal, grey duck, and the blue mountain duck, but the last-named was scarce. `I saw stilts, the black-fellows ... wading about ...' <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,120</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> A moulting bird is called a mounu [sic: ?maunu], and at this season great numbers of paradise and grey ducks and teal were taken. <i>Ibid.</i> 64 Parera - the grey duck, once extremely plentiful. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,14</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> GREY DUCK (<i>Anas superciliosa</i>), Parera of the Maoris, is the common brownish native duck found throughout New Zealand. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80</i>)

>  pa[a]rera, a water-bird (<i>Anas superciliosa</i>), grey-duck: this is also the general name for the north-west wind, but is restricted by the Pakeha to the duck. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> At Ardross the Rosses reared both paradise and grey ducks from eggs and they did well. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,20,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Anas ... superciliosa</i> ... Grey Duck. PARERA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,90</i>)

>  gray duck n : any of various ducks having more or less gray plumage: as d : the black duck (<i>Anas superciliosa</i>) of Australia and New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,993</i>)

>  An odd black-backed gull calls from the lake ... and at night a boss grey duck will quack authoritatively to a few companions. <i>(1962,Landfall Country,326</i>) [NOTES: Kidson `Annus Mirabilis']

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Grey duck .. .. parera, maunu .. .. <i>Anas superciliosa</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> GREY DUCK <i>Anas superciliosa Maori name</i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,90</i>)

>  <b>MIKAERE</b> There were putakitaki (paradise ducks), parera (grey ducks), tataa (brown ducks) and whio (blue ducks). ... The pukeko (swamphen) were caught only as a last resort, because their flesh was stringy and tough. <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,75</i>)

>  ... the light-eyed Teal slunk silently from view; or ... the little Grebe with rosy breast, dived and sported with restless activity; close by a group of sober Grey Ducks; whilst the watchful Paradise Drake basked on the sunny bank above ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/47</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

 <i>n.</i>

Kiwi, Straight-billed Kiwi, Grey Kiwi, Blue hen of the diggers. - The breeding season extends through several months, eggs having been obtained on the West Coast during a considerable portion of the [506] year. <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,505</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

 <i>n.</i>

### Grey Lynner
 
 <i>n.</i> a resident of the Grey Lynn suburb in Auckland.

### grey moray
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> Grey Moray <i>Gymnothorax nubilus</i>. Blue-grey with irregular darker markings... The smallest and most slender New Zealand moray, averaging 40-60cm, reaching about 100 cm. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,47</i>)

### grey mullet
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Mugil cephalus</i>, = kanae

### grey owl
 
 <i>n.</i>

### grey parrot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAYLY</b> During our ramble [in Queen Charlotte</i>'s Sound] I saw Wood Pigeons, Parroquets, Grey Parrots, Poey Birds & Quails & vast variety of singing Birds but no animal great or small or any fruit Trees of any kind whatever. <i>(Nov 15, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 Nov 15 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/213]

>  <b>BAYLY</b> There are great numbers of large grey Parrots that have very beautiful plumage and small Parroquets flying in great plenty so that I frequently killed two or three at one shot. <i>(Apr 12, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 April 12 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/206]

### grey petrel
 
 <i>n.</i>

### Grey Power
 
 <i>n.</i> a lobbying organisation for older people

>  Rotorua's the cradle, if that's the right word, of Grey Power. The movement, officially known as the National Superannuitants Association arose here in 1984 on a tidal wave of elderly rage at the super surcharge imposed by Superann herself, Ann Hercus. <i>(Jul 2, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

### grey power
 
 <i>n.</i> political influence exercised by older people especially in an organised way. [From c1990? In NZPOD and Australian Modern Oxford 1998 (AMO) but not in DNZE or Collins. It may be distinctive NZ and AusE; at least there may be earlier NZ citations.]

### grey racer
 
 <i>n.</i> an elderly person on a mobility scooter [modelled on boy racer]

>  Mrs Shannahan has been using a mobility scooter since she lost both legs in 1999.... Mrs Shannahan said the term "grey racer" was fitting for many who rumbled through Masterton streets. While the most hi-tech scooters could only reach a speed of 10kmh, that was enough to cause harm to pedestrians. <i>(Jul 18, 2003,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

### grey snipe
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Limosa Baueri</i> [Also: the Kuaka]

>  The Kuaka (<i>Limosa Baueri </i>) is the bird spoken of as `curlew' and `grey snipe' by the colonists. Large flocks are to be seen on our rivers, feeding on the mud-banks. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/222</i>)

### grey teal
 
 <i>n.</i> a widespread grey-brown duck [<i>Anas gracilis</i>]

>  The grey teal, it should be pointed out, is a protected bird, and sportsmen should exercise caution when shooting to make sure they are not destroying this and other protected ducks. <i>(May 20, 1933,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  This concentration of ducks affords a good opportunity of ascertaining if any of the rarer species are present in the district but among the concourse of water-fowl in this sanctuary not a single specimen of black teal, brown duck, or grey teal could be seen. <i>(May 25, 1940,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Returned up the Avon in canoe having shot a bittern and grey teal which J.Deans stuffed. <i>(May 21, 1849,Torlesse Papers,79,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The paradise duck (<i>putangi-tangi</i>: Casarca variegata) a bird of beautiful plumage, but not of such flavour as the grey duck or teal, was considered a prize. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,105</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>tete. 1. n. Spatula rhyncotis, spoonbill duck. 2. Elasmonetta chlorotis, brown duck. ... 3. Nettion castaneum, grey teal. ... 4. Fuligula novae-zealandiae, black teal. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,479</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Pateke - the grey teal; ... The name is sometimes spelt `patake'. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Pateke - the grey teal; ... The name is sometimes spelt `patake'. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> GREY TEAL <i>Anas gibberifrons Maori name</i>: Tete. ... Like the Grey Duck has pale throat, but noticeably smaller ... <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,88</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Teal, grey .. .. tete .. .. <i>Querquedula gibberifrons </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>110. Anas gibberifrons gracilis</i> Buller, 1869 <i>Grey Teal </i>Australia, Tasmania and New Zealand ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,37,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Grey Teal Anas gibberifrons gracilis </i>BULLER, 1869 ... OTHER NAME:<i> Tete. ... Native to Australia, New Guinea and New Caledonia. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,145</i>)

>  Grey teal, and the blue mountain or rapid duck, were very plentiful here; there were also large flocks of the red bill or oyster catcher, feeding on the cockles and small mussles [sic] that are laid bare at low water. <i>(Nov 10, 1863,Otago Daily Times,9</i>)

>  Grey teal came darting through, necks bent to check our decoys. <i>(May 29, 2004,Dominion Post,E16</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> The grey teal is a protected bird but is often accidentally killed by duck shooters, mistaking it for the much larger grey duck. <i>(2001,Which New Zealand Bird?,58</i>)

>  Grey teal, blue mountain or rapid duck, together with the red-bill or oyster-catcher, formed another part of the collection. <i>(Feb 15, 1873,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  Up the creeks in the bush grey teal and blue duck were plentiful, but now they are all gone, and the black teal are rapidly going also, and in all probability will soon be simply a memory of the past. <i>(May 23, 1889,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  There are no other ground birds, and no pigeons; but a few kakas, tuis, and mokos, and we have an occasional visit from a pair of Paradise ducks and grey teal. <i>(Oct 25, 1894,Otago Witness,33</i>)

### grey warbler
 
 <i>n.</i> a tiny greenish-grey songbird, Gerygone igata, the favourite host for the eggs and young of the shining cuckoo (also called riroriro) (See [also piripiri](../A#also-piripiri),[teetotum](../T#teetotum),[warchie](../W#warchie))

>  The migratory shining cuckoo.. sometimes visits gardens where it may lay an egg in the nest of the grey warbler, another insect-eating native bird which can live and breed in the garden. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,25</i>)

>  As I enjoyed the last of the light a pair of grey warblers alighted on a branch barely three metres away. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> Even the grey warbler is affected.. riroriro's insistent aching call.. abruptly stops. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,111</i>)

>  Result every other grey warbler in Godzone goes up on the last note, but there is.. a flock of rebel grey warblers near Waikanae which goes down on the last note. <i>(Dec 23, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  GERYGONE ASSIMILIS, Buller. Piripiri. Warbler, Teetotum. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/58</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Warbler, n.</i> This English bird-name is applied loosely to many birds of different genera in Australia and New Zealand. ... In New Zealand, it is now only specifically applied to the - Bush Warbler - <i>Gerygone silvestris</i>, Potts. Chatham Island W.- <i>G. albifrontata</i>, Gray. Grey W.- <i>G. flaviventris</i>, Gray; Maori name, <i>Riro-riro.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,498</i>)

>  <b>PARKER</b> `Grey Warbler (<i>Gerygone flaviventris</i>) also belongs to an Australian genus. It is remarkable for its curious and beautifully formed nest, and as being the foster-parent to the Long-tailed Cuckoo, which lays its eggs in the Warbler's nest.' <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,119</i>)

>  <i>Wari.bler </i>(wari.bl&etilde.r), <i>n. [Add.]</i> In New Zealand various species of the genus <i>Gerygone</i> are called <i>bush warblers, gray warblers,</i> etc. See under GERYGONE. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2033</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> GERYGONE FLAVIVENTRIS. (GREY WARBLER.) ... <i>Native names</i>.- Riroriro and Koriroriro. \{- This is the Grey Warbler, the well -known Riroriro of Maori history and song.\} <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,107,{1888 2nd edn 1/44} ed.</i>)

>  23. <i>Gerygone flaviventris</i>, Gray. Grey Warbler, Teetotum, Piripiri. -The warbler is a true pensile nest-builder ... <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,284</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts]

>  <b>POWELL</b> GREY WARBLER (<i>Pseudegerygone igata</i>), Riroriro of the Maoris, is a small brownish grey bird abundant in all parts of New Zealand. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,86</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Warbler, grey .. .. riroriro .. .. <i>Pseudogerygone igata </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>254. Gerygone igata</i> (Quoy and Gaimard) <i>Grey Warbler (Riroriro) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,67,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Grey Warbler Gerygone igata</i> (QUOY & GAIMARD, 1830) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Riroriro, teetotum. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,278</i>)

>  <b>Moon</b> The grey warbler is the only New Zealand bird to build a hanging nest. <i>(2002,Photographic Guide to Birds of NZ, A,118</i>)

>  They've [sc. shining cuckoo] flown here all the way from the western Pacific to lay their eggs in the nests of the grey warblers hereabouts. <i>(Jan 5, 2004,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  Of the bronze-winged Cuckoo, he tells us that, like its relative at home, it makes use of the nests of other birds, and, of course, relieves itself of the care of providing for its young. No bird appears to suffer so much from the imposition of this parasite as the Piripiri or Grey Warbler. <i>(Jun 25, 1870,Otago Daily Times,3</i>)

>  The grey warbler, or riro-riro, is a pretty little songster, and when it is building its peculiar nest it sings its pleasing song every time it succeeds in placing a feather or cobweb in it. <i>(Apr 7, 1894,Otago Daily Times,1</i>)

>  In an address at New Plymouth on "The Song Birds of New Zealand," Mr. Johannes C. Andersen said that although he had written down 100 songs of the tuis, sixty of the bell-birds, and fifty of the grey warblers, these were only a fraction of the songs it was possible to record. <i>(Mar 23, 1926,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Grey warblers at Mount Egmont have distinctly a different song. <i>(Apr 13, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  Numbers of native birds such as tomtits, white heads, grey warblers and fantails are expected to increase in the Otaki catchment of the Tararua Forest Park ... <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 /11</i>)

>  The trim little grey warbler is also fairly numerous on the island. <i>(Apr 27, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,38</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 24. GERYGONE FLAVIVENTRIS. Gray. Grey Warbler. Riro-riro. <i>G. assimilis</i>, BULLER. <i>G. aucklandica</i>, PELZ. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,10</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... to say nothing of Korimako and Pitoitoi ..., we never saw or heard the notes of either the Piopio, the Tieke, or the Hihi, all of which birds were at one time more numerous even than the Huia. The <i>Zosterops</i> was everywhere abundant, also the Grey Warbler and Rifleman, and ... we found the Tomtit comparatively plentiful; the Parakeet chased its mate through the tree-tops with sharp cries of `twenty-eight'; ... and among the tangle of the underwood the ever-present Flycatcher displayed its pretty fan-like tail. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/14</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> [NB used by Thatcher for street or gutter gratings: possibly the immediate source of SI rural metaphor.GRIDIRON]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> Effect a few improvements in the Town,/ And get the gratings properly laid down./ Not in that curous gridiron style,/ To catch the heels of every poor file -/ <i>Ibid.</i>13 The Tay-street gridirons are a vile delusion and a snare./ Dee-street as yet with gratings I observe is not supplied. <i>(1864,Invercargill Minstrel,8, 13</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>
