 <i>v.</i> [first three cits below are not of verb]

>  The system of great triangulation cast over India is not that of `net-work' as cast over the British Islands, but what is technically termed or `gridiron' `chain series'. <i>(1876,TrNZI,9/97</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <b>EDGE-PARTINGTON</b> This run has been `gridironed', that is, strips have been purchased to protect the owners against selectors. <i>(1883,Random Rot,309</i>)

>  <b>WARDON</b> ... `gridironing' land. <i>(1892,MacPherson's Gully,23</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> [check this cit] <i>(1902,State Experiments,234</i>)

>  <b>E. JOLLIE</b> [Allom made the note when he borrowed the MS from Jollie's wife, and made this comment on Jollie's account of buying the best parts out of a run in 1856] This was probably about the beginning of the grid-iron process of picking the eyes out of the country, so much practised afterwards. <i>(1878,Reminiscences</i>) [NOTES: 1907 July A.J. Allom's marginal note in 1841-1865 E. JOLLIE Reminiscences (1878) (MS;ATB) 39]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> At one time this corner [of the Clarence block], `Stanleyvale', must have been a separate holding. The bulk of the valley bottom has been grid-ironed (freeholded) and the remains of the old homestead and paddocks are still in evidence. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,185</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> Once when the Press was supporting the parliamentary candidature of a member of an old family, a friend said to me: `The Press forgets that that lot `grid-ironed' Canterbury.' `Grid-ironing' was one of the old-time devices by which some landholders so arranged their properties as to keep out land-seeking strangers. Such methods were partially justified by the runholders' insecurity of tenure. <i>(1958,Making of a New Zealander, The,104</i>)

>  The quantity of land so gridironed did not exceed 5000 acres. <i>(Oct 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

>  Where is the case for the charge of "gridironing" if the honourable member himself never intended to gridiron? <i>(Aug 21, 1890,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  <b>PEACHE</b> He had bought in as much land as he could afford to freehold and, as others were taking up the `gridiron' leaseholds, he would have to have a great deal more capital to ensure this would not continue. <i>(1876,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1876 July 4 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 47]



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to purchase strategic parts of land e.g. by roads or rivers, to prevent others from buying.

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> I fully appreciated the emphasis my lawyer had put on the need to secure all thirteen blocks which comprised the 45,000 acres to prevent any neighbouring run-holder from gridironing me out. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,62</i>)

>  The quantity of land so gridironed did not exceed 5000 acres. <i>(Oct 4, 1879,Weekly News,19</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the purchase of land in a gridiron-shaped pattern so that neighbouring or intermediate portions could not be purchased by others.

>  <b>MORRIS</b> A man purchased land in the shape of a gridiron, knowing that nobody would take the intermediate strips, which later he could purchase at his leisure. <i>(1898,Austral English,176</i>)

>  <b>REEVES ed.</b> Mr, now Sir John Hall, then a squatter of squatters, and since reputed, although wrongly, to have been the originator of the 'gridiron' system of land buying. <i>(1883,Canterbury Rhymes,127</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> .. supposed to have invented the 'grid-iron' system of land buying. <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,82</i>)

### gridironed
 
 <i>adj.</i> [(hist.)]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> ... in exchange for the freehold we surrendered round the lake, they [Lands Department] gave us an area of freehold land at Cora Lynn to fill in a gridirioned area behind the old homestead. <i>(1980,Tussock Ranges,169</i>)

### gridironer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) one who purchases land strategically in the shape of a gridiron.

>  <b>BAKER</b> .. the intervening land was rendered useless to another prospective settler and might be bought at the gridironer's leisure. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,26</i>)

### gridironing
 
 <i>n.</i> [1883 citation belongs with the verb to gridiron]

>  <b>OVERTON</b> The system of gridironing in the early days was a practice largely followed by purchasers of land. Strips of land were taken up which made it impossible for an outsider to come in between, thus enabling as man to hold a larger area of country than at the time he could pay for. <i>(1949,Fifty Years Sheep Farming,82</i>)

>  I accused the honourable member of dummyism; I really mean't gridironing. <i>(Aug 21, 1890,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  <b>BRADSHAW</b> Facilities have been used in many cases for the purpose of what is called `gridironing' the country. <i>(1883,New Zealand As It Is,32</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Gridironing ... a term used in the province of Canterbury, New Zealand. A man purchased land in the shape of a gridiron, knowing that nobody would take the intermediate strips, which later he could purchase at his leisure. In other provinces free-selection (q.v.) was only allowed after survey. <i>(1898,Austral English,176</i>)

>  It was thought that the free-trade foolishness had died in New Zealand; that it had been buried with plural voting, the public soup kitchen, the conservative property tax, land gridironing, and other swindles and unpleasantnesses. <i>(Apr 27, 1912,NZ Truth,357/4/4</i>)

>  gridoroning n. system of taking up land in separate parallel strips, to reduce the value of the intervining pieces with the object of acquiring them subsequently at a reduced price. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  <b>MARKS</b> Mr John McKenzie (Seddon's Minister for Lands) ... flourished a colored [sic] map illustrating the `gridironing' that had taken place in Canterbury land. <i>(1934,Memories (Mainly Merry),96</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> Some of the runholders now thought of two new devices to preserve their estates. The first consisted of buying a series of 20 acre sections fronting on a road, [123] so spaced as to leave between them sections of 19 acres. Under the Waste Lands Regulations, none of less than 20 acres could be bought except by having it put up to auction. Few people would go to the trouble of having this done; and anyone who did was in all probability a man of small means, who stood little chance of outbidding the runholder. It usually happened, therefore, that all these 19-acre sections were retained without having to be purchased. This was known as `gridironing.' <i>(1938,High Country,122</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealanders have coined or adapted many expressions to meet local requirements, as illustrated by the following: ... gridironing: purchasing land in strips to oust other potential settlers <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  gridironing: purchase of areas of land leaving small strips between. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> When investigating the change from pastoral lease to freehold in any locality, the student, brought up on Pember Reeves (see e.g. S.E.I. [State Experiments 1902] 234) searches immediately for evidences of the methods by which the runholder retained his grasp of the land. These practices called gridironing and spotting can be defended: there is no need to do so here. If spotting is practiced - the purchase of selected areas with water and shelter for the control this gave over the surrounding country - a study of the Crown grant index maps and of the dates of the original titles supplies no evidence of it. <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,13</i>)

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> `Gridironing' was the practice of buying twenty acre sections fronting on a road, so spaced as to leave sections of 19 acres between them, and as these had to be auctioned the small farmer stood little chance of out bidding the runholder who usually retained them without having to purchase them. `Spotting' was buying up key positions, fertile land, and leaving the barren, water courses, entrances to valleys, road lines ... <i>(1949,Sheep and Sheepmen of Canterbury,94</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> 24 and fn <i>(1952,John Grigg</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Where land was auctioned the system known as `grid-ironing' was useful: the leaseholder would buy a series of sections each of, say, twenty acres along a frontage, leaving perhaps eighteen acres between sections. Since no one but a poor man would want so small an area, the leaseholder could count on outbidding any rival. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,156</i>)

>  gridironing n. purchasing strips of land in shane of gridiron, thus rendering intervening blocks worthless so that they could be acquired cheqply later at gridironer's pleasure. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),616</i>)

>  gridironing n. (Obs.) purchasing strips of land in shape of a gridiron, thus rendering intervening blocks worthless so that they could be acquired cheaply later at gridironer's pleasure. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

>  <b>OLIVER</b> The runholder was protected against intruders: cottages, fences and homesteads gave him a pre-emptive right over a set number of contiguous acres. Fences and cottages could be so disposed as to give the runholder rights over the best of the land - Sources of water and wood, strips surrounding isolated and thus inaccessible pockets. `Grid-ironing' and `spotting', as these selective practices were called, were certainly used by many runholders to tie up large areas, but probably not to the extent alleged by horrified land tenure radicals in later years. <i>(1968,Story of New Zealand, The,108</i>)

>  <b>FRANCIS</b> A [112] powerful landowner ... had for years dominated the Haast Valley and his cattle wandered and grazed rent-free over the lush valleys and river flats - rent-free because he had leased all the access. This practice, called grid-ironing, was not uncommon and was undertaken by numerous big landowners. <i>(1983,Wildlife Ranger,111</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The country has had quite enough of government by the Canterbury squatters, with their notorious gridironing system of land monopoly. <i>(Apr 16, 1904,NZ Observer,2</i>)

>  He had inquired into the Canterbury gridironing system. <i>(Oct 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the purchasing of strategic parts of land e.g. near rivers and roads, to dissuade others to buy.

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> A runholder might buy up all the good land in an area, leaving only worthless, unworkable land between runs, a practice known as grid-ironing. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,10</i>)

>  <b>Relph</b> The practice of 'gridironing' - whereby a series of blocks scattered over a property were freeholded - also caused problems. <i>(2007,From Tussocks to Tourists,72</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> The squatters contended that they had to adopt all these expedients of spotting and gridironing in self-protection. <i>(1940,William Rolleston: a New Zealand statesman,61</i>)

>  The method of either gridironing, that is buying up the freehold of strategic parts around ... <i>(Nov 15, 1952,Weekly News,33</i>)

>  <b>ACKROYD</b> Not all runholders went in for gridironing and spotting and much buying was done in self-defence; if runholders did not purchase strategic areas on their runs, speculators would do it for them. [grid-ironing] <i>(1993,Politics, Economics & Pastoral Land,11</i>)

### grievance industry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Yet what has grown around this simple document has become a grievance industry designed to line the pockets of a number of the participants while doing little to further those Maori at the bottom of the socioeconomic heap. <i>(Dec 17, 2001,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  Sadly, a poorly drafted act, coupled with inadequate attention to its implementation, allowed a major grievance industry to blossom. <i>(May 1, 2002,Dominion,10</i>)

>  By accepting compensation [the Chinese community] would, in a single action, have joined the "grievance industry" - an activity synonymous with failure, inertia, and an unwillingness to accept a responsibility for one's own outcomes. The Maori grievance industry is unique as Maori are the indigenous people, and have a fulcrum of newly enacted laws around which to litigate, and they clearly suffer severe handicaps which they do not seem to be able to unilaterally resolve... Of concern is that we now have a large Pacific Islands minority coat-tailing the Maori grievance industry. <i>(Feb 21, 2002,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Cue all those jibes about "Maori time" and the new ones about the "grievance industry" and the Treaty "gravy train". <i>(Mar 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  In the brave new world of employment relations. bosses and workers are turning in big nunbers to employment lawyers and advocates to guide them through a thicket of legal complexities. This is the new grievance industry. <i>(Jun 27, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

>  Prime examples of policies that foster a mindset of blame, dependency and political correctness are those promoting the Treaty-based grievance industry and separate Maori development. <i>(Jun 5, 2002,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  Certainly there are some people who do well out of what is described as the grievance industry, and probably feel a bit disenfranchised once the settlement comes in because then they have got nothing to do... <i>(Dec 2, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Surely this grievance industry has become a negative influence and should be closed down by the government without delay. <i>(Dec 9, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  A lot of Maori are not tied up with the grievance machine. <i>(Dec 2, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

### grievant
 
 <i>n.</i> a person bringing a personal grievance claim before the Employment Tribunal

>  When the business's part-owner went to sort that out, the grievant, who was standing by the door, alledgedly hassled and grabbed him... Here, an investigation meeting took place but it was unusual in that it proceeded without the grievant or her representative being present... The lessons to be learnt from these two cases involving drinking grievants is that, except in truly exceptional circumstances, a proper investigation must be carried out before an individal is dismissed. <i>(Oct 17, 2002,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  The Employment Tribunal, and the Employment Court on appeal, upheld their claims and awarded between three and six momnths pay for reasonable notice and $11,000 to $15,000 compensation for humiliation and distress to each grievant... As regards compensation for humilation aqnjd distress, the Court of Appeal held that the awards made to the grievants failed to distinguish between the impact of not having a job and that of the manner in which the terminations of employment were implemented... "After exercising the element of job loss, we consider that [an award] of $7000 to each [grievent] would have been appropriate," the Court of Appeal said. <i>(Mar 11, 2002,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  There was a difference of opinion.. as to whether.. the grievant was a good performer or not. <i>(Nov 7, 2002,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### griffin
 
 <i>n.</i> Collocation usually straight griffin, the dinkum oil esp. straight griffin, the facts, the true story; cf griff. (Early in NZ, then ? obsolete; thence back to NZ through WW2.)

>  I don't take much stock of texts, mum, but I reckon that one over your head is the real straight griffin, and a fellow ought to hang on to it ... <i>(Sep 30, 1905,NZ Truth,15/7/8</i>)

>  Affiliated unions are equally blameworthy for not giving the `straight griffin' to the parent body that the evil has to be stopped or considerably lessened. <i>(Jan 23, 1909,NZ Truth,188/3/4</i>)

>  <b>MacCALLUM</b> I don't mean that sort of oil. What I mean is the dinkum oil, the straight griffen and the low down about the horses. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,21</i>)

### Griffin weed
 
 <i>n.</i> verbena, =vervain, also Griffin's weed <i>Verbena officinalis.</i>

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Vervain (<i>Verbena officinalis</i>), a species few would suspect of harm, but which by overrunning grass has proved a nuisance in a small way, is an alien of note. It has invaded us from the north, a quarter from which comparatively few road-plants have come. Its proper name has been temporarily lost, it has been renamed, and, what must be almost unique in popular botanical nomenclature, that name again lost and the plant locally rechristened ; finally, it has resumed its correct designation. This alien from southern England appeared near Frazertown in the late 'seventies. It was first noticed on a station belonging to Mr Nairn; there it began to spread, and there it became known as Nairn's weed; the station then became the property of the late Mr Griffin. Now Mr Griffin, as chairman of the Wairoa County Council, touched the business and bosom of every up-country settler in the district. People knew him who did not know and had never known his predecessor; his office advertised the plant : presently, even in the [299] immediate vicinity of its origin, the earlier designation was dropped, the later one assumed. It became known up and down the east coast as `Griffin's Weed'. <i>(1926,Tutira,298</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> VERVAIN (<i>Verbena officinalis</i>), also called blue top, griffin weed, and missionary weed, is a slender tough-stemmed perenial 1 to 2 ft. high, with a few opposite rough, cleft leaves, and small lilac flowers, ... It is becoming very common in the country from Wairoa to Gisborne [and is also called recorded from Christchurch]. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,146,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Griffin weed [=] vervain [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,34</i>)

### griffin's head
 
 <i>n.</i> a vervain

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> <i>(1928,Tutira,299</i>)

### Griffins Sampler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Karl trusts his tutor enough to bring him his "biscuit box of broken dreams", his stories stored in a Griffins Sampler Box based on tragic events of his childhood. <i>(Jul 19, 1993,Dominion,10</i>) [NOTES: I knew these in the 1930s as "Griffins' Sampler Biscuits". They were sold in fancy packets for a little more than the cost of an ordinary packet and contained a few of each of Griffins' lines, including walnut creams and iced wafers. They were a rare treat for school lunch when your mother was hardpressed to cut lunch for one reason or another - DEH]

### grim dig
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also dig](../A#also-dig))

>  The Grim Digs' doings / Put me in a humour serene. <i>(Oct 16, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/173 : 5</i>)

>  Into the Public Relations Office of the 2nd NZEF in Maadi Camp there marched, with a crisp New Zealand slouch, a very old, and very grim, dig, ... >  [in column `Diggers Out of Uniform', in which Grim Dig is the main character.] `I'm an expert,' said the Grim Dig. `Served a long, hard apprenticeship in some tough units ... <i>(Jun 4, 1955,Upper Hutt Leader,5</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [-->1951 15 F 8 Wellington H.S. 26 GRIND -->1951 16-18 M 32 St Bede's, Chch 26 GRIND (3) -->1951 15 F 8 Wellington H.S. 26 GRINDING]

### grog
 
 <i>n.</i> any alcoholic liquor; booze (? espec. beer). (Though used commonly for `booze' in early (1905) Truth, probably re-introduced to NZ after WW2???)

>  [??] ... publicans can at present sell grog all over the country with impunity ... <i>(Feb 2, 1864,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Feb 2 Press (Christchurch) in 1966 Turner English Language in Australia and NZ 130]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> (The Ohinemuri Deputation) [?liquor] Eicke's constitution, we hear,/ Was so delicate .../ That on the journey he'd be queer,/ Unless sustained by bottled beer;/ Simpson said pulling [sc. rowing] made him dry,/ .../ .../ So of grog they took a good supply/ When they went to Ohinemuri./ [33] Their life was one continual shout,/ They stayed up there and idled about/ .../ But the beer and the grog was running out./ Of course they'd had many a korero [kore|ro],/ Which led to nothing at all ... <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,32</i>)

>  [?] Edgar Clough left for S. Africa ... and old Abner [Clough, an island drunkard] has gone to Waitangi to see him off he says but I fear grog will be all he sees. <i>(Feb 14, 1900,Chudleigh Diary,399,1950 ed.</i>)

>  ... the S.M. said he was bound to tell where he had got his grog. <i>(1905,NZ Truth,21/1/8</i>)

>  The wage earners are bulging with money and spare time. ... At any cost grog and sigerettes for all. <i>(Jan 5, 1919,Chudleigh Diary,465,1950 ed.</i>)

>  ... the slumbering form of the sleuth [King Country] policeman who set out to keep night-watch in the fern for a motor-load of illicit grog, and who was found ... with a `dead marine' tied to his foot bearing a joyful note from the waipiro-laden gauntlet-runners. <i>(Jul 11, 1921,Quick March,25</i>)

>  <b>FOXTON</b> Grog could be bought on the sly. It appeared that there were `shebeens' all along the line. <i>(1921,At The Front,121</i>)

>  Taking the old man's car, and there'll be some grog. <i>(1948,Landfall,6/110</i>) [NOTES: (Ballantyne)]

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: grog: alcohol <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `I'll have some grog aboard too. I'll be a bit lit.' <i>(1956,Sweet White Wine,91</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> The rest of us had had a feed and were coming right again so quite a bit of grog was bought for the evening session. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,181</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> He had lain for a month in a native hut visited by pubescent girls who supplied him with home comforts and African beer. ... `I had the dingbats for a while,' he said. ...@Well, when I had the dingbats there was a bloody great Negro's face an inch away from me all the time. It must have been the bad grog.' <i>(1985,Horse,25,C1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Name and the Game)]

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> ... the Maori drink only two kinds of grog - the beer in the half-gallon jar and the beer in the bottle. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,64</i>)

>  <b>WALLIS</b> Take some grog to while away the small hours. <i>(1963,Point of Origin,128</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> ... it didn't look as though he could take much grog. <i>(1971,Bastards I Have Met,18</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> `I'd been on the wagon for about a year and the grog got to me.' <i>(1975,Milford Road,170</i>)

>  <b>HOLMES</b> Thus old digs could carouse snugly in their clubrooms, happy in the thought that the rest of the population was denied grog, ... <i>(1977,Best of Homespun,64</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> The wowsers are gone. Let's get stuck into the grog. ... There's booze enough to last until dawn. <i>(1981,Jonesy,67</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> Some of the army men stationed at Whekenui must have been rather hard cases who liked their grog. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,85</i>)

>  grog ... 2. Austral. & N.Z. inf. alcoholic drink in general, esp. spirits. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,492</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 331; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 218]]

>  <b>FRAMCIS</b> Anyway, that was the end of old Bert. Funny how station cooks seem to hit the grog.' <i>(1983,Wildlife Ranger,79</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Thin-fingered hands around the glass - ... Split chin upwards, and the dark grog [sc. stout] practically seen outside your skinny throat. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> When he was on the grog his mind began to cast about for some excitement, ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,166</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> I'd been on the grog and lost my best dog/ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,75</i>)

>  `We can't handle you because you run away and get on the grog.' So they said, `We'll fix you. We'll send you to The Island.' `That's an island in the Hauraki Gulf.' <i>(Feb 14, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,19</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> 1. alcoh liquor of any kind; a drink of alcohol see also sly-grog 1832, 1858 Thatcher Col Songster 39 2. on the grog 1959.] ->This sense became obsolete except perhaps in its nautical or naval application; and in its association with SLY-GROG, illicit spirits. [1851 Feb 14 ?? 8 Two packing cases and a log,/ A cupboard - all bereft of prog -/ A shelf of bottles - but no grog -/ My weary senses battled.]

>  A little brandy & water commonly called grog [by the seamen] or Spruce beer with a little brandy & sugar commonly called kalibogas is the liquor drank on these [`Marooning'] Expeditions. <i>(May 7, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/271</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> Sidey came from Leachs' to borrow some grog - took two bottles back with him - <i>(Dec 23, 1856,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,59</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> We have found a nice quiet place ... It is two shillings a meal but ... they do not sell grog, having only a wine and beer licence. <i>(Oct 19, 1862,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... order enough bottled beer ... but no grog <i>(Jul 20, 1861,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,67</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> (The Caledonian Gathering) And the booth they visited for prog,/ And sometimes took a nip of grog;/ There was Grieve, one of your famed Town Board,/ McIvor to o his orders roared/ <i>(1864,Invercargill Minstrel,6</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> They [sc drunken sawyers] were incapable from too much grog, or else lacked skill, to swim. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,129</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... yet the landlord [of the shanty] made money; chiefly, I fear, by poisoning with bad grog a number of his customers. The way some of these land-sharks will keep an unfortunate man in a state of intoxication for weeks ... is horrible to contemplate, and when his money is done, they will knock him out remorselessly. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,271</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> [?] If your hand's a bit unsteady frae the ither evenin's grog. <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,87</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

 <i>n.</i> grog-seller

>  [Aviemore Visitors' Book] Fantabulous <i>(Oct 15, 1966,Dominion,3/1</i>)

### grog-artist
 
 <i>n.</i>

### grog-bar
 
 <i>n.</i> a sly-grog shop; unlicensed bar [(obs.)]

>  .. forty unlicensed grog bars in Ashburton. <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1/5</i>)

### grog-den
 
 <i>n.</i> [(hist.)]

>  Where a year ago only three grog-dens warned the passer-by in the harbour not to land on the beach, a neat row of wooden houses ... extends along the line of high water. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/xvi/198/2</i>)

### grog-party
 
 <i>n.</i> a drinking-party.

>  <b>HYDE</b> ... the first real grog-party. <i>Ibid.</i> 90 At Christchurch, where the wet brigade had joined them and turned their progress into the first real grog party, a man yet fatter had repeated it. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,85</i>)

### grog-seller
 
 <i>n.</i> [(hist.) 1839 J.D. Lang in 1839 7 [check quot]] (See [Cf grog-shop keeper](../C#cf-grog-shop-keeper),[grog-vendor](../G#grog-vendor))

>  Of the others, the most part are following the occupation of grog-selling; ... but the grog seller has decidedly the most lucrative business ... <i>(Aug 9, 1834,NZ Independent,30,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> `Brandy Ben' was a notorious grog-seller, and was well known to the police. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/xi/110,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

### grog-selling
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Of the others, the most part are following the occupation of grog-selling; ... but the grog seller has decidedly the most lucrative business ... <i>(Aug 9, 1834,NZ Independent,30,1979 ed.</i>)

### grog-shanty
 
 <i>n.</i> A grog-shop; cf shanty.

>  <b>HAAST</b> The alluring grog-shanty, as usual, was not missing. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,82</i>)

>  <b>DAVIES</b> All the stores were "grog shanties" as well. <i>(1884,Poems & Other Literary Remains,273</i>)

>  They thereafter proceeded to "knock down" their cheque in approved bush style at the first grog shanty. <i>(Dec 23, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  I reached the grog-shanty in safety. <i>(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,163</i>)

>  Kyeburn, April 14. - ... Permit me ... to call attention ... to the rush of grog shanties which has taken place in our locality. <i>(Apr 16, 1869,Mount Ida Chronicle</i>) [NOTES: 1869 Apr 16 Mount Ida Chronicle in 1985 Bromby Eye Witness History 52]

>  <b>WHITEWORTH</b> He'd been a fighting man, and waiter at a low grog shanty ... <i>(1875,Cobb's Box,5</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Men were engaged .... in building .... a store and a `hotel', though both would in reality be `grog shanties', and would retail liquor by the nobbler', as a dram was called. <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,79</i>)

>  The man who does not permit overindulgence in many ways is doing a service to the two by keeping people away from "grogshanties." <i>(Dec 20, 1937,King Country Chronicle,5</i>)

### grog-shanty keeper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The shanty keeper is a cross between the store and the pub. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,14</i>) [NOTES: No directly attributable date given for Douglas MSS which is probably the MSS entitled "Islets, Towns and Glaciers" which generally appears to refer to the 1860s but to have been written about 1890-1905.]

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> Gerit Alexander Chalmers of Moaflat believed that those most likely to profit from land reform were 'the small publicans and grog-shanty keepers who gain a living out of the working people employed here'. <i>(1980,Southern Gentry,65</i>)

### grog-shop
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> After dinner went to Kororarika, ...; numbers of Europeans on the beach, indulging themselves in the grog shops which are now becoming general. <i>(Apr 28, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,309,1961 ed.</i>)

>  .... unless it be intended to put down grog-shops in New Zealand, while we allow gin-palaces in our own country <i>(Feb 1838,Christian Observer,9</i>) [NOTES: 1838 Feb NZ & Colonization (Christian Observer) 9]

>  <b>WALTON</b> They [the sailors and convicts in N.Z.] lead a most reckless life, by keeping grog-shops, selling spirituous liquors both [36] to European and natives, ... <i>(1839,Twelve Months' Residence in NZ,35</i>)

>  ... two grog-shops <i>IBid.</i> 1840 108 There are upwards of 2000 British subjects now settled in different parts of the islands, of whom several hundreds consist of a most worthless class of persons - such as runaway sailors, convicts who have escaped from ... NSW and VDL, keepers of grog-shops, and other vagabonds of dissolute habits, who ... practice frad, ... promote and take part in wars ... infect ... the natives with disease and a taste for ardent spirits. They really deserve the name which has been given them, `Devil's Missionaries' <i>(1839,Important Information Relative to NZ,29</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> To distinguish what are termed grog-shops and ship stores, the former is a small place where spirits reduced below proof are sold by the glass, whereas in the latter, spirits are never sold less than one fifth of a gallon, old measure. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/280</i>)

>  Kororarireka is a wretched place of about 50 houses most of which are grog shops. There are about one or two good stores. <i>(1840,Journal of Ensign Best,215,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Bar Room, Tap or Grog Shop is not a place of resort of the New Zealanders. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,75,1956 ed.</i>)

>  Paddy Woods arrived from Akarog via Port Levi and Raupahi, haveing in view to set up a store and grog shop at the latter place <i>(Feb 14, 1849,Torlesse Papers,56,1958 ed.</i>)

>  There are many stores very fairly stocked, and one or two decent eating houses - grog-shops, of course, in plenty. <i>(May 17, 1864,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS KING</b> The dancing rooms, prostitutes, billiard rooms, grog-shops ... were among the inevitable facilities in gold-rush Queenstown. <i>(1971,Wakatip,112</i>)

>  [Fox River Settlement] had seventy-five public-houses - grog-shops, saloons, or what-not - <i>(Sep 10, 1921,Quick March,11</i>)

>  <b>ST BRUNO</b> Ashore, blue night mantled ... the whaling station, veiling soft the haphazard settlement of weatherboard grogshops and general stores;... <i>(1952,Hellbuster, The,10</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> The beauty times are when you get in with the mob and do the rounds of the brothels and grog-shops. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,276</i>)

>  This general disregard for everything is not at all unlikely to make for the closing up of this glorified grog shop. <i>(Feb 8, 1977,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  Most of the old grog shops are gone, apart from the occasional tatty bottle store clinging to fading pub custom. <i>(Apr 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

### grog-shop keeper
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete)

>  Grog-shop-keepers are to vie with the `land-sharks' [from Sydney, in plundering the natives]. <i>(Aug 28, 1839,Colonial Gazette,627</i>)

>  ... grog-shop keepers <i>(1844,NZ Company Reports,12/77H.</i>)

### grog-tent
 
 <i>n.</i> The booze-tent at a showgrounds, etc. [ca1970s Cambridge]

### grog-up
 
 <i>n.</i> a drinking session; occas. a party; a booze up. [ca1946 Weir House]

>  <i>(Nov 5, 1969,NZ News,7/2</i>)

### grogged up
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>WEBBER</b> You remember telling me you didn't want any of your grogged-up Old Dig friends giving her the lowdown on you?' <i>(1962,Look No Hands!,124</i>)

### groggery
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> I might say with propriety that six eighths of all the European houses are nothing more than grogeries. Here are in capital letters over the door `Tap'. One in particular so-called Hotel, a man and his family the only occupants, over the door a large sign one third as large as the front, with bold letters `Victoria Hotel.' Her Majesty must feel proud to have her name in blazon letters embellishing a house, where all kinds of satanical devices are practiced with [sic] impunity. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,69,1959 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In the eighteen-twenties the debauchery natural to a whaling port took place mainly on board visiting ships. In the thirties it moved to numerous `grogeries' ashore. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,45</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a pub, hotel.

>  O'Shaunessy's Groggery <i>(Aug 19, 1905,NZ Truth,9-1-2</i>)

### grogging
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Her husband ... had been banished to a hut behind the house where he held secret grogging sessions with supplies brought to him from the Henley pub ... <i>(1980,All My Lives,35</i>)

>  <b>COX</b> The close to me ... was the TV1 lounge - it looked like viewing as usual in there, though apparently they liked to watch TV in the dark, to cover illicit grogging ... <i>(1987,Dirty Work,21</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> Committee work taking up too much of your grogging time, Dunstall? <i>(1981,Fish Factory,32</i>)

### groghouse
 
 <i>n.</i> a grog shop [(obs)]

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> ... six groghouses in the place. <i>(Dec 1836,Letters & Journals,1/17</i>)

### grommet
 
 <i>n.</i> a young (and inexperienced) surfer, snowboarder, or skateboarder.

>  It's taken 10 years in the industry for me to go from grommet to a person who other people know is around to stay. <i>(Oct 2, 2002,Dominion Post,E5</i>) [NOTES: Snowboarding]

>  Known affectionately as Granny Grommet, she took up surfing in her retirement. <i>(May 17, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  Young surfies have been called grommets for decades and so it seems are snowboarders... In one profile, Jah Harris is dubbed "New Zealand's original grommet". <i>(Jul 19, 2003,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  <b>Tracey Slaughter</b> There weren't many people down there: a couple of grommets wagging school to surf... <i>(2007,Six Pack 2, The,156</i>)

>  ...Johnson's music is a mellow contrast to the stereotype of hooning grommets and wild wipeouts. <i>(Feb 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> something disliked [also grummet, shepherds' grummet 1984 16 M E93 Pakuranga Coll. 3 GROMET -->2. New kids. -->1984 16 M E78 Pakuranga Coll. 25 GROMMITS] (See [also grummet](../A#also-grummet),[shepherds' grummet](../S#shepherds'-grummet))

### grommie
 
 <i>n.</i> a young and inexperienced surfer, grommet

>  And a bunch of young grommies out there, tearing it up. <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>gromwell, corn [s] Lithospermum arvense</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,35</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>gromwell, yellow [s] Amsinckia hispida</i> (R. & P.) Johnson <i>Ibid.</i> 89 <i>yellow gromwell [s] Amsinckia hispida</i> (R. & P.) Johnson <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,35</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> YELLOW GROMWELL (<i>Amsinckia calycina</i>) ... This plant, introduced from southern South America, has a limited distribution in New Zealand. <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,126</i>)

### groper
 
 <i>n.</i> Any of a group of large-mouthed, edible New Zealand and Australian fish. (?Esp. or ?orig.South Island.) Oligorus gigas; =hapuku, see also bream, brim.

>  . ... got 11 gropers. Jan 22 ... caught 18 Horbockers. <i>Ibid.</i> 146 Simson and men out fishing: got 11 Gropers. <i>(Jan 16, 1844,Piraki Log</i>)

>  Many ... varieties of sea-fish are caught round the coast, such as barracouta, groper, rock-cod, ling, flounders, soles, gar-fish, and others, all which are plentiful; ... <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,32</i>)

>  The Groper, or Hapuka, <i>Oligorus gigas</i> [sic], frequents deeper water ... <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>) [NOTES: (Black Stewart Island)]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> The first on the list of edible fishes is the Hapuku or Whapuku of the Maoris (<i>Oligorus gigas</i>), or Habuka, as the name is generally pronounced by Europeans, who in the South apply the name Groper to the same fish. It is also occasionally called the Cod fish, which is altogether erroneous ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,102</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Hapuka, Groper .. [Scientific Name] <i>Oligorus gigas </i> <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> 36 GROPER (see HAPUKU). <i>Ibid.</i> 40 HAPUKU (<i>Oligorus gigas</i>). The first on the list of marketable fishes is the hapuku, or whapuku of the Maoris (<i>Hectoria gigas</i> of Count Castelnau), or habuka, as the name is generally pronounced by Europeans, who in the south, apply the name groper to the same fish. It is also occasionally called the cod-fish, which is altogether erroneous, as it is more properly the representative of the sea-perch of European seas. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ</i>)

>  <b>DOUGALL</b> [They] caught [at Stewart Island] trumpeter, blue cod, and a couple of hapuka (groper) ... <i>(1888,Far South,4</i>)

>  2. <i>Mokohinou Islands.</i> - Hapuku (or groper), hiwihiwi, barracouta, maomao, spotty, parrot-fish, leather jacket, korokoro-pounamou. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/203</i>) [NOTES: 2. <i>Mokohinou Islands.</i> - Hapuku (or groper), hiwihiwi, barracouta, maomao, spotty, parrot-fish, leather jacket, korokoro-pounamou.]

>  Moki are very plentiful, but we only get a few trumpeter now and then. Of course, the cod and groper are plentiful, also butter-fish and barracouta. <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/53</i>) [NOTES: (Henry)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Groper</i>, a fish. In Queensland, <i>Oligorus terra-regina</i>, Ramsay; in New Zealand, <i>O. gigas</i>, `called by the Maoris and colonists <i>`Hapuku'</i> (Gunther) a large marine species. <i>(1898,Austral English,176</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Hapuku, ... Oligorus gigas, groper. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,43</i>)

>  The class of fish supplied to this market before the war contained nothing so good as our groper (hapuku) or snapper, both of which occur in enormous quantities in these seas. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/136</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  Groper or Hapuku <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i> ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/268</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  POLYPRION OXYGENEIOS (Bloch and Schneider). <i>Groper; Hapuku</i>. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/114</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i> (Bloch & Schn.). GROPER; HAPUKU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,32,1971 ed.</i>)

>  23. <i>Polyprion prognathus</i> Forster. Hapuku or Groper. Maori: Hapuku <i>(1930,TrNZI,61/143</i>) [NOTES: (Young) [check vol]]

>  <i>groper n</i>. name given to several large fish. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i>: Groper, hapuku. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i> (Bloch and Schn.). Groper. Hapuka. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/409</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Under present conditions, the Stewart Island grounds offer ... the usual catch ... blue cod, groper, trumpeter, moki and greenbone. <i>(1940,Rakiura,221</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Hapuku (also spelled hapuka) - the groper. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,61</i>)

>  <i>ha[a]puku</i>, a sea-fish (Oligorus gigas), groper, vulg. harbuka. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  groper : edible fish. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Groper (Hapuku) <i>Polyprionum oxygeneios</i> (Bloch and Schn.) (Syn. <i>Perca prognatha</i> Bloch and Schn., <i>Sciaena gadoides</i>, Richardson and <i>Centropristis gigas</i> Owen) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,406</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> GROPER <i>Polyprion oxygeneios (Bloch & Schneider)</i> ... Maori name: HAPUKA.[70] The flesh of the Groper is well flavoured and finds a ready market. Unfortunately, both the Bass and Groper are sold under the one name `Groper'. <i>Ibid.</i> 169 Groper Polyprion oxygeneios Hapuku; Hapuka; Habuka; Whapuka <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,69</i>)

>  ~ A large edible sea fish of New Zealand; in Australia a different fish. The Maori name is `hapuka' or `hapuku'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),188,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Bass groper .. .. moeone, toti .. .. <i>Polyprion moeone</i> ... Groper .. .. hapuku, kapua, whapuku <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  ~ n. large edible sea-fish; kind of rock cod; in New Zealand the hapuku [Maori <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GROPER or HAPUKU <i>Other names: Polyprion oxygeneios</i>; whapuku (Maori). Groper has a wide usage in the South Island, while hapuku is largely used in the North Island. The word `hapuka' is a corruption of hapuku. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,224</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> HAPUKU or GROPER [<i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i>] ... Hapuku is a Maori name widely used by North Island fishermen, while groper has a predominantly South Island usage. In some localities both names are used. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,52</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family SERRANIDAE. The bass (<i>Polyprion moeone</i>. . .) and the groper (<i>P.oxygeneios</i>. . .) are two hefty members. ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,219</i>)

>  <i>groper ... n.</i> large edible sea-fish; kind of rock cod; in N.Z. the hapuku [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> [spg grouper] <i>HAPUKU</i> (Grouper, whapuku) <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,203,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <i>groper n</i>. NZ = HAPUKU. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,332</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 492; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 218]]

>  [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 492; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 218] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Hapuku Polyprion oxygeneios</i> ... Widely known throughout New Zealand as either hapuku (often pronounced hapuka) or groper, the former generally in the North Island, the latter in the South. The species also occurs around southern Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,82</i>)

>  groper 1280-1313 GROPERS 1250-1316 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  It used to be the common practice for the early settlers of Otago to cure a good supply of groper for winter use, either pickled in barrels or smoked. <i>(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  The groper with the dilated Eddie Cantor eyes. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,159</i>)

>  Capt. Smith. The fish, which at Port Nicholson is known by the name of habooka, but which is called groper to the southward. <i>(1843,NZ Journal,98/263</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>P. moeone.</i>

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>GROPER</i> (two species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i> (ii) <i>Polyprion moeone</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) New Zealand Groper; Hapuku (ii) Bass. (c) Other common names: (i) Hapuka, Stone-eye, Blue-nose, Bonita (ii) Deepsea Groper. (d) Principal localities where taken: [Mainly easten coasts, Foveaux, and Chathams] ... [27 Groper and Bass] should not be confused with the Australian and tropical groupers which they resemble superficially. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,26</i>)

>  [index to common names 283-293] groper, deepsea 1292 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [[?omit as a suggested name]]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>GROPER</i> (two species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i> (ii) <i> Polyprion moeone</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) New Zealand Groper; Hapuku (ii) Bass. (c) Other common names: (i) Hapuka, Stone-eye, Blue-nose, Bonita (ii) Deepsea Groper. (d) Principal localities where taken: [Mainly easten coasts, Foveaux, and Chathams] ... >  <b>DOAK</b> Gold ribbon groper <i>Aulacocephalus temmincki. </i> ... The gold ribbon groper was given its name by skindivers. It has previously been recorded from many parts of the central Indo-Pacific region, ... <i>(1972,Fishes,33</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>GOLD RIBBON GROUPER Aulacocephalus temmincki</i> ... Until recently the gold ribbon grouper was considered to be in the family Serranidae, hence the designation grouper, but ichthyologists have decided that it has more features in common with the grammistids or soapfishes and it is now placed in this family. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,209,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Epinephalus daemelii.</i>

>  <b>FISHER</b> Even the Morepork (sometimes called the Kelpfish) thought that the only way out was to go past my ear. It was a big fish - at [38] least twelve pounds. They have a lot of meat on them and have been known to be mistaken by new chums for the Spotted Black Groper. <i>(1977,Angels Wear Black,37</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>SPOTTED BLACK GROUPER Epinephalus daemeli </i>... Spotted black grouper, or saddled roack cod as they are known in Australia, ... in New Zealand rarely reaches 80cm ... This species ... in New Zealand is restricted to coastal and offshore waters from Whangarei up to North Cape. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,205,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Spotted black groper Epinephalus daemelii</i> Widespread in the Indo-Pacific region, including Australia, where it is known as saddled rock cod, and northern New Zealand. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,84</i>)

>  <b>DOAK</b> Toadstool groper <i>Trachypoma macracanthus</i> (Aust: Pacific Perch) ... first discovered in New Zealand waters, at the Poor Knights Islands, in 1969. Because of its distinctive appearance we named it the toadstool groper, ... <i>(1972,Fishes,32</i>)

>  <b>DOAK</b> Toadstool groper <i>Trachypoma macracanthus</i> (Aust: Pacific Perch) ... first discovered in New Zealand waters, at the Poor Knights Islands, in 1969. Because of its distinctive appearance we named it the toadstool groper, ... <i>(1972,Fishes,32</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>TOADSTOOL GROPER Trachypoma macracanthus </i>... A typical thick-bodies grouper shape, this species is orange-red in colour with some paler patches and is uniformly covered on both body and fins with [205] close-set white dots, resembling the colour pattern of the amanita toadstool. ... In New Zealand it is only found around the offshore islands of eastern Northland ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,205,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Toadstool groper Trachypoma macracanthus</i> New Zealand and eastern Australia. Golden orange with a reddish tinge, covered with numerous small white spots (named from the Amanita toadstool). <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,84</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (See [bass groper](../B#bass-groper))

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] groper, bass 1288-1312 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <i>Polyprion americanus </i>Bloch and Schneider. This deep-water representative of the genus is now identified by Waite ... with the wreck-fish of the Atlantic. It is occasionally taken outside Otago Heads, and is known by the local fishermen as `bass groper' or `black bass'. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/228</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago),]

>  <i>P. americanus:</i> Bass groper. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <i>Polyprion americanus</i> (Bloch and Schn.). Bass groper. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/409</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Bass Groper <i>Polyprionum moeone</i> Phillipps <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,406</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 406]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BASS GROPER (MOEONE) <i>Polyprionum moeone</i> Phillipps. This large fish was at one time thought to be the well-known Groper or Hapuku but it is now known to be specifically different. It is wider and deeper in the body. It also has larger eyes and bigger scales. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,224</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Bass Polyprion moeone Black Bass; Bass Groper <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BASS [<i>Polyprion moeone</i>] ... Bass-groper and the Maori moeone are alternative names. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,52</i>)

>  [species name] bass groper .. [scientific name] <i>Polyprion moeone</i> .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Bass Polyprion moeone</i> ... Sometimes (and correctly) called bass groper. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,83</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> He turned, knowing it was Tom Shepherd, a sudden great warmth flowing over him. `Why, Tom - ' `You old bastard! I've waited hours.' <i>(1952,Julian Ware,238</i>)

>  [species name] bass groper .. [scientific name] <i>Polyprion moeone</i> .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### Groppi mocker/Groppi mokka
 
 <i>n.</i> dress clothes; dress uniform; a dinner suit. (from Groppi's, a Cairo restaurant) [(WW2 Egypt) Note also: A dinner jacket [?a mishearing or transformation: P.Munz was not certain] ca1975 p.c. Professor Peter Munz misheard the expression (for `dinner suit') as used by John O'Shea (Wellington) ....to put on a smokey gropper.]

>  <b>McGILL</b> groppi mocker dressing up, originally to visit Groppi's teashop/hotel in Cairo, WWII; Groppi's Light Horse was combatants' name for base troops at Cairo. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,53</i>)

>  Where will the dark little winds carry him? Perhaps to Groppi's, or to one of the "Jardin-Dancing". <i>(Jul 12, 1943,NZEF Times,III/107 : 5</i>)

>  <b>THE SARGE</b> He went away in search of his <i>Groppi mokka</i> which consisted of a freshly pressed.. pair of slacks and a bush shirt.<i>Ibid.</i> 156 <i>groppi mokka</i> (Kiwi and Ar.) - fine clothes, groppi being a posh restaurant. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,110</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> ... the girls of Cairo were never treated more liberally and often to the daintiness of Sault's and Groppi's. <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,38</i>)

>  `This,' said the Grim Dig. `Please forward back sheesh . . ., also one overdue fare from Groppi's to Bab-el-Louk.'] <i>(Mar 25, 1959,Upper Hutt Leader,5</i>)

>  The Groppi tea shop in the heart of the commercial centre [of Cairo] still does a thriving business, selling tea and ice cream to middle class Egyptians and occasional tourists. The tea house fashionable even during the war, insinuated itself into the language with `groppi mocka' being the best clothes the Kiwis could muster for a day on the town. <i>(Oct 4, 1982,Evening Post,41</i>)

 <i>n.</i> A lavatory. [ca 1941 St Patrick's College Silverstream the grot(s), `the toilet(s)' 1980 Lynfield College, Auckland (Goldie Brown Collection) grot: toilet]

### ground lark
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pipit](../P#pipit))

>  The other birds ... were ... the Fan-tail (<i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>), the Ground-lark (<i>Anthus Novae Zelandiae</i>), the New Zealand Titmouse (<i>Certhiparus Novae Zelandiae</i>) ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/164</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Pihoihoi </i> - This bird resembles the canary in shape and size; ... It might not be improperly designated the ground-lark, which it very much resembles. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),58</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Of animal life nothing was visible, with the exception of the Cigale Zelandica, and a brown ground-lark very common in New Zealand. [fn 1 Alauda Novae Zelandiae, Gruel.] <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,I/325</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 35. Alanda Novae Seelandiae. Gmel., ... Kogoo aroure of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound.<i>Forst.</i> Kataitai of the natives of Cook's Straits. <i>Dieffenbach </i>. A `Ground Lark' is given under the name of Pihoihoi, by Mr Yate; Piohiohi, by Mr Polack; Pi-o-oie, by M. Lesson, which may prove to be the above species. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,II/192</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/192]

>  <b>ROBERTS, WHS</b> ... I think it was the Norwegian rat, which had destroyed the Maori rat and overrun the country. It also caught quail, ground larks, grass birds, and anything else that came its way. <i>(Jun 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 June W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 31]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The <i>Fringillidae </i>, or Finch family, has two species; one of them, the Kataitai or ground-lark, is the most numerous bird in the country. It rarely sings unless its nest is approached, when it rises in the air a short distance and sings, evidently for the purpose of drawing off the danger. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/25</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 33. ANTHUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Gml. Ground Lark. Pihoihoi. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,13</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANTHUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW -ZEALAND PIPIT.) ... <i> Native names </i>. Pihoihoi and Whioi; `Ground-Lark' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,132,1888 2nd edn 1/63 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i> Pihoihoi </i>, n. <i> anthus Novae Zelandiae, ground-lark </i>. <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,134</i>) [NOTES: [check order of cit]]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i> Ground-Lark, ... (1) In New Zealand, a bird also called by the Maori names, Pihoih </i> oi and <i> Hioi.</i> [1888 Buller] <i>(1898,Austral English,176</i>)

>  The ground-lark is everywhere a common bird. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,195</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The centipede (<i> were </i>), the caterpillar (<i> whe </i>), the Maori-bug (<i> kekerengu </i>), the birds <i> torea </i> ... and the ground lark (<i> hioi </i>: Anthus N.Z.), a sacred bird, were all supposed to have been brought in the canoes. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,181</i>)

>  Occasionally a ground lark makes a short flight over the tussocks ... <i>(1914,History of North Otago,43,1978 ed.</i>)

>  The ground-lark (<i> Anthus novae-zealandiae </i>) has practically ceased to be a denizen of the Town Belt; ... <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/278</i>) [NOTES: Bathgate, Otago]

>  <i> Austranthus novaeseelandiae Gm. Ground-lark; Pipit. This engaging little bird is so well adapted to open country and native tussock associations that with the spread of pastoral areas it is more than maintaining its numbers. </i> <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/96</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> <i>Ibid.</i> 275The birds I noticed were the bell-bird, the blight-bird, the yellow-headed tit, the ground lark, the little parakeet, the banded dotterel, and the native snipe. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,275,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I never saw the native quail, the cuckoo nor the morepork in the Central [Otago, c1860s], but I saw the sparrow-hawk, the rock wren, and plenty of hawks and pihoihoi (ground larks). <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,121</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> NEW ZEALAND PIPIT (<i> Anthus novaeseelandiae </i>), also called the ground lark, is a common bird throughout the country. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,86</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i> Hi[i]oi, ... 2. Anthus novaeseelandiae, pipit or ground-lark </i>; a bird. = <i> whi[i]oi pi[i]hoihoi, ka[a]taitai, wha[a]ioio. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,52</i>)

>  <i> ground lark </i> A name used in Australia and New Zealand for the bird properly called `pipit'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),189,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The New Zealand pipit, sometimes inaccurately called ground lark or native lark, is a local race (<i> Anthus novaeseelandiae </i>) of a widely distributed species ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/776</i>)

>  <i> ground lark </i> n. in New Zealand the pihoihoi or hioi: in Australia the pipit. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),61</i>)

>  <i> ground lark n. bird,</i> in N.Z. the <i> pihoihoi </i> or <i> hioi; in Australia the <i> pipit </i>. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

>  <i> Pipit Anthus novaeseelandiae </i>(GMELIN, 1789) ... <i> New Zealand Pipit A.n. novaeseelandiae </i> (GMELIN, 1789). <i> Auckland Islands Pipit A.n. aucklandicus </i> GRAY, 1862. <i> Antipodes Pipit A.n. steindachneri </i> REISCHEK, 1889. ... OTHER NAMES:<i>Pihoihoi, groundlark, native lark. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,270</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> ANTHUS NOVAE-ZEALANDIAE. - Ground Lark (<i> Pihoihoi </i>). <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/102</i>)

>  The ground-lark (<i> Austranthus novaeseelandiae </i>) was common on the fringes of rocky beaches ... <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/18</i>) [NOTES: Hamilton Poor Knights]



 
 <i>n.</i>

### ground parrot
 
 <i>n.</i> indigenous parrot, the kakapo [aka kakapo]

>  A WHITE kakapo, the New Zealand ground parrot, is among the latest additions to the Wellington Museum. <i>(Jul 7, 1899,Colonist (Nelson),4</i>)

>  He had been particularly anxious to secure specimens of the ground parrots which were known to have inhabited the Macquarie Island in the early days. <i>(Jan 8, 1912,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The rare ground parrot, the kakapo, is also represented in the collection. <i>(May 5, 1909,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> On the hills bounding the Grey River I caught four kakapos or green ground-parrots. <i>(1848,Great Journey,81,1952 ed.</i>)

>  ... a great addition has been made to our knowledge of the kiwi and ground parrot (kakapo) peculiar to New Zealand. <i>(Apr 12, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> Another bird is becoming very scarce and will probably soon be extinct; it is called the ground parrot from its not perching on trees ... <i>(Mar 4, 1853,Traveller under Concern,85,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> There is a green ground parrot too, called the kakapo, a night bird, and hardly ever found on the eastern side of the island. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,85,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: also ix/139]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 46. STRINGOPS HABROPTILUS. Gray. Ground Parrot. Kakapo. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,18</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> STRINGOPS HABROPTILUS. (OWL PARROT.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Kakapo \{,Tarapo\} and Tarepo; `Ground-Parrot' of the colonists. - <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,26,1888 2nd edn 1/177 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> ... he has thus caught thousands of ground parrots with a line fifty feet long. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,187</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> [The Maori judged] its value not by its extent but by the great number of Wekas (Wood hens) and Kakapos (Ground parrots) which ... had here enjoyed an undisturbed existence ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,52</i>)

>  46.<i> Stringops habroptilus</i>, Gray. Ground parrot, Night parrot, Kakapo. - It is still abundant in many parts of Westland; notwithstanding the persecution that constantly pursues it for the sake of supplying the requirements of museums and collectors. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,479</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> Stringops, owl-parrot - ground-parrot of the colonists. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/149</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Ground-Parrot, </i>... (1) <i>... Psittacus pulchellus</i>, Shaw. For the <i>Ground-parrot</i> of New Zealand, see <i>Kakapo.</i> [1793 Aust] <i>(1898,Austral English,177</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... the range ... had in old times had been a well-known hunting-ground for the <i>kakapo</i> (night-parrot or ground-parrot) ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/459,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> These men in their young days made periodical [122] expeditions up into the lakes region, hunting for the <i>weka </i>..., the <i>kakapo</i> or ground parrot, kiwi, and the rare and elusive <i>takahea</i>, the blue-plumaged notornis which is now believed to be extinct. 133 [caption] The <i>takahea</i> or Notornis Hochstelleri ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 121</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Ka[a]ka[a]po[o]</i>, ...Stringops habroptilus, ground parrot. <i>Ibid.</i> 391 <i>Tarepo[o], ... Stringops habroptilus owl parrot </i>or<i> ground parrot. = ka[a]ka[a]po[o]. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,91,6th ed.</i>)

>  <i>ground parrot n 1</i> : KAKAPO <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1003</i>)

>  <i>Kakapo Strigops habroptilus</i> GRAY, <i>1845 ... OTHER NAMES</i>: Tarapo, tarepo, owl <i>parrot, ground parrot, night parrot.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,242</i>)

>  <i>ground parrot NZ</i> = KAKAPO <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,332</i>) [NOTES: [see CCON 613]]

### ground truth
 
 <i>n.</i> (Geology) .

>  The concluding day of the meeting sought a plan for "ground truth". <i>(1994,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,105/72</i>)

>  In their never-ending search to augment NZ's hydrocarbon reserves and slow the inevitable decline of the West, seismic stratigraphers from the NZGS recently conducted ground-truth data acquisition in the Hakataramea Valley in South Canterbury and discovered what may prove to be New Zealand's best hope for petro-independence. <i>(Oct 1980,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,50/88</i>)

>  Let's get back to "ground truth". <i>(Mar 1987,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,75/5</i>)

>  Consequently, high-resolution 3.5 kHz sub-bottom profilers, air-gun seismics, gravity and magnetics were run alongside GLORIA to provide "ground-truth" for the sonographs. <i>(Aug 1988,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,81/26</i>)

### ground weta
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Three species of giant weta and an undescribed species of ground weta live in the Kaikoura ranges: the Kaikoura giant weta is restricted to South Marlborough; the bluff weta is found only in South Marlborough and at Mt Somers in mid-Canterbury; and the scree weta reaches its northeastern limit in the area. <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,305/34</i>)

>  Weta, and in particular ground weta, are an indicator of successful rat control within Matuku. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/37</i>)

>  11 One result of the loss of our insectivorous birds is the alarming increase of the ground weta, which seems to have something in common with the mole cricket, well known to English gardeners. <i>(Aug 20, 1932,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> There are four kinds [of wetas]: the <i>bush-weta, </i> the <i>cave-weta</i>, smaller in body with very long legs and feelers - the rock-weta found under rocks on mountain sides, <i>ground-weta</i>, as small as a cricket and found buried under the soil. ... The male has a huge horny mandibled head, the female a long stout ovipositor which has been stigmatised as a sting and earned for the insect the undeserved names, scorpion, and herself and her mate wood-devil and taipo. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,452</i>)

### ground-beetle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> [The morepork castings in the Canterbury Museum] are hard pellets, of an oval form, and of the size of a Sparrow's egg, composed chiefly of the hard elytra and heads of various coleopterous insects, among which I noticed particularly the shiny covering of the mata (<i>Feronia antarctica</i>), a handsome ground-beetle which is found on the Canterbury Plains, but does not occur in the North Island. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,20,{1888 2nd edn 1/195} ed.</i>)

### ground-hunting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the poisoning and shooting of noxious animals on the ground rather than by aerial methods.

>  .. the ground-hunting operation, using bait stations, proved about $1 a hectare cheaper than the aerial drop and was deadlier. In the paired trial, ground-hunting was marginally cheaper than the aerial operation... [groundhunting] <i>(Nov 16, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,14</i>)

 <i>n.</i> ground parakeet =?

 <i>v.</i>

### ground-sluicing
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining; also ground sluicing]

>  (Pyke Goldfields Report) [see preceding entry] <i>(1865,AJHR,C-4a-10</i>)

>  <b>BAT HGATE</b> In Otago, the principal kind of mining is ground sluicing similar to the method in Cornwall .... [called] `streaming' .... the water is carried across gullies in wooden-boxes, or canvas hose, called fluming. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,91</i>)

>  The primitive mode of working, still extant, is `ground sluicing' which means the running of water over and upon a face of alluvial drift. Even `hydraulic sluicing', by the compression of the water in a leathern pipe, is .... <i>(1890,Papers Read at the Mining Conference,64</i>) [NOTES: (L.O. Beal)]

>  <b>BLYTH</b> Ground Sluicing: Washing by water under low pressure alluvial deposits in trenches cut out of the bed rock. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> Neither the development of ground-sluicing nor the tunnel claims made Ross and Donoghue's distinctive. The absorbing topic at `the Ballarat of Westland' was the deep leads. These produced the most imposing mining works on the West Coast and gave Ross a permanence unusual for an alluvial goldfield. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,188,1962 ed.</i>)

>  ... ground-sluicing is now more generally adopted, unless where the ground is very deep. The sand is usually washed over the wide sluice-boxes, covered with different materials, such as plush, green baize, coir matting, and copper-plates coated with quicksilver, which catch the fine gold, while the lighter particles float away. <i>(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,215</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

### ground-tie
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [saddle horses] to stand and wait for riders, while reins are left lying on the ground.

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> If you have any horse-training ideas (I dislike the term horse-breaking, which often literally means just that) always teach your young horse to 'ground-tie' - in other words to stay put once the reins are thrown over its head. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,65</i>)

>  <b>PETER</b> ground-tie: leave reins down on horse and it will stand and wait for you. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,5</i>)

### groundhunter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hunter who kills noxious animals by working on the ground as opposed to aerial control.

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> I was sharing the back cabin with a ground hunter and a couple of spare jerry cans of fuel. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,91</i>)

>  <b>RALSTON ET AL</b> A second problem Ericksen highlighted is the inappropriate incentive structure which motivates groundhunters. [ground hunter] <i>(1996,Mountains of East Asia and the Pacific,193</i>)

>  <b>RALSTON ET AL</b> Groundhunters employ combinations of traps and cyanide poisoned baits to control possums. <i>(1996,Mountains of East Asia and the Pacific,191</i>)

### groundsel
 
 <i>n.</i> [?omit <i>Senecio</i> spp.]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> (Queen Charlotte Sound) Amongst the known kinds of plants are common and rough Bindweed, Nightshade and Nettles, ... Brambles, fnh Rubus. [ed. [<i>Rubus cissoides</i>, the `Bush-lawyer'; perhaps other spp.]]] Eyebright [fni Euphrasia. [ed. [There are a number of species; Euphrasias are generally alpines.]]] and groundsel: [fnj Senecio. [ed. [<i>Senecio lautus</i>, described by both Solander and George Forster.]]] but the species of each are different from any we have in Europe. There is also Polypody, Spleenwort and about twenty different sorts of ferns ... <i>(Feb 1777,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/804-805]

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... <i>knot-grass, brambles, eye-bright, groundsell</i>, and minor herbaceous vegetation, supposed to be solely indigenous to New Zealand, abound throughout the country. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/295</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> ... the prevailing species of trees are remo [rimu], totara, and tall manuka, all indicative of inferior sil.... The bush-like woods on the east side are of white birch, likewise no recommendation of the soil, but the chief objection to the district is, that the prairie soil affords scarcely any food for cattle ... it grows only the wiry tree grass [tussock] ... and <i>Phormium tenax</i> [flax], no aniseed, perennial groundsel, or milk thistle, and very little couch grass. ... [224] Like the narrow plains of the Waikauroa, it grows only the wiry tree grass or something [225] similar, and <i>Phormium tenax</i>, no aniseed, perennial groundsel, or milk thistle, and very little couchgrass, ... <i>(Aug 16, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Aug 16 Tuckett's Diary in 1898 Hocken contributions 223]

### group housing
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim writes: Hist. (mainly 1950s). A cluster of terms associated with the <i>group housing scheme</i> introduced in 1953 whereby numbers of low-cost houses in a subdivision were built by a private builder for sale - <i>group house, group housing, group builder</i>. Often the State developed the subdivision and provided services, selling the developed block to a private builder. The scheme offered home ownership as an alternative to State rental houses and was very popular... These terms are not recorded in DNZE. Note - 1. Of possible linguistic interest is the official use in this context of the term <i>block</i> in the sense of an area subdivided for group (or State) housing and <i>site</i> (not <i>lot</i>) in the sense of an individual section in such a block. There is no trace in the literature I have seen of the English term <i>housing estate</i>. 2. Group houses are associated in my recollection with <i>parades of homes</i>, at which the variety of homes in a group housing block were open for inspection by prospective buyers. This term seems to extend slightly the ordinary meanings of <i>parade</i> and is possibly a distinctive NZE compound.]

### grouse
 
 <i>adj.</i> great, fine, excellent; `beaut'. [1940s at least. Green says 1920s, Aust & NZ, but only gives it as a noun," the grouse", a form I have never heard. 1. [??] [I heard the word for the first time in November 1949 from A.Wright (lived in Auckland and overseas), and very often since then, especially among younger people.] -->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 4 GROUSE (3) -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 8 GROUSE->1953 17 M A38 Thames DHS 8 GROUSE [something good] -->1953 18 M A40 Thames DHS 28 GROUSE [boy/girlfriend] -->1960 16C F B1 Nelson Girls C. 4 GROUSE -->1960 16C F B2 Hawera Tech, C. 4 GROUSE -->1960 16C F B5 Wellington Girls C. 4 GROUSE -->1960 17C F B10 Napier Girls H.S. 4 GROUSE -->1984 16 F E35 Pakuranga Coll. 4 GROUSE [someone/something liked] -->1984 14 F E119Wgton Girls C. 4 FUCKIN GROUSE [ -->1984 14 F E136 Wgton Girls C. 4 LIKE GROUSE (MAN) -->1984 14 F E119Wgton Girls C. 8 GROUSE [someting good] -->1984 14 F E130Wgton Girls C. 8 GROUSE -->1984 14 F E134Wgton Girls C. 8 GROUSE -->1984 14 F E135Wgton Girls C. 4 GROUSE -->1984 14 F E137Wgton Girls C. 4 GROUSE -->1984 14 F E137Wgton Girls C. 8 GROUSE -->1984 14 F E138Wgton Girls C. 4 GROUSE -->1984 14 F E138Wgton Girls C. 8 GROUSE -->1984 14 F E139Wgton Girls C. 4 GROUSE -->1984 14 F E139Wgton Girls C. 8 GROUSE -->1984 14 F E142Wgton Girls C. 8 GROUSE -->1984 14 F E143Wgton Girls C. 4 GROUSE]

>  <b>BAKER</b> In constant use by our youngsters ... grouce <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> always predicatively: `That's the grouse': that's excellent: Australian: C. 20. Partridge, 1938. Excellent to eat; hence excellent: cf. English s. lovely grub!, excellent! <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,310</i>)

### GrowBulk
 
 <i>n.</i> a breed of high-bulk wool sheep

>  Exporter H Dawson Wool has found carpet and bedding manufacturers prepared to pay a premium for wool from GrowBulk and other high-bulk sheep... The GrowBulk project started in 1982 when, with the Wool Board's help, AgResearch scientist Roland Sumner began screening rams throughout New Zealand looking for high-bulk wools... Gore farmer Donald Morrison, who took on some GrowBulks as a trial in 1997, said they had far exceeded his expectations. <i>(Sep 20, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand breed of sheep with high-bulk wool qualities.

>  Almost all the sheep are Romneys, with 100 Texels kept, Mr Cameron says, for when payment based on meat yield is introduced by meat companies, and 200 Growbulks, a Texel-Romney-Poll Dorset composite created for their high-bulk wool which are also outstanding meat producers. <i>(Feb 23, 2006,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

>  Last season Paterson used only GrowBulk rams over his Coopworth-Romney flock. <i>(2000,Wool Grower,Spring 21</i>)

>  GrowBulk sheep carry Romney, Poll Dorset and Texel genes ... <i>(2001,Wool Grower,Autumn 18</i>)

>  Gore farmer Donald Morrison, who took on some GrowBulks as a trial in 1997, said they had far exceeded his expectations. He now had 1200 GrowBulk ewes and a commercial flock of 4000 half, three-quarter and seven-eighth GrowBulk-romney crosses .. I see the GrowBulk as an evolution of the romney ... <i>(Sep 20, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

>  WoolPro targeted increased bulk as a research priority in the late 1990s, helping fund the development of the GrowBulk breed by AgResearch and four multuplying breeders. <i>(2002,Wool Grower,Spring 3</i>)

### growing-day
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a day on which conditions are optimum for a definite amount of growth to occur in a cereal crop.

>  From this data we may conclude that every variety of wheat has a definite amount of growth to make, which occupies a definite number of normal "growing-days" (according to the variety grown) .. the remedy lies, when spring-sowing, in the selection of a variety which will not require a greater number of "growing-days" in a normal season.. Each variety of wheat, grown under normal conditions, will require a certain number of "growing-days" after seeding before it is fully developed..The number of growing days required varies according to the variety. <i>(Apr 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,315</i>)
