>  [Some omissions from NZPOD] are farewell as verb), judder bar(s), morning team, Zip (P) (water-heater); some widely used children's words would seem to deserve a place alongside neat ...: hex (a spell), growl (`Does your mother growl?'... <i>(Apr 26, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>) [NOTES: (Forrest Scott)]

>  We got growled.. because we didn't identify Tame Iti's little mates in this picture from the cover story in <i>Mana</i> No. 9. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 85</i>)

>  <b>Marisa Maepu</b> Mum didn't growl me about the trousers. She didn't even growl me for saying bloody. <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,25</i>)

### growler
 
 <i>n.</i> (? London growler.)

>  <b>BRUCE</b> Our first cabbies [? c 1860s] ... drove London growlers (as four wheelers were dubbed), while Tom Goodyer [80] followed with the first hansom ... <i>(1932,Early Days of Canterbury,79</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Maori name kumukumu [for Red Gurnard, <i>Corrupiscis kumu</i>] is often shortened by Europeans to `Kumu'. The Gurnards often make a grunting or growling noise when taken out of the water, and in some countries they are called `Growlers' on account of this peculiarity. The grunting noise is caused by the escape of air from the air-bladder through the pneumatic duct. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,168</i>)

### growthy
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of pasture or seasons] with wet and warm growing conditions.

>  <b>TAIT</b> Five hundred cows are carried. This figure is often increased when a growthy year is experienced. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,3</i>)

>  Mature pastures, particularly those with a high grass content, are normally safe from the bloat point of view than short 'growthy' pastures, especially those containing a large amount of clover. <i>(Apr 1968,NZ Journal of Agriculture,11</i>)

>  In a growthy year like last year, we had the ewes coming behind the lambs, cleaning up as they went. <i>(Sep 1986,Journal of the Polynesian Society,17</i>)

### grub
 
 <i>n.</i> grass grub <i>Odontria.</i> (See [grass grub](../G#grass-grub))

>  ... extra areas were rapidly turned over with the minimum of cultivation - cultivation was partially done by the `grub' ... The experience of the first sudden grub attack was sufficient to cause a considerable reduction in sheep numbers ... <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/323A</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> dig up with a grubber

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Grub ... to clear (ground) of the roots. To grub has long been English for to dig up by the roots. It is Australian to apply the word not to the tree but to the land. [1852] <i>(1898,Austral English,177</i>)

>  grub v.t. to clear land of the roots of felled trees. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  He had bought forty acres of blackberry and gorse, higher than his head, which he grubbed away at for years, and ended up milking a small herd of beautiful jersey cows... Dad grubbed thistles or gorse to clean up the grassed areas. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/26</i>)

>  Within two weeks she has grubbed blackberries, discovered that the couple managing the farm are cheating her, sacked them and made friends with the local teenagers. <i>(Dec 23, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

### grub ground
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a Maori wheat and potato planting.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Long prior to the [eighteen] eighties the 'grubbed grounds' as these cultivation lands used to be called, had reverted to a wild state. Only the name remained to show that they had been stumped by native labour. [grubbed ground.] <i>(1921,Tutira,75</i>)

>  <b>NEWMAN</b> Wheat we bought from the Maoris, who used to grow patches of it in their cultivations or 'grub ground.' <i>(1910,Big Bug Book, The,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large area used for growing vegetables.

>  <b>BALFOUR</b> Commenced to finish the fence around the grub-ground. <i>(May 2, 1870,Balfour Diaries</i>)

>  <b>BALFOUR</b> We all then laid off for a new grubground for me on Hermit hill. <i>(Apr 17, 1871,Balfour Diaries</i>)

### grub harrow
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EVANS</b> The use of turnips as winter feed for sheep required an implement which would grub out turnips when they were eaten down to ground level. These requirements led to the introduction of Grub Harrows... first made by Robert Hutton, of Otipua, South Canterburv. "I made the first set about fifty four years ago (1884)". <i>(1956,History of Farm Implements,11</i>)

>  <b>HAWKE & SCOTT</b> Locally made grub harrows were first made in South Canterbury about the 1880s, and were popular until the first disc harrows were imported from America. <i>(1985,Early Farm Machinery in NZ,53</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an implement that can root up the remains of turnips and other root crops [ known elsewhere as drag harrow.]

>  <b>EVANS</b> These requirements led to the introduction of grub harrows. <i>(1956,History of Farm Implements,11</i>)

>  These grub harrows (often known as 'drag' harrows) proved very popular. <i>(1956,History of Farm Implements,12</i>)

### grubber
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stump-puller or jack.

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> With a 'monkey' grubber you can quickly clear .. Stumps, Trees etc ... <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,xix</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [shearing] a sticky sheep where the fleecewool yolk has not risen.

>  a sticky sheep - a grubber. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) a kick in rugby which causes the ball to bounce and roll along the ground [grubber kick Rugby & Rugby League. UK, Austral., Sth Africa.] (See [grubber kick](../G#grubber-kick))

>  I was one-on-one with the fullback and I'd seen a bit of space behind, so I put the grubber through and scored it. <i>(Jul 4, 2005,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

>  One moment he was nowhere, the next he'd flashed on to a Mauger grubber and was gone. <i>(Aug 10, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> The usual NZ generic name for Eng. `mattock, adze' for rooting up weeds, plants etc. Of two main kinds: (a) one blade in the form of a heavy hoe or adze (the handle is, of course, the short pick-handle) and the other blade in the form of an axe; (b) with one hoe-like or adze-like blade with nothing on the back end; mattock (US? grub-hoe)

>  FOR SALE, AT the STORES of the UNDERSIGNED, the following variety of AGRICULTURAL IMPLEMENTS, by GRAY; - ...Grubbers ... <i>(Apr 6, 1861,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> The first suggested they [sc. ice-axes] were `grubbers which had been sent down for Ryan', another believed `they were picks ...' ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,84</i>)

>  ... plant some ... trees, in what are termed `grubber-pits'. These pits are prepared by removing the turf with a mattock. <i>(1903,AJHR,G1: 93</i>) [NOTES: 1902-3 Appendix to Journals G1 Lands and S. Report 93 [Otago]]

>  Constable Harvey was early on the scene, and he made a search and found outside the bedroom door the head of a heavy gorse grubber, and it is believed that this was the weapon Littlewood employed when he attacked his wife. <i>(May 3, 1913,NZ Truth,410/5/5</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> STUMP GRUBBING - ... With a `MONKEY' GRUBBER you can quickly ... clear Stumps Trees, etc., from your land. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,xix</i>) [NOTES: [?specialised stump grubber]]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> ... the beautiful loose earth ... that crumbles to the blows/ Of the grubber, ... <i>(1979,Collected Poems,469,1969 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Well ... the next morning we're taken out, lectured, and given grubbers and gorse-slashers, cutting blackberry ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,253</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> And he picks up the grubber to go after the thistles that are everywhere over-running the place. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,124,1979 ed.</i>)

>  Sunday we spent navvying with picks and grubbers. <i>(Aug 1953,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,5/19/105</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Stock, secondary fires, and the slash-hook or grubber have all been, and are being, used to this end. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,247,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> ... at the hut Gunn found a mattock or what a Canterbury man would call a gorse grubber. ... `... grub out every gorse bush you can find ... You might be able to dig out a rabbit, too, with that grubber.' <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,90</i>)

>  `Grab hold of that bloody grubber,' I said. `What's a grubber?' asked Mortimer [the Pom], like a nong. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,147</i>) [NOTES: 1973 Grant `NZ Short Story']

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Ewan and the two girls could ... go up to the Maize paddock with grubbers and grub out teatree roots that the plough had missed. <i>(1978,Elver, The,45</i>)

>  Three chips with a grubber, then the tree is put in. <i>(Jul 15, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to grubber kick

>  The sheer variety of kicks adds to the dilemma. To grubber or not to grubber? Banana or bomb? Spiral or skew it off the side into Row B? <i>(Aug 17, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A4</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere] a grub-hoe used in dry soil cultivation and for removal of weeds.

### GST
 
 <i>n.</i> a single rate tax on goods and services with no exemptions [See 1985 Budget; see HNZD2.]

The GST office advises that customers should check that they are only being billed for the retail price. <i>(Jul 20, 1986,Sunday News,21</i>)

>  For example, a builder wants to know how to charge GST on a partially built house, although his biggest worry appears to be how the changes will affect Uncle Albert's Orgasmic Orchestra, the band he plays in. <i>(Jun 28, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

### guaranteed price
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) [The words are not used in the 1936 Milk Marketing Act which in 1936 was actually the Primary Produce Act and only later in amendments labelled Milk Marketing Act. The act talks about fixing prices and says that "the general purpose shall be to assure to the producer a net return.. equivalent to the return he would have received if such dairy-produce had been acquired by the Crown for export in accordance with the foregoing purposes of this Part of this Act." [Primary produce Marketing Act 1936: NZ Statutes 1 Edw. VIII. Session I 5/70]]

>  <i>(Oct 30, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,3/2</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,44, 67</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> For the farmers there was to be a `guaranteed price'. The state would purchase the farmer's produce, pay him a price based on average prices over a period-thus abolishing the element of speculation in his income-and sell it abroad. Sales in a good year might yield a reserve to be drawn on when prices fell. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,259</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... the institution of a guaranteed price for farm products. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,150</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLANDE</b> I'd have thought this guaranteed price proposal would have made you dairy farmers supporters of the Labour Party. <i>(1978,Elver, The,59</i>)

>  They [sc. farmers] are alright as long as they get a "guaranteed price". <i>(Nov 24, 1938,NZ Observer,8</i>)

>  [Nash] We shall set up machinery to fix the value of the land, from which the farmer gets his product, at a figure that is sustained by that guaranteed price. <i>(1936,NZ Parliamentary Debates,244/683</i>)

>  My objections to the Guaranteed Price are many. <i>(Dec 7, 1938,Tomorrow,93</i>)

>  Uppermost in the dairy-farmers' thoughts and conversations to-day are the recently announced guaranteed prices. <i>(Sep 28, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,78</i>)

### guffy
 
 <i>n.</i> spotty <i>Pseudolabrus celidotus.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> We shook these in the water and chased the fish downstream. In this way we caught hosts of `creekies' (minnows) and bullies and sometimes enough whitebait for a fritter each for tea. As well, we went fishing for `guffy' from the rocks. Often we caught a crayfish and at low tide sometimes several. ... (1890; Akaroa) <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,133</i>) [NOTES: >1890 Akaroa in Sutton-Smith History of Children's Play (1981) 133]

>  So [OEDS2] -1944] omit. <i>(1944,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),190,7th ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> SPOTTY (PAEKIRIKIRI) <i>Pseudolabrus (Lunolabrus) celidotus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) ... [<i>ibid.</i> 275] The name Spotty is derived from the presence of spots on each side of the fish. The name Butterfish used in the North Island for them is from the yellow or buttery colouring on the undersurface. In Canterbury they are called Guffy, but for what reason I have yet to learn. It is a very pretty fish, more especially when seen in the water. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,272,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROT</b> The popular name [spotty (<i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>) however refers to the general spotty nature of these fishes, and is quite appropriate. It is frequently, but quite incorrectly referred to as the Butterfish, and in Canterbury it is generally known as the `guffy'. <i>Ibid.</i> 170 Spotty Pseudolabrus celidotus Butterfish; Guffy <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,133</i>)

>  <i>guffy</i> A small New Zealand sea fish, edible but not much liked. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),190,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SPOTTY ... <i>Other names: Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>; butterfish, kelpie, guffy. None is widely used. Paketi, pakirikiri (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,257</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SPOTTY [<i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>] ... Other names include paketi, kelpie, guffy, butterfish and pakirikiri. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,22</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> GUFFY The name generally given in Canterbury to the more common Spotty. <i>Ibid.</i> 207 SPOTTY The common little Spotty is known to every boy or girl who has even gone fishing along the tide line. ... Other names are kelpie, guffy, butterfish, pakirikiri and paketi (Maori). Gets its most common name from a large, black spot on its upper side. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,114</i>)

>  <i>guffy n</i>. NZ = SPOTTY n. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,334</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  [species name] spotty 2 .. [scientific name] <i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i> .. [other name(s)] guffy <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] guffy [TS] 1720 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> rock cod <i>Lotella rhacinus.</i>

>  <b>SORENSON</b> COD, ROCK (a) Scientific name: <i>Lotella rhacinus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Rock Cod; Kelp Cod. (c) Other common names: Parrotfish; Soldiers, Kelp, Kelpies, Moreporks, Marblefish, Maori Chief, Wrasse, Bourbon, Guffy, Cloudy Bay Cod, Rasp. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,18</i>)

### gully
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that is broken with gullies.

>  Colson asked Hinde GBP300 for his 37 and a half acres of gulley. <i>(1856,BLANK ENTRY,218</i>) [NOTES: in SCHOLEFIELD (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

>  Very attractive farm .. Flat contour, some gully .. Contour is mainly rolling with a little gully. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,74</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a small ravine; a small deep and steeply-sided valley; an eroded watercourse.

>  `The deep gullies, which were worn by torrents from the hills.' <i>(1773,Hawkesworth's Voyages,iii: 352</i>) [NOTES: >1773. `Hawkesworth's Voyages,' vol. iii. p. 532 Captain Cook's First Voyage, May 30, 1770 [Morris]]

>  <b>LIEUT. BRETON</b> [Morris] `Gullies or ravines' In the Gullies high Timber remained. <i>(1833,Excursions in NSW (etc.),166</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> Then there's what we call a gully which of course we take to mean / Just a small and narrow valley in which bush is sometimes seen; / You perchance, were you a new chum might describe it as a dell, / Bushy gully suits me better, serves my purpose just as well. <i>Ibid.</i> 3 (`An Old Chum on New Zealand Scenery') Then there's what we call a gully, which of course we take to mean/ Just a small and narrow valley, in which bush is sometimes seen;/ Bushy gully suits me better, serves my purpose just as well;/ ... Bush, too, means the native forest; you will never, I'm afraid,/ Hear a self-respecting bushman call a bush a leafy glade,/ ... <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,2</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> ~ a narrow valley. The word is very common in Australia, and is frequently used as a place-name. It is not, however, Australian. Dr. Skeat (`Etymological Dictionary') says, `a channel worn by water.' Curiously enough, his first quotation is from `Capt. Cook's Third Voyage,' b. iv. c. 4. Skeat adds, `formerly written gullet:... <i>(1898,Austral English,177</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Roads, as the term is generally understood, were for a time unknown. At first, a pathway through the bush or along the felled road line, diving ... into a deep gulley, or skirting a slippery sideling, and barely passable for a horse; then a formed clay track, ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,1</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Past fern-girt gully and gurgling creek, with a wealth of bush beside;/ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,103</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> [Every] gully and every mountain side of our back ranges will be cultivated. <i>(1912,Impressions of a NZ Pastoralist on Tour,147</i>)

>  Gulley.- The depression between two spurs on a hill. A small valley. A depression on the flat caused by an old watercourse. <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland [get full cit]]

>  <b>AYSON</b> Thomas found that he had to ride along a ridge at the edge of the bush over very rough country consisting of rocks, scrub, flax, crab holes, and deep little gullies running into the bush between each ridge. <i>(1937,Thomas,84</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Gully ... is used in a wider sense than in America, being applied to any ravine or valley formed by steep hillsides. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,86</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> On the tussocky sides of gullies the pleasant-tasting snowberries grew in profusion, while a berry known as the wind-berry grew on a little plant similar to the snowberries and both kinds were eaten and enjoyed. <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,5</i>) [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 5]

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> Around [the Four Peaks Pleasant Gully Hut] there were numerous gullies filled with bush. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,17</i>) [NOTES: 1952 Blakiston My Yesteryears [f. c1869] 17]

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: gully <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  ~ A small narrow valley or stream-bed. spg gulley <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),190,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> Around and beneath us scrub-filled gulleys fell away to green flats .. <i>(1959,Stone, The,56</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Wild turkeys were run in flocks, sometimes 40 to 50 in a mob, would take to wing, and fly hundreds of yards across gullies to a distant hillside. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,120</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> gully: deep valley with steep slopes and not usually very wide. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary to 1964 Sargeson Collected Stories 1935-1963 300]

>  ... an army of tree-ferns marching up some lost and [51] conquered gulley, ... <i>(1971,VUWTC'71,50</i>)

>  Gully: A small valley <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)

>  <b>HODDER</b> Then there are hill and gully excursions [for picnics]. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,56</i>)

>  Tramping boots crunch on the gravel of the Translator Track as it edges around gullies full of bush. <i>(Sep 27, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> Near Drybread are three baches in a gully running back into the Dunstan Range. <i>(2007,Drybread,7</i>)

>  He.. came upon a place.. close by a gully, where flies were buzzing about. <i>(Jan 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  the gullies one long black mid-afternoon yawn... <i>(Apr 13, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> And so we went up the orchard gully - there was a creek, and up the orchard gully was a beautiful sun-trap where fig trees, pears, apples, plums and cherries were all planted. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,170</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The Plains were covered with Fern or with Tea trees or Kicaitore, in the Gullies high Timber remained, .... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),63,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We had several deep little gullies to pass over which are both unsafe and unpleasant for Equestrians. <i>(Mar 13, 1839,Journal,2/73</i>)

>  <b>F & S MATHEW</b> A small creek discharged itself into the lake, coming out of a gulley.<i>Ibid.</i>1/106 [Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ] We traversed the site of the intended town ... [sc. Russell]; the country seems everywhere the same, hills and dales covered with thick brushwood, the real scrub of New South Wales, and divided by deep ravines and gullies full of luxuriant vegetation, ... </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 2/6 Every little gully leading from the hills forms the course of a fine running stream. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/8 Crossing a deep and broken gully the sides of which are so precipitous that you would have stared had you been told you were to ride up them without dismounting. <i>Ibid.</i> 3/26 [Sarah] In some of the deep glens or gullies, that we passed. <i>(1840,Journal,1/30</i>)

>  The timber grows principally in the gullies between the hills and mountains. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,19/244</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> ... but as these gardens are always in the small level spots in the vicinity of the gullies, their produce is no criterion of the general goodness of the soil, ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,25,1925 ed.</i>)

>  Stream and gullies ... and extensive flats. <i>(1843,Letters from Settlers,11</i>) [NOTES: (1842) 1843 Letters from Settlers 11 (HS Tiffin)]

>  (CH Kettle (surveyor)) We came to a running gully. <i>(1843,NZ Journal,95/219</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> We intended to cross this range and made therefore for one of the gulleys..... we found a fine potato-ground in the gulley, and leeks and cabbages growing wild! <i>(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/378</i>)

>  <b>BARNICOAT</b> ... on clambering over a gulley. <i>(1843,Journal,108</i>)

>  (Clifford) ... owing to deep gullies with which it is intersected <i>(Apr 3, 1844,NZ Company Reports,XVII (1845-62)</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Prick shrub, Tumatakuru, in the deep gullies <i>(1844,Journal,69</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... a spur of the table-land separated by two deep gullies. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/224</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> .. the whole country in the neighbourhood is a succession of hills and gullies. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/85</i>)

>  <b>WAKFEIELD</b> These ridges vary in steepness, ... and the intervening spaces are either precipitous gullies or valleys of easier slope, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 210 ... the steep sides of the picturesque gullies, which numerous tributary streams have cut out of the table-land on their way to the river. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,127</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> ... it was a succession of plunges, jumping, slipping, stumbling, and falling among interlaced roots, fallen trees, deep holes, bogs, streams, and gullies. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,36</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> The further we advanced up the valley the worse we found the walking, the gulleys deep and the streams rapid and numerous. <i>(Mar 11, 1853,Traveller under Concern,86,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> As we proceed northward, these gullies become deeper and more frequent <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,63</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  ... he was living ... on the spot ... to prevent the sheep crossing over a narrow pass in the gully, by which they would be liable to go astray ... <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Between the Rakaia and Mr. M---'s station is a place they call the half-way gully, but it is neither a gully nor half-way, being only a grip in the earth, causing no perceptible difference in the level of the track, and extending but a few yards on either side of it. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,vii/104</i>) [NOTES: 1860 in 1863 Butler First Year vii/104]

>  <b>WALKER</b> (Central Otago) Found ourselves in the evening on a high gulley where somebody had sunk a hole but found nothing. <i>(Jan 11, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... it is a constant object for my walks up the gullies, exploring little patches of bush to search for ferns, which grow ... by the creek. <i>Ibid.</i> (1874) 94 Ravines and steep gullies. <i>ibid.</i> 176 ...numerous deep ravines or gullies as we call them. <i>Ibid.</i> (1870) 176 The hill-track was about as bad ... with ... creeks at the bottom of the numerous deep ravines, or gullies as we call them. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,64,1870 ed.</i>)

>  This was Wetherstone's Gully ... `Gully' meaning nothing more than a strip of ground lying between two hills, and having a `creek' flowing down its centre. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> The fore-ground is open fern land, partially broken and intersected by a fresh-water stream and one or two shallow gullies. <i>(1879,Waikato War,56</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> The narrowed gully - just a ditch/ With flowering koromiko rich -/ [fn] Veronica. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/122</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small valley or ravine.

>  <b>ADAMS</b> As we proceed northward, these gullies become deeper and more frequent. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,63</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> .. creeks at the bottom of the numerous deep ravines or gullies as we call them. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,176</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> .. up the track past the house and on across the horse paddock to the mouth of the bush gully. <i>(1954,On the Hill,17</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a place where gold is found. [goldmining]

>  Every week tells us of some new gully being discovered. <i>(Jul 4, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  The battery of the Undaunted Company, Nuggety Gully, is almost complete, and the mine manager (Mr Porter) fully anticipates commencing crushing operations in two or three weeks at the outside. .. A party of three Chinamen are reported to have struck a patch in a small gully at the Stony Creek crossing. .. Miners who happen to be well acquainted with the locality state that several very heavy finds were obtained the good old days in the same gully. <i>(Oct 7, 1882,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  The Golden Crescent had 36oz out of their last paddock when they cleaned up on the 9th inst., after about two weeks' work, which finished them with the gully. They did not work quite up to the top of the gully, but as far up as any mining had been done before. <i>(Apr 24, 1901,Otago Witness,20</i>)

### gully bag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> `There will be nothing improper: it will be very different from the assembly dances [in 1916] in your town where the cream-puffs, the jam-tarts and the gulley bags all gather for a fling. <i>(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,28</i>)

### gully bed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the bottom of a gully or gorge.

>  It is important .. that more gullies be planted, because stable gully beds are a big factor in successful hill country farming. Stable gully beds facilitate the maintenance of stock crossings and fencelines ... <i>(Jun 1958,NZ Journal of Agriculture,560</i>)

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> The almost vertical walls of deep, badland gullies collapse, as unit blocks into the gully bed ... <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,55</i>)

### gully fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cyathea cunninghamii</i>

>  <i>Cyathea Cunninghamii </i> <i>(1912,NZ Ferns</i>)

>  <b>DOBBIE</b> (And) <i>Cyathea Cunninghamii </i> <i>(1921,NZ Ferns</i>)

>  <b>DOBBIE</b> (And) <i>Cyathea Cunninghamii </i> gully-fern <i>(Nov 21, 1921,Letters</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Cyclosorus penniger</i> (Forst.f.) Copel. Common name: <i>Gully fern</i> Maori name: <i>Piupiu</i> ... This common fern is found in woods throughout New Zealand. It has been placed in <i>Polypodium, Goniopteris, Aspidium, Nephrodium, Dryopteris</i>, and <i>Thelypteris</i>. [Williams identifies as <i>Blechnum</i> spp.] <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,28</i>)

>  More palatable are the young fiddleheads of many species, especially the hen and chickens fern, kiokio (<i>Blechnum novae-zealandiae</i>), gully fern (<i>Pneumatopteris pennigera</i>, shining spleenwort (<i>Asplenium oblongifolium</i>) and the shield ferns (<i>Polystichum richardii</i> and <i>P/vestitum</i>). <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/77</i>)

### gully head
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a beginning of a valley, as it develops from a hillside.

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> Fog lay thick in all the gully heads. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,73</i>)

>  <b>LAFFAN</b> All gully heads occur in the shoulder position in the landscape. <i>(1973,Soils and Tunnel-gully Erosion,128</i>)

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> .. patches of mist were gathering in the gully heads ... <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,91</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> The dogs are kennelled in a gully head where the mutton bones go to die. <i>(1995,Many Coated Man, A,147</i>)

### gully heading
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) erosion at the head of a valley where a whole hillside is inclined to slip downwards.

>  <b>Lands & Survey</b> In 1958, severe gully heading became apparent and was tackled locally by retiring and replanting the trouble spots ... <i>(1975,Prosperity from Pumice Land,33</i>)

### gully screamer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) sheep farmer [occ. musterer.]

>  Fundamental difference between 'gully screamer' (sheep/beef farmer) and 'gumbooter' (dairy farmer). <i>(Apr 4, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,20</i>)

### gully trap
 
 <i>n.</i> a water-sealed trap in the outlet from sinks, basins, etc to outside drains [In common use but no citations and not found in dictionaries. Jim Cameron writes : In NZPOD and Macquarie 1981 but not DNZE. Not in NSOD or Collins. OED entry and citation dated 1892. Informants do not recall its use in England in modern times. Is it still current there?]

### gully tree fern
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Cyathea cunninghamii</i> (See [also gully fern](../A#also-gully-fern))

>  <b>F15</b> The Gully tree fern, another giant, might be easily confused with mamku [sic], were it not for its preference for damp valleys. <i>(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion Post</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> The ... gully tree fern (<i>C[yathea] cunninghamii</i>) has similar trunk characteristics. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,86</i>)

>  Two tree ferns, the mamaku and the gulley tree fern (<i>Cyathea cunninghamii</i>) are emergent species, while others, such as the soft tree fern, silver fern and wheki (<i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>) are sub-canopy species. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/76</i>)

### gully workings
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EVANS</b> These questions have reference to gulley workings mainly as I am aware the miner cannot cut a Race on those Runs to work the terraces with ... <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,18</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Nov 30 in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 18]

### gully-rake whip
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TE MANUWIRI</b> ... cracking my gully-rake whip <i>(1907,Sketches of Early Colonization,66</i>)

### gully-raker
 
 <i>n.</i> [NZ & Aus, stockwhip]

>  gully-raker n. a long whip used by drivers of bullock teams, etc. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  gully-raker n. bullock driver or drover's long whip; whence gully-raking, cattle duffing by raiding gullies. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

>  gully-raker NZ & Austral. colloq. cattle-thief, stockwhip. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,334</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> [gully raker]

>  <b>LYON</b> ... indeed, I have known hatters or gully rakers, as men fossicking up back creeks were called, who relied very largely on ... their dogs' hunting success. <i>Ibid.</i> 158 On all goldfields the hatter, the fossicker and the gully raker were a feature. The former might have an isolated claim which he worked without a mate; ... The two latter were generally to be found in out-of-the-way creeks and gullies where they often obtained a fair amount of gold ... . <i>(1952,Faring South,103</i>)

>  The annual picnic of the Presyterian Sunday school took place yesterday. .. The Grey River Argus says of the entertainment that it is "the oddest, absurdest, drollest, and altogether the most amusing that has ever shaken the wrinkles out of an old gully-raker." <i>(Dec 27, 1884,West Coast Times,2</i>)

### gully-raking
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering] a searching of gullies and steep, awkward places for small groups of sheep during a muster.

>  A man could get a bit of sheep work, footrotting down on the plains or gully raking on the high country, until late in May ... <i>(May 9, 1962,Weekly News,38</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> Cattle roundup, mountain muster, gully raking through the fern ... [gully-raking] <i>(2000,Country Bloke and Other Verse, The,133</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> .. two of the more experienced musterers may be kept on to do some gully-raking - searching for small groups of sheep which can be brought in. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,124</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHARPE</b> If no more than a couple of hundred sheep have been missed, two or more experienced musterers may be kept on to do some gully-raking - searching for small groups of sheep which can be brought in. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,124</i>)

### gully-riven
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> The gully-riven littoral of Waireka, five miles south- west of New Plymouth was the theatre of an engagement ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/171</i>)

### gully-seamed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> The Kapenga tableland ..., a gully-seamed broken plateau, is covered with a thick growth of manuka and monoao shrubs, tutu and fern, with many ti or cabbage trees ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/399,1983 ed.</i>)

### gullying
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but sp. to NZ pastoral high country] erosion on steep country where gullies are filled with slipped soil.

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> In some cases, when valleys are V-shaped and narrow, gullying is precipitating hundreds of tons of debris directly into the river beds. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,124</i>)

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> .. in recent years the removal of material by the gullying of the displaced masses has played the most impressive part. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,54</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> The most obvious symptoms of soil erosion in hilly country are scarred hillsides due to slips, mass flowage and gullying. <i>(1961,Agriculture,251</i>)

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> Gullying of subsoil after the surface has been stripped by frost and wind erosion, on the Blowhards. <i>(1973,Hold This Land,40</i>)

### gullyraker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shepherd or musterer working in high or broken country.

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> Dominion of the gullyraker, place of mountains, tall and steep ... <i>(2000,Country Bloke and Other Verse, The,132</i>)

>  a musterer: a gullyraker. a shepherd: a gullyraker. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### gullyrunner
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shepherd.

>  a shepherd - a gullyrunner. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### gulper
 
 <i>n.</i> [Cf growler, grunter.]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Gulper <i>Eurypharynx pelecanoides</i> Vaillant <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,398</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 398]

### gum
 
 <i>n.</i> the gum of speargrass, used as a scent by early Maori

>  gum - tallama'er [taramea] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,109,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: also in Begg (1979), p. 282]



 
 <i>n.</i> As a tree-name for (mainly) Australian varieties of eucalypt, occas. for New Zealand genera; and for transf. and fig. uses (See [gum tree](../G#gum-tree))

>  We are familiar with the blue-gum and the Pinus insignis, with the ... macrocarpa ... <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/621</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Here and there clumps of tall trees rise above the shrubs, and ... there is a thick plantation of red and blue gums, to shelter the garden ... <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,26,1870 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> variant of by gum!

>  Gum!, by A mild oath: low coll., and dial.: from ca. 1825. Pierce Egan in The Life of an Actor. God corrupted; or, as Ware suggests, a telescoping and slovening of God almighty. In C. 20, esp. in Australia and New Zealand, often gum! <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,361</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [see also bush gum, fossil gum, swamp gum, resin, an occas. early use, occurs now ? esp. in technical writing:]

>  Note. In speaking of Mercury Bay I had forgot to mention that the Mangrove trees found there produce a resinous substance very much like rosin, something of this kind I am told is found in both the East and West Indias. We found it first in small lumps upon the Sea beach, but afterwards found it sticking to the Mangrove trees and by that means found out from whence it had come. [fn 1955] ... The `resinous substance' was kauri gum ,the exudation of the great kauri tree, Agathis australis, washed down the river ..., or caught in the mangroves on the way.] <i>(Nov 16, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/204,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> [to GUM] ... we looked about in search of resin, which is brought down by the streams from various parts of the interior. ... [305] ... The pine, called by the natives cowree abounds with resin; and I believe it is the only tree of that species in the island that has any. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/304</i>)

>  <b>DARWIN</b> It is said, that by digging in the barest spots, lumps of the kind of resin which flows from the kauri pine are frequently found. [441, Dec 24] ... a quantity of resin oozes from the bark [of the kauri], which is sold at a penny a pound to the Americans, but its use was then unknown.] <i>(Dec 23, 1835,Voyage of the Beagle,(ch. 18) 408, 441,(Everyman edn) ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> A great quantity of white Transparent Gum is found about the root of the Coudie. <i>(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),33,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Kauri (Dammaris Australis, or Pinus Kauri). This tree is of the genus Pine; ... It will scar[c]ely be believed by an English timber-merchant, that I have measured a Kauri-tree whose circumference was forty feet ... the gum oozing out of it, when the bark was wounded, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 37 From the trunk of the [kauri] tree oozes a gum, insoluble in water ...; also a kind of resin, which will answer the purpose of that useful article in ship-building. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),36</i>)

>  <b>LANG</b> Kauri Gum has recently been sold in quantities to the Americans for [pounds] 18 a ton.] <i>(1839,New Zealand (various authors/titles),59</i>)

>  <b>WARD</b> gum or resin. [FN] Mr Darwin states that this resin is collected and sold at a penny a pound to the Americans but its use is kept secret. <i>(1840,Important Information Relative to NZ,34</i>) [NOTES: (Wm Yate)]

>  <b>WILKES</b> ... potatoes and gum are also exported; ... <i>(1852,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/318</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar in 1852 Wilkes U.S. Exploring Expedition 1/318]

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> The natives are fond of chewing the gum of the kowdie tree. <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,26</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The produce is Flax, Pork, Potatoes, Corn and Gum etc. <i>(Dec 25, 1842,NZ Journal,35,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The Kauri produces large quantities of resinous gum, which is very hard, transparent, and tasteless. It has been stated that this rosin is only soluble by means of heat, ... <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),44</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> ... it is agreed between us to collect gum say from 50 to 60 tons of gum of a good kind ... <i>(1972,Gumdiggers, The,104</i>) [NOTES: 1845 Nov 22 Agreement Mair and Clarke in 1972 Reed Gumdiggers 104]

>  kauri gum, the (usually hardened fossilised) resin from the kauri tree; kauri gum, is, and perhaps was, the common usage (except in combination, and, perhaps, on the Northland gumfields c1870-1900). <i>(Apr 1906,Macmillan's Magazine,478.1</i>)

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> Wheat, gum ... flax are in great demand. <i>(1851,Letters & Journals,2/248</i>)

>  Freeman asked him where he was going, to which he replied he was going to see him dig gum. <i>(Apr 29, 1911,NZ Truth,305/5/4</i>)

>  ... but near Kaitaia ... it is growing on deeply shaded clay banks ..., and also on the margins [77] of `potholes' from which gum has been dug on the moorlands. <i>(1915,TrNZI,47/76</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> The low rolling clay hills had ... been the site of a vast kauri forest, and, being so remote, was still virgin country as far as gum diggers were concerned. ... Armed with a long spear of rod iron, we `diggers' tested these hillocks for gum. <i>(1951,Confessions,40</i>)

>  [fn 1955] ... The `resinous substance' was kauri gum,the exudation of the great kauri tree, Agathis australis, washed down the river ..., or caught in the mangroves on the way.] <i>(1955,Cook's Journals</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Beaglehole (ed. fn)to 1769 Nov 16 Cook Journals (1955) 1/204]

>  kauri ... 3. Kauri gum', in New Zealand, is the resin given out by the kauri tree and used in making varnish. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),190,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> When the spear met with obstruction an experienced digger knew at once ... whether he had located gum ... <i>(1970,Way Up North,30</i>)

>  gum ...5. N.Z. See kauri gum <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,498</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 335 & 410; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 220]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MANDER</b> ...Bruce told them they could get a gum outfit at the Point Curtis pub, ... and that they would be shown the way to the gum-field. ... <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,59</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Almost every shelf in the hut was loaded with gum carvings. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,193</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> names for commercial or export grades and kinds of gum.

>  <b>PATTERSON</b> If [the gum] is perfectly cleaned and of good quality, [the digger] sells it as `rescraped' or `first-class gum', the proportionate value of which is nearly double that of ordinary - that is, `half-scraped'. Then there is `three-quarter scraped]', a quality and value intermediate; `inferior ordinary' and `washed nuts' completing the list, be the gum white, black or bush. <i>(1904,Kauri-Gum,7</i>)

>  The terms dust, seeds, chips, nubs, & c. are the names given to certain of the lower kauri gum grades according to the relative sizes of the pieces. From `dust' the size of the fraqments increases in the order given, as does also the purity of the grade. Chalk, or `chalky gum,' is a particular grade recovered from swampy country and is characterized by its white or chalky appearance and large water content. <i>(1935,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,17/371</i>) [NOTES: (Hosking)]

>  <b>REED</b> (v. p62 plate) Black sugar, Chalk, Bright Seeds, Dial or Bright, Black Flour Dust, Bright Dust, Rescraped Bush, Bright Chips. [see alsoBUSH=BLED GUM] <i>(1948,Gumdigger, The,56</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Some of the classifications under which gum was exported give an indication of the appearance of some of the grades: bright, rescraped bush, bright chips, black flour dust, chalk, bright seeds and black sugar are some of the names. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,70,1960 ed.</i>)

### gum bleeder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Subsequently European gum bleeders devised a method of scaling the trunk using 12-inch long climbing hooks or hand spikes, and 1 1/2 inch toe spiked fixed to wooden-soled boots. <i>(1973,Kauri,23</i>)

### gum climber
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Gum climbers and bleeders scaled kauri using a weighted line and rope, and later, taking advantage of kauri's soft outer layer of bark and sapwood, hand spikes, climbing hooks, and boots equipped with toe spikes. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,32</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a harvester of kauri ''bush'' gum from living trees.

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> He was a good fencer, bullock driver and gum climber but not so good as a farmer. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,74</i>)

### gum country
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) land on which kauri gum has been collected and debris is present.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Whatever the causes this burnt kauri country - known as 'gum country' - was considered to be of little value. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,199</i>)

>  This experiment was taken with a view to seeing if by some such means partially drained swamps in gum country could be brought into some degree of profit ... <i>(Jun 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,517</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (in Northland) (See [also kauri-gum land](../A#also-kauri-gum-land))

>  ... the road followed the watershed ... passing over open `gum' country with a very monotonous vegetation. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/351</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> For its size the kauri has little leaf fall, and with nothing going back into the ground it takes heavy toll of the country. It seems that the kauri gum, which over the years or centuries has permeated the soil, has also had an adverse effect on fertility. Another theory is that in this high-rainfall country excessive leaching of the denuded areas has done considerable harm. Whatever the causes this burnt kauri country - known as `gum country' - was considered to be of little value. <i>(1972,Wake of The Axe,199</i>)

### gum hair
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> He made delicate posies and circlets of flowers from the white `candle-gum', and clusters of `grapes' from the smoky `black-jack', and from both white and yellow gum he spun great tresses of `gum hair' that might have honoured any museum in the world. <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,154</i>)

### gum hunting
 
 <i>n.</i> gumdigging [[In 1840 1) gum could be picked up off the surface of the ground (cf gum (n) 1839 quote) 2) gold-digging was not current as a popular term.] =]

>  Smith, who has a turn for gum hunting. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,24/302</i>) [NOTES: (S. Revans.)]

### gum sack
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MANDER</b> [A gumfield storekeeper speaks] `I've got some good fresh ship's biscuit, if that's any good to you.' `Yes. I'll take a couple of pounds.' Allen stuffed the things into his gum-sack. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,96,1971 ed.</i>)

### gum shack
 
 <i>n.</i> a shack used by gumdiggers

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> It looked nothing like the picture of Babylon in our Bible History; some old gum shacks, empty now, here and there a rough dwelling, abandoned heaps of gum chips, an empty store. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,74</i>)

### gum store
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ADAMS</b> The Lichtenstein, Arnoldson and Company gum store, a building of four storeys, went up in flames. <i>(1973,Kauri,26</i>)

### gum tree
 
 <i>n.</i> [also elliptical gum, used for Australian eucalypt and its timber.]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> He used Gum tree from Sydney for Keels, Coudy planks, Hoc-car [aka] Timber and Kicaitore Knees, sometimes Pouriedie Keels. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),34,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... the plantations about it ... have grown up well, and the willows, gum-trees, and poplars shelter it ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,84,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> ... the slab whare [e-acute] will have given place to the weatherboard cottage, garden and [3] orchard will now be visible, and the gum-tree of Australia ... will be preparing to take the place of their dispossessed ... brethren of New Zealand ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,2</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> She pulled up her gloves, hummed to herself and said to the distant gum-tree, `Shan't be long now.' But that was hardly company. ' <i>(1922,Stories,458,(1984 Alpers) ed.</i>)

>  <i>gum tree n. </i>popular name for any species of Eucalyptus, of which there are more than 100 varieties; the exuded resinous sap of certain trees; (colloq. phrase) up a gum tree, in a difficult situation. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [is this NZ-grown bluegum, or a native??]

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a deep depression left in gum-land country following kauri gumdiggings.

 <i>n.</i>

### gum-spear
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a spear or thin steel shaft used for probing the soil for kauri gum

>  <b>HAY</b> When the end of the [gum-] spear touches buried gum, there is a peculiar clip or feel, which the digger knows.] <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,26</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> They generally work in parties ... taking it in turn to remain at home to ... scrape the gum clean ... The first time I saw them at work with their gum-spears, they puzzled me ... The `spear' is simply a spike of iron, about a foot long, on the end of a pole ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,54</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> It is commonly said that a blanket, a spade, a gum-spear, ... a billy, ... [etc] constitute all that a gum-digger needs in the way of outfit. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/25</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> A gumdigger's outfit ... consists of a spade, a gum spear and a piece of sacking ... the gum spear is a four-sided rod of steel, about four feet long, and pointed at one end. It looks very like a fencing foil, with a handle like a spade stuck in the end of it, instead of a hilt. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,147</i>)

>  The ordinary method of searching for the gum is by first feeling for it a little below the surface with a steel-pointed piece of iron about 1/2in. in diameter and 4ft. long, called a `gum-spear' ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,155</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> A gum spear and a spade leaned against the sack wall. <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,232,1974 ed.</i>)

>  gum-spear: long pointed spear for probing ground for kauri gum. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> `What's this?' I asked him. `A gum-spear, boy. A gum-spear. Used to work with it on the gumfields.' He took hold of the spear and gave me a demonstration of how to use it when searching for kauri gum in the earth. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,112</i>)

>  gum spear n. Tapered steel spear up to six feet long used by gum diggers to locate deposits. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The gum-spear, a slender, rapier-like instrument, fitted with an ordinary spade handle, varied in length from about three feet for use in shallow ground, to twenty-five or more for working deep swamps. Some time after its introduction, the spear came to be fitted with what was called the `joker,' or `toggle'. This was a coil of fine wire, bound round the spear [50] a few inches from the point. It allowed the spear to be thrust into the ground with the minimum of resistance, there being no `drag' on the shaft. It is said that the forerunner of the `joker' was discovered by accident. A digger, pushing his spear into the ground, found that it had penetrated a boot-eyelet, and was surprised to find that, instead of forming an obstruction, the spear penetrated the ground more easily; the coil of fine wire was devised as an improvement. An experienced digger could detect at once, by the feel, and the resistance met by the spear, whether he had struck timber, a stone or gum. <i>(1972,Gumdiggers, The,49</i>)

>  The constable held the dangerous job of sole policeman at Hikurangi where the infuriated gum-digger holds sway and if necessary charges with a gum spear. <i>(Sep 27, 1913,NZ Observer,17</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a large rubber overshoe ranging from calf to knee height; a waterproof rubber boot, reaching to the knee (usu. pl.). (= Brit. wellington)

### gumboot clone
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a vine cutting.

>  I'd always meant to have a yarn to Willie about the 'gumboot' or 'Abel' clone and foolishly kept putting it off. The gumboot clone (a clone in this case is simply a vine cutting) was so called because it was discovered inside the gumboot of a returning Kiwi by a customs officer at Auckland airport. It is seen as important to have a mixture of different clones to make great pinot and Ata Rangi owes about a third of its content to the gumboot clone. <i>(Sep 4, 2002,Capital Times,25</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> . and Paul Juanavich, then the only gumbuyer in Gumtown, paid us five shillings per sack. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,254</i>)
