>  <b>REED</b> Mr. Joe Thomas... told me that he was a gum - buyer, as his father had been before him; that there was still a market for good quality gum. <i>(1961,North Cape to Bluff,31</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> Storekeeper, gumbuyer, stockman and one-time whaler, no picture of Houhora's early days would be complete without him. <i>(1970,Way Up North,49</i>)

### gumdigger
 
 <i>n.</i> [1953 14 M A10 Thames DHS 24 GUM-DIGGER (DENTIST)]

>  ...Annie Pringle, ..., sued a dentist ... for the maintenance of her illegitimate child. Lawyer R. Moody, of Auckland, appeared for the gum-digger. <i>(Jun 7, 1913,NZ Truth,415/7/7</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealanders have coined or adapted many expressions to meet local requirements, as illustrated by the following: ... gumdigger: dentist <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> a dentist is a gumdigger <i>(1941,NZ Slang,52</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> gum digger: adolescent's familiar and somewhat derogatory reference to the family DENTIST. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,49</i>)

>  gumdigger/gumpuncher dentist; from diggers for kauri gum. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,53</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a person who dug for kauri gum

>  gum digger n. digger for resinous gum on sites of ancient kauri forests; dentist. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  Of the kauri gum diggers 9/10ths are furnished by this tribe, & 2/3rds of the Auckland prostitutes. <i>(1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/437,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (F.D. Fenton)]

>  <b>LUSH</b> We passed through an encampment of Kauri gum diggers. <i>(Feb 4, 1867,Waikato Journals,104,1982 ed.</i>)

>  ... a gum-digger ... <i>(Jan 4, 1871,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... you really do meet here and among the gum-diggers in the north, the strangest mixture of refinement and rough life. <i>Ibid.</i> 53 Our nearest neighbours are a colony of gum-diggers, at the Kaiwaka swamp ...; they have built quite a little village of these `nekau' huts ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,33</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> ... Maori gum-diggers ... and the bushmen ... on the island having mostly brought about this result. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,153</i>)

>  At that price, gum-diggers would earn from 30/- to [pounds] 4 a week. <i>(1875,Official Handbook of NZ,252</i>) [NOTES: Vogel]

>  ... when `the heroes of a hundred drunks' are helplessly prone on the floor [of a Kaipara bar] ... a couple of stretcher men speedily whip away the `dead drunk' off to an outbuilding in the rear ... known as `the dead house', there to sleep off the effects of the debauch. On one Sunday, 18 bushmen and gumdiggers were lying `heads and tails' in the deadhouse ... <i>(Jun 10, 1876,Weekly Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1876 June 10 Weekly Herald in 1987 Scott Seven Lives on Salt River 52]

>  The Kauri mills and Kauri gumdiggers constitute the population [of Mercury Bay], a rough lot. <i>(Jun 24, 1879,Chudleigh Diary,281,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The finding [of kauri-gum], collecting and bringing to market, affords a sufficiently profitable occupation to have constituted a distinct class of men, who go by the name of gum-diggers. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/245 He would fight and brawl with any sawyers or gum-diggers that might be hanging about; ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/15</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> The gumdigger therefore plays a most important part in the province of Auckland ... he is universally looked down upon by the sober-sided settler ... `He's [144] only a gumdigger,' is an expression I have commonly heard used, to imply that the individual indicated was a person of no importance. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,143</i>)

>  So so long as the farmer persists in cutting down the native growth ..., or so long as the gum-digger and bush-larrikin are allowed to put a match into anything that will burn, there is not much hope for either prevention or restoration. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/496</i>)

>  ... when Flannigan went there with a party of Maori gum-diggers. <i>(Oct 1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,17</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> The wage of the gum-digger is very low, as skilled labour is not necessary, so that it supplies work for anyone who is industriously inclined. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,95</i>)

>  He is described by those who had met him as a hard-working gum-digger, living the life of a recluse, perfectly inoffensive, and quiet. <i>(Apr 29, 1911,NZ Truth,305/5/1</i>)

>  <b>STALLWORTHY</b> In 1890, a meeting of about two hundred gumdiggers was held at Kaihu ... when it was resolved ... to form a Gumdiggers' Union. <i>(1916,Early Northern Wairoa,54</i>)

>  Gum-diggers' camps are scattered over the whole area <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/189</i>) [NOTES: (Ferrar)]

>  There were three main types of transaction in which a gum-digger engaged: the payment of royalty; the selling of the gum he dug; and the buying of provisions and stores. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/95</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Disaster came about when the splendid-looking, leonine-bearded Scot heard a fellow digger's strictures on the probable circumstances of his birth. <i>(1934,Journalese,226</i>)

>  gum-digger : one who digs for kauri gum. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> She lugs in a kerosene-tin full of newly gathered kauri-gum nuts ... The Hoppy Crummers are supposed to live on the sale of kauri-gum nuts - the dirty little nobs of kauri-gum that the gum-diggers wouldn't bother with. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,55,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> (gloss.) kauri gum: gum deposits from the enormous kauri of Northland, formerly collected by gumdiggers and exported for use in the paint and varnish industry. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,273,(1974 ed.Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> The low rolling clay hills had ... been the site of a vast kauri forest, and, being so remote, was still virgin country as far as gum diggers were concerned. ... Armed with a long spear of rod iron, we `diggers' tested these hillocks for gum. <i>(1951,Confessions,40</i>)

>  gumdigger n, NewZeal: one that digs fossil kauri resin <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1011</i>)

>  gum digger n. digger for resinous gum on sites of ancient kauri forests; dentist. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> To see a fit and experienced gumdigger sinking a hole large enough to allow freedom of action, deep enough to get the gum, ... was to see something special as a test of competence, fitness and endurance. <i>(1970,Way Up North,30</i>)

>  gum-digger n. N.Z. a person who digs for fossilized kauri gum in a gum-field, an area where it is found buried. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,498</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 335; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 220]]

>  The man then, according to prisoner, attacked him with a gum-digger's spear. <i>(Jun 3, 1911,NZ Truth,310/5/1</i>)

>  The constable held the dangerous job of sole policeman at Hikurangi where the infuriated gum-digger holds sway and if necessary charges with a gum spear. <i>(Sep 27, 1913,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  Last summer a gum-digger was seen to fire the scrub near the company's Mangatawhiri bush. <i>(Aug 30, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REED</b> 1 ... tucker - normally bread-and-butter, or cheese, ... wrapped in a piece of newspaper, gum-digger style. <i>(1972,Gumdiggers, The,14</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> 1 ... tucker - normally bread-and-butter, or cheese, ... wrapped in a piece of newspaper, gum-digger style. <i>(1972,Gumdiggers, The,14</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BARLOW</b> The sober-sided settler .... hardly even has a good word for him; `He's only a gumdigger', is an expression I have commonly heard used, to imply that the individual indicated was a person of no importance. The title `gumdigger' itself may have something to do with the matter. It is not a nice word, and looks too much like `gravedigger' at first sight <i>(1889,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,144</i>)

### gumdigger's bitch
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1900 Northland Gumfields from the late Jack McBride p.c. 1984 Aug 6 Mr. F.N. Wardell, 51 Falcon St Christchurch 7 -]

>  <b>McGILL</b> gumdigger's dog, as mad as/as skinny as/as useless as very mad/skinny/stupid/useless etc. Sometimes, particularly in the case of skinny, it is a `gumdigger's bitch.' <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,39</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> gumdigger's dog, as mad as/as skinny as/as useless as very mad/skinny/stupid/useless etc. Sometimes, particularly in the case of skinny, it is a `gumdigger's bitch.' <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,39</i>)

### gumdigger's dog
 
 <i>n.</i> In various expressions of derogatory comparison [Letter from David McKenzie 13 Apr.]

>  <b>HELMER</b> ...he'd rummage about in the tucker for something to take to bed with him. Peas, corned beef, it didn't matter. ... `Musta been a pudgy individual.' `Naw, skinnier'n a gum digger's dog, eh?' <i>(1964,Stag Party,83</i>)

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> ... that squint-eyed kid looked about as stupid as a gumdigger's dog. `Made a Joe Hunt of yourself as usual, ...' <i>(1976,Travelling Man,133</i>)

>  The lambs were breaking away everywhere, the accursed canine useless as a gumdigger's dog, and in fury this peppery Hawke's Bay cocky thrust clenched fists to the sky, yelling: `O God! Send me a dog, send me a dog!' <i>(Oct 28, 1984,NZ Times,13</i>) [NOTES: (J. Henderson)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> useless as a tit on a hand/as a gumdigger's dog very useless; eg `I hate to say this, but when it comes to gardening, you're as useless as a tit on hand/as a gumdigger's dog.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,118</i>)

>  She [sc. Patricia Bartlett] once said "the permissive" Highet's powers to review indecent movies were "as obsolete and powerless as a gumdigger's dog." <i>(Nov 16, 2000,Evening Post,9</i>)

### gumdigger's soap
 
 <i>n.</i> kumarahou. (lathers when rubbed on hands) [(Northland)] (See [kumarahou](../K#kumarahou))

>  Golden Tainui or Kumarahou (Pomaderris kumeraho) - Another fast growing 2-3m high shrub, northern in origin, but certainly at home in Wellington's clay soils... Gum Diggers Soap is another name for this species. When water is added to leaves and rubbed in the hand, a lather forms. Seemingly utilised by the gum diggers of old to clean their hands. <i>(Nov 11, 1993,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  gumdigger's soap [=] kumarahou [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,35</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> POMADERRIS KUMERAHO. Kumarahou is at home in the gumlands of Auckland, those broad tracts of hungry country where great kauri forests once grew, leaving deposits of resin - ... Flower heads rubbed between wet hands make a lather, hence the name `gumdiggers' soap'. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,88</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Pomaderris kumeraho</i> A. Cunn. Common names: Poverty weed, gumdigger's soap said she funked because her knuckle was inside the ring.' <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,84</i>)

>  <b>SALE</b> The kumerahou flowers also had the quality to crush into soapy lather. Hence the name, and presumably the use, of `gumdiggers' soap'. <i>(1982,Four Seasons,32</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> [On gumland] other characteristic shrubs are <i>Dracophyllum lessonianum</i> and the strikingly yellow-flowered kumarahou or `Gumdigger's Soap' (<i>Pomaderris kumeraho</i>). Among herbs, sedges and the umbrella fern (<i>Gleichenia circinata</i>) are prominent. [253: fn 80] Kumarahou flowers rubbed together with water form a sort of lather. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,114</i>)

>  One such plant, the pomaderris kumeraho, did show moderate cleaning ability... It was known last century as gum diggers' soap, referring to its use by the bushmen who worked in the forest felling trees. <i>(Aug 26, 1992,Independent Herald (Wellington),17</i>)

>  One of the most esteemed traditional medicines in the Far North is the boiled leaves of kumarahou or gumdigger's soap. It was used for asthma, colds and bronchitis. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

### gumdiggers' special
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A train leaving Auckland for New Lynn Saturday 1.30pm (up to 1910?): part time gumdiggers caught it to work the fields near Auckland. <i>(1955,NZ Herald</i>) [NOTES: [T. Walsh] 1955 Jan or Feb NZ Herald [p?]]

### gumdiggers' spuds
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The native orchids with their flowers of white, pink, blue or mauve. We knew them as "gumdiggers' spuds" because of their bulbous roots. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,207</i>)

### gumdigging
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>GREY</b> It is what is known as kauri-gum-land and indications of the gum-digging industry presented themselves the whole way through. <i>(1879,His Island Home,34</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> In settlers homes in gum-digging districts are to be found specimens of anateur gum-carving. <i>(1889,Kaikoura Coast,152</i>)

>  <b>ARTHUR</b> .. the little work they do, as in the gum-digging line. <i>(1894,Kangaroo and Kauri,82</i>)

>  <b>STALLWORTHY</b> The gumdigging industry continued to drag along, giving the digger a poor remuneration for his work ... <i>(1916,Early Northern Wairoa,56</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> Ross had learned enough of the facts of the gum-digging life ... to be ... interested in its peculiar atmosphere. <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,229,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Kauri-gum-digging settlement 10 m. [sic] from Hokianga. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,273</i>)

>  <b>BARTLETT</b> On the mantelpiece in the dining room, among the ornaments and family photographs, stood an especially fine piece of kauri gum the colour of clear honey. It had probably been dug by Bob on his farm or on a gum-digging `holiday'. <i>(1974,Emigrants, The,273</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BARLOW</b> In most of the gumdiggers' huts (or whares, as they are called), and in settlers' houses in gumdigging districts, are to be found specimens of amateur gum-carving, among which, hearts are by far the most popular subject. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,152</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... there is not a single kauri pine now standing on the gum-fields; ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,54</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> [Cf gum-hunting, also kauri gum-digging.]

>  Paora Toki is gone gum digging up the Thames. <i>(1871,McLean Papers,37/72</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy)]

>  <b>GREY</b> A great many of the natives abandoned their kaingas and went gum-digging, when that article brought a high price. <i>(1879,His Island Home,34</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Yet gum-digging has often been a great assistance to settlers. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/27</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> Gumdigging is exceptionally hard work ... <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,146</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> `gum-digging' is a standing resource for the unemployed <i>(1889,Forest Flora,155</i>)

>  A curious feature of gum-digging at the Barrier is that they get what is termed `old gum' in the forest as well as in the `open' .. <i>(1890,TrNZI,22/82</i>)

>  The cause of the increased output is ... that a greater number of men now engaged in gum-digging ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,155</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> When the children of the settlers desire a little pocket money, they will often ask permission to go gum-digging in some newly ploughed paddock. <i>(1892,Plants of NZ,64</i>)

>  Gum-digging may be roughly divided into two classes - viz., that on the `winter fields', or the high tea-tree ranges ... <i>(1908,TrNZI,40/170</i>)

>  The last time he saw deceased alive was about 9 a.m. on April 5 going by the Wairakei bridge to follow his occupation of gum-digging, having his pikau, with spear, spade, and axe in his possession. <i>(Apr 29, 1911,NZ Truth,305/5/2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRUCE</b> Purdie the dentist... was ... among the leading practitioners in the somewhat primitive days of the art of `gum digging' <i>(1932,Early Days of Canterbury,135</i>)

### gumdigging camp
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Within a few minutes a crowd of some 40 Maoris from the gum-digging camp.. were on the scene. <i>(Feb 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,45</i>)

### gumfield
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) the land in North Auckland where fossil kauri gum could be found (often pl on analogy of goldfield(s).) [cf.(gum)digging, (gum)diggings]

>  <b>SENIOR</b> The gum-fields ... indicate that the fine forests have disappeared at an alarming rate <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,182</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> There are tracts of country, known as gum-fields, in which kauri-gum is to be dug up most plentifully. [15] Gum-fields are poor lands usually, though some are adapted for settlement. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/14</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> He lives an entirely isolated and a fearfully hard life out on the gumfield ... <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,144</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> A remarkable fact in connection with gumfields is that in many cases ... new resin has been formed after they have been allowed to rest for a few years [With all drawbacks the Auckland gumfields have proved far more beneficial to the district than its goldfields. <i>(1889,Forest Flora,155</i>)

>  <b>BROOKES</b> ... a large gumfield 2 miles to the eastward of Wellsford. <i>(1892,Frontier Life,190</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Among some foreign scum/ I am now upon a gumfield pokin' round for kauri gum!/ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,94</i>)

>  A particularly atrocious murder was committed on the northern gumfields on April 8, and now that the frightful tragedy has come to light great excitement has been created. <i>(Apr 29, 1911,NZ Truth,305/5/1</i>)

>  An employer of labour on the gumfields is almost an unknown quantity. The digger, then, does not receive wages in the general acceptance of the term ... [or ? 1924, 7/95] <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/95</i>) [NOTES: (Firth)]

>  <b>MANDER</b> It was the gumfield that really settled it. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,45,1971 ed.</i>)

>  ... it is almost generally believed in New Zealand that the non-resinous impurities composed of sand, humus, vegetable fibres, charcoal, c., from the gumfields ... are desirable and even necessary ingredients for the manufacture of linoleum. <i>(1935,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,17/370</i>) [NOTES: (Hosking)]

>  gumfield: area where kauri gum is found under ground. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> With every shipload of Dalmatians coming to work on the gumfields that fear grew. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,24</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> When they arrived in Aotearoa, they worked in the gumfields of the north. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,45</i>)

>  Josip Babich, who worked long ours in the gum fields of the Far North to save money for the plot of land where the winery is still situated, would be proud. <i>(Jun 8, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area containing the remnants of kauri trees and gum.

>  <b>HAY</b> Gum-fields are poor lands usually <i>(1883,Brighter Britain,II 14</i>)

>  This county contains every description of country, from .. ordinary bush lands to gumfields. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,231</i>)

### gumfields
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A fire, started by some thoughtless person on the gumfields behind this place, came unpleasantly close to us last Wednesday. <i>(Jan 22, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

### gumland
 
 <i>n.</i> area of poor soil, once supporting kauri forests and containing kauri gum (often pl). Also (rarely) kauri-gum(-)land. [Probably ellipt. for kauri-gum-land, poor quality land, formerly (?originally) under kauri forest, hence where kauri gum has been, or may be, found. ?Sg as a descriptive term - contrast gumlands (see also gum country).]

>  Our road now led over open kauri-gum land with the usual sparse vegetation ... <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/350</i>) [NOTES: (Cheeseman)]

>  Here, then, on this gumland, is where the old Kauri forests grew .... <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,3/203</i>)

>  <b>McKENZIE</b> ... large areas .... were composed of gum-land. <i>(1935,Gael Fares Forth, The,179</i>)

>  In Northland, where such holdings were often scattered and separated by tracts of wild and unreclaimed gumland, development took the form of assistance to individual farmers. <i>(Oct 1952,NZ Geographer,111</i>) [NOTES: Metge]

>  GUMLAND Land with a badly podsolised soil and which once carried kauri forest. The name derives from the fact that such land often yields fossilised kauri gum. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  gum-land The land where the Kauri gum is found by probing and digging. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),190,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> Contiguous to my front boundary was an area of `gumland', poverty stricken stuff covered in fern, scrub, and blackberry .... <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,57</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Another type of shrubland widespread in the north is found on terrain known as `gumland'. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,114</i>)

>  For several miles after leaving Waikiekie to the southward the land is poor, "gum land," as it is usually called. <i>(Feb 26, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ?Mainly land under which kauri gum may be found. [? Plural form reinforced by gumfields, goldfields: check]

>  ... on our farm and in the surrounding bush, though these are distinctly not gum-lands. [check cit] <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/16</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> ... on the comparatively clay hills which are so frequent in the North Auckland Peninsula, and which, from the quantity of kauri-resin that has been dug from them, are locally known as `gum-lands'. <i>(1914,NZ Flora,144</i>) [NOTES: [is this merely the pl. of use 1 above]]

>  Because of the prevalence of the fossil resin `kauri-gum' in the clay stone areas, these are locally known as `gum-lands'. [? merely pl. of 1 above]. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/193</i>) [NOTES: (Given)]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Wherever the soil is extremely poor, as on dry gravel plains, or particularly `sour', as that sterile clay, wet in winter and baked hard in summer, which forms the Auckland gumlands, a more or less dense association of manuka occurs. <i>(1927,NZ Plants,101</i>)

>  This, of course, on the open gum lands of North Auckland in midsummer is far from ideal ... <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/56</i>) [NOTES: (Hansson)]

>  They were originally clothed with Kauri forest, and are fairly typical `gumlands' soil. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,72</i>)

>  It [<i>Evagrostis Brownii</i>] is common in the northern part of North Island on the `gumlands' and in open manuka country ... <i>(1936,DSIR Bulletin,49/67</i>) [NOTES: (Allan)]

>  <b>LEVY</b> Mangroves characterise the tidal mud flats, while certain poor clay soils, popularly known as gum-lands, were dominated by the kauri and its associate the tawhero. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,3,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> POMADERRIS KUMERAHO. Kumarahou is at home in the gumlands of Auckland, those broad tracts of hungry country where great kauri forests once grew, leaving deposits of resin - ... Flower heads rubbed between wet hands make a lather, hence the name `gumdiggers' soap'. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,88</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The gum-land soils of North Auckland are infertile grey to dark-grey compact silts, silt loams and clays usually containing kauri-resin or, as it is usually called kauri-gum. <i>(1934,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,15/408</i>) [NOTES: (Grange)]

>  <b>REED</b> The father ... had come down to the wharf to meet his boys, who were trudging after him to the recently purchased little gumland Government section three miles up in the hills. <i>(1946,Farthest North,14</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Much arduous effort must be put into gum-land country before it will respond, but, given such effort, the response is good. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,15,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Several miles beyond this block, out across the rolling gumland hills, thickly covered with short manuka scrub, lay a back-block section, a bush farm in the early stages of development ... <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,38,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> With gumland rapidly turning into pastoral farms kumarahou is now found mainly in roadside scrub. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,88</i>)

>  SUCCESSION IN GUMLAND SCRUB COMMUNITIES <i>(1987,Triennial Report Botany Division,61,1982-1984 ed.</i>)

### gumless
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  ... late at night, having been temporarily `bushed', he wandered, footsore and gumless, into camp. <i>(Mar 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,429</i>)

### gummint
 
 <i>n.</i> the government

>  The gummint would have to act decisively to ensure women took their proper place on the fire duty rosters. <i>(Feb 27, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  On Easter Monday at Te Papa I witnessed thousands of New Zealanders deliver a message to Prime Minister Helen Clark, and her gummint, though I doubt any of them were there to see it. <i>(May 2, 2000,Evening Post,Flair : 5</i>)

>  Yet again Sandra Coney reminds us that the answer to all our woes lies in gummint, gummint and even more gummint. <i>(Jul 15, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

### gummy
 
 <i>n.</i> elderly person?

>  It's the gummy ones who want to be in the fun. <i>(Jul 19, 1943,NZEF Times,III/108 : 6</i>)

>  Hervey finally flung out to his own quarters with the Parthian shaft that a "gummy" could fill our place better than its present occupant. <i>(1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. / 14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a gumdigger

>  It is there that the gay and festive gummy and wife are found in their pristine loveliness. <i>(Apr 19, 1890,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  The 'pianner' nuisance is becoming intolerable here; even the gummies and Maoris are going in for them. <i>(Dec 6, 1890,NZ Observer,18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> A gum-digger: New Zealand and Australian coll.: C. 20. (O.E.D. Sup.).] A (kauri) gumdigger.

>  We soon were giving those gummies a hand to get some tucker ready <i>(Apr 1906,Macmillan's Magazine,476</i>)

>  gummy ... 2 n. NZ gum-digger; (in pl.) NZ & Austral. gumboots. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,335</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> gummie gumdigger. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,53</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an old ewe that has lost its teeth. [(often plural)]

>  Aust 1965 Gunn.] An old ewe which has lost its teeth. <i>(Sep 21, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Ackand Gloss.]

>  Jerry Fail... attended the sale on the Culverden estate, and bought most of the `gummies' for breeding purposes at as low a price as 1s 3d a-piece. A rumor [sic] that he is to have the barren ancients filled [sic] with false teeth is entirely without foundation. <i>(1908,NZ Truth,152/1/7</i>)

>  Gummy. An old sheep that has lost its teeth. Also used as an adjective, g. ewe, etc. (C.[=ca1890-1900]) <i>(1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary. But see Ibid. above]

>  Old ewes that have lost their teeth. ... <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,50</i>)

>  gummy 2 ... 2 n. NZ & Austral. old ewe with no teeth [GUM 2] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,335</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Ages of sheep. Although there are many variations, the following names are usually used: A sheep is a lamb until weaned. After weaning a lamb is called a hogget until shorn. A male lamb is a ram lamb until castrated, then a wether. A female lamb is a ewe lamb. After shearing, a ewe or ram lamb is usually called a shorn hogget until cutting the first two teeth. It is then called a two-tooth. Some use the term two-tooth as soon as the lamb is shorn. Next year the sheep is a four-tooth, then a six-tooth and then an eight-tooth or full-mouthed sheep. It is full-mouthed as long as the teeth remain perfect but then becomes broken-mouthed and gummy. <i>Ibid.</i> 96 Gummy. An old sheep that has lost all its teeth and reached the end of its useful existence. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i> Splachnidium rugosum. </i> [(?Southland, Stewart Island)]

>  <b>DEWAR</b> Wood was plentiful [in the Southland bush c1900] - broadleaf, gummy, black pine, dry rimu, with easy access to the river. <i>(1953,Chaslands,76</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Splachnidium rugosum</i> GUMMY ... Fawn-brown tufts, darkest at base, the wrinkled branches filled with clear gummy liquid which exudes if the plant is broken. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,314</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Elliptical for gummy-shark (See [rig](../R#rig))

>  <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>. Synonyms: <i>Galeus antarcticus</i> and <i>Galeorhinus antarcticus</i>. The smooth-hound, or gummy (<i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>) ... is one of the small sharks commonly taken by trawlers around new Zealand, and popularly termed `dogfish.' The family is distinguished by the blunt pavement-like teeth - hence the popular name `gummy,' in use in Australia, where the species is also common. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/263</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i> Guenther. GUMMY; MANGA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,7,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i> Guenther. Smooth-hound (gummy, dogfish). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/400</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <i>gummy</i> The name used in Australia for a kind of shark also found in New Zealand waters, a delicacy for the Maoris. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),190,8th ed.</i>)

>  <i>gummy n</i>. Victorian and Tasmanian shark; sheep that has lost its teeth. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  <i>gummy n.</i> Victorian and Tasmanian shark;toothless sheep. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  The dogfish, or gummy shark, can be a metre long and has small teeth ... <i>(Jul 14, 2005,Dominion Post,A9</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> of a kauri-tree, overloaded with kauri gum.

>  <b>REED</b> The `gummy' Kauri, as its name implies, was weighted with an overload of resin, and a log - known as a `sinker' - cut from such a tree could be very troublesome. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,96</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep with sticky or gummy wool, where the grease had failed to rise, as a result of climate or poor animal health.

>  <b>SQUIRE</b> Gummy - that sticky handle typical of greasy merino wool. <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,21</i>)

>  sticky sheep: a gummy. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an aged sheep, without teeth.

>  No broken-mouthed ewes should be put to the ram. I have had gummies give me 100 per cent. <i>(Apr 1908,NZ Farmer,296</i>)

>  After four years a sheep is full-mouthed, failing or gummy. <i>(1919,Livestock in New Zealand,17</i>)

>  Even 'gummy' ewes will rear satisfactory lambs on good pasture. <i>(Dec 1952,NZ Journal of Agriculture,504</i>)

>  One six-year-old ewe may have sound teeth, which, on easy country may ensure her another year of life, but others in the same age group may be 'gummies.' <i>(Jan 30, 1963,Weekly News,39</i>)

>  <b>SQUIRE</b> Gummy - An aged and toothless sheep. <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,21</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Gummies: Old ewes that have lost their teeth. Also term to describe gumboots, rubber boots or wellington boots worn by farmers. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,50</i>)

### gummy /gummie
 
 <i>n.</i> gumboot, rubber boot, or wellington boot. [most commonly used in plural form; note Fiordland friction boot in 2010 citation]

>  As I empty the water out of my gummies and massage some life back into my toes, I take pause to survey the surroundings. <i>(2006,Directions,70,Spring ed.</i>)

>  The names of the 20-plus parts which make up your average gummie are descriptive and dramatic in themselves. <i>(Sep 24, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)

>  <b>SLIGO</b> Doris had been there too, looking tough in gummies and a sweater full of holes. <i>(1987,Final Things,193</i>)

>  1 (farming) old ewes that have lost their teeth 2 (coll) gumboots <i>(1952,Straight Furrow,53</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> gummies: calf high RUBBER BOOTS <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,49</i>)

>  gummy ... 2 n. NZ gum-digger; (in pl.) NZ & Austral. gumboots. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,335</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  When I put on my gummies and take Spotty for a walk along the rocky foreshore I don't want to be anywhere else in the world. <i>(Mar 2003,North & South,122</i>)

>  many urban children rarely get the opportunity to slip on a pair of gummies and spend time on a farm -- let alone get close to some of the animals. <i>(Apr 2001,Elements,12</i>)

>  Who needs damp and clingy gummies as a start to a day's digging? <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Journal of Agriculture,33</i>)

>  Can one imagine Brash in Swanndri and gummies in the National heartland? <i>(Nov 8, 2003,NZ Geographic,24</i>)

>  A number of columns discussing the merits of rubber gummies, or Fiordland friction boots, for tramping also stirred enthusasts to write to me, culminating with Skellerup asking to reprint one column in thier in-house magazine and in return offereing me a pair of gummies ... <i>(Aug 2010,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,34</i>)

>  They [Skellerup Hikers] don't like ice and, unlike gummies, take a bit of effort to dry out. <i>(Aug 2007,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,169/44</i>)

>  Being eternal optimists (and freeloaders) Hype still attended, whisked said cockies off their gummies and sank enough sav blanc to flood the mighty Waikato. <i>(Sep 22, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  Though no records were broken in the big event - biffing a gummy down the main street - thousands of people took advantage of the balmy weather to chuckle at, or occasionally admire, the efforts of the gumboot throwers. <i>(Apr 15, 1998,Dominion,3</i>)

>  We like our gummies as black as the inside of a cow but, more often than not, with a red band. <i>(May 2007,NZ Geographic,60</i>)

>  As we squelched in our gummies alongside a dairy farmer's boggy paddock, I was mystified to see Dave was carrying only a plastic bucket. <i>(Nov 30, 2004,Dominion Post,E8</i>)

### gummy shark
 
 <i>n.</i> aka spotted gummy shark, lemon fish, flake (See [gummy](../G#gummy))

>  The dogfish, or gummy shark, can be a metre long and has small teeth ... <i>(Jul 14, 2005,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The smooth hound (<i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>) is the small spotted dog-fish frequently taken in Lyttelton Harbour and around the coast...is often known as the Gummy on account of its flattened pavement-like teeth. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,192</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 192]

>  <b>POWELL</b> GUMMY SHARK OR DOGFISH (<i>Emissola antarctica</i>), Manga of the Maoris, grows up to 3 1/2 feet in length.... <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,61</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Makoo = mangoo</i>, n. <i>Mustelus antarcticus, gummy shark</i>. [Ngai Tahu] <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,170</i>)

>  <b>BRAMBLEY</b> I lose patience with people who prattle about `gummy' sharks when they mean pioke, or dogfish - `lemon-fish' when it reaches the shops. Instead of teeth, the doggy has cross-hatched boney ridges ... <i>(1966,Sea-cockies of the Manukau,43</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Gummy shark, spotted dogfish .. .. manga, mango .. .. <i>Mustelus lenticulatus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> GUMMY SHARK <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>: These harmless members of the shark family <i>Triakidae</i> live on smooth, sea bottoms ... They are slenderly built and have a fairly smooth skin, and are good eating. Also known as Sweet William Shark, Spotted dogfish, gummy, and by the Maori name `mango' <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,224</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>SPOTTED SMOOTH-HOUND Mustelus lenticulatus</i> (Gummy shark, spotted dogfish, rig, pioke) <i>(1982,Collins Guide,56,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Of the many sharks found in New Zealand waters, the gummy and school sharks are the most commonly eaten. Both are relatively small in size, and are marketed as lemon fish or flake. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,79</i>)

>  [species name] rig .. [scientific name] <i>Mustelus lenticulatus</i> .. [other name(s)] spotted dogfish, gummy shark, kini, pioke, smooth hound, deepsea bream, lemonfish, spotted gummy shark, sweet william, dogi <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Rig Mustelus lenticulatus</i> ... Other names include spotted dogfish, gummy shark, smoothhound and pioke. Often listed as <i>M. antarcticus</i>, but this name now applies only to a similar Australian species. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,30</i>)

>  It's only a gummy shark,' Dad told her. `It hasn't got any teeth.' Anyway, the big fish was already dead. <i>(Aug 29, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),76</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] shark, gummy [TS] 198-200, 214-6 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### gummy-gummy
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a short tree with palatable leaves, favoured by cattle, thought to be Nothopanax or Pseudopanax colensoi, or a corruption of the Maori kamahi (sp Weinmannia). (See [ride the gummy-gummies](../R#ride-the-gummy-gummies))

>  <b>BUCKINGHAM</b> The cattle used also to 'ride the gummy-gummies' which were small trees with wide shiny leaves - good feed, they loved it. They learnt to straddle these trees and push them over so that they could get at the tasty leaves. <i>(1969,From Hethersett to Quarry Hills,44</i>)

>  <b>DEWAR</b> The bullock, replete with gummy leaves, walked actively. <i>(1953,Chaslands,133</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> In the winter months her lactation was helped to spin out from her spring calving with gummy leaves. [gummy] <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,87</i>)

### gummy-weed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Sea Cactus or Gummy-weed (<i>Splachnidium rugosum). </i> <i>(1961,Flora NZ,18,1929 ed.</i>)

### gumrush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> [caption] This Herekino store is a sign of the depressing Northland times after the gumrush. <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,176</i>)

### gun
 
 <i>n.</i> an expert fast shearer, gun shearer; cf. ringer. ?Orig of gun n1. in big gun: .bd;see 1933 Acland: or go great guns [also gun shearer (station and farming)] (See [also gun shearer](../A#also-gun-shearer))

>  In contract sheds only fast men are welcomed by the `boss'. Some men who are what we term `guns,' are kept going all the season, and in many cases good honest shearers are cast off to make room for these `guns.' Of course, the `gun' shearer who comes along does not know that this has happened, as the `gun' shearers are as a rule good unionists, and would not demean themselves by `waiting for a death.' At the same time, the `boss' knows when the gun is cutting out, perhaps 200 miles away, and he knows from the other boss which way the `gun' is going, and everything is in readiness when he arrives at the shed. <i>(Oct 6, 1911,Maoriland Worker,31/17/1</i>)

>  Gun Big gun. A really fast shearer, one who could ring most sheds (see Ringer), e.g. `So-and-so's a gun shearer.' The term is also applied to the fastest shearers in a shed. <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Gun, big gun.-A really fast shearer, one who could ring most sheds. E.g. `So-and-So's a g. shearer.' The term is also applied to the several fastest shearers in a shed. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

>  [Gloss] gun, gun-shearer: a fast shearer. <i>Ibid.</i> 128 ... that wonderful old gun ... was no longer shearing there, but the ringer was Lew Aitken. <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> There have been some great battles in the past and sometimes `gun' shearers, as they are called, used to gather at a particular shed for the express purpose of `having a go' at each other. <i>(1951,High Country,29</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> GUN. A shearer who has reached the very top, and who has outstanding ability. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,156</i>) [NOTES: (glossary: Shearing Terms)]

>  <b>MEEK</b> GUN: A crack shearer. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

>  Gun: A top-class shearer. <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing)]

>  GUN: A top-class shearer. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>)

>  gun: a top class shearer. <i>(Feb 24, 1977,Dominion,11</i>)

>  Gun: Fast shearer of outstanding ability. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> I was up with the `guns' with a top tally of 163 and had my eye on the `ringer' status, the highest and most coveted accolade in any large shed. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,115</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Gun. A fast shearer <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  gun: A really crack fast shearer, but not the same as the fastest in the shed (see `ringer'). In a particular shed there may be several guns or none at all. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/24</i>) [NOTES: - -->1989 NZ English Newsletter 3/24 (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  gun n. champion e.g. gun shearer. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(? obsolesc)]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Lighting up the pipe he had been smoking. `Eating too many cigarettes so took to the old gun, Bert. <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,164</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [AND see n above.] ?Esp. and orig. in gun shearer ?or attrib. use expert; very good. Cf gun chain in freezing works; see gun n1 above, see real gun. [1911 see cit n1.]

>  [excellent (?a bound collocatioon GUN CUTTER)] A gun cutter, swamp lingo for a good man <i>(Mar 11, 1916,NZ Truth,6/7</i>)

>  1933 see n1: Acland cit <i>(1933,Press, The</i>)

>  a gun outfit [=a very good place] [Aviemore Visitors' Book] sv Fantabulous <i>(Oct 15, 1966,Dominion,31</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> ... I often visited him on his coastal farm ... long after our `gun' days were over. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,137</i>)

>  gun, gunny: good, latest <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  gun ... NZ & Austral. fast shearer; also transf., atrib. (a gun Rugby player, a gun cocky). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,335</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 498, sense 6.a and b]]

>  Labour and National have teams of MPs, key staff, party officials and gun strategists, who get up at well before dawn to meet, phone-conference and plan. <i>(Jul 6, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  You don't have to be a gun mountainbiker to get involved in this venture. <i>(Feb 1997,North & South,17</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of a shearer] champion, very fast

>  <b>BARNES</b> Using the machine shears top or "gun" shearers have shorn well over 700 lambs in a day. Donny Hammond is one of those "gun" blade shearers. To really appreciate the skill of a "gun" shearer it should be understood that a beginner will take ten minutes to shear just one sheep. <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),117</i>)

>  Many of the students he has trained have gone on to become full-time shearers, some of them reaching the open class in competitive shearing, a level at which they can be regarded in the 'gun' category. <i>(Dec 5, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> great person

>  "He's a gun," she said. "Absolutely awesome." <i>(Sep 11, 2000,Dominion,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(obs criminals')]

>  There [in the Beautiful City] the `gun' and the `spieler' are banished. <i>(Apr 14, 1906,NZ Truth,43/5/8</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a person expert at any activity; a highly skilled person. [[Cf OED 7b great (big) gun a person of distinction or importance.]]

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> All the `guns' of the tennis world were there, ... <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,90</i>)

>  Though there is no rule existing which debars fielders [i.e. bookmakers] from adopting such pointing tactics, the clubs could easily put a stop to it by refusing to license these guns when they apply the next time for registration. <i>(Oct 29, 1910,NZ Truth,279/2/5</i>)

>  He remembers starting out with [a lambing percentage of] 80 per cent and the days when a farmer who had 100 per cent was considered a "gun". <i>(Aug 8, 2003,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Then he came over and cleaned up all our best `guns' on the pool-table. <i>(1971,Bastards I Have Met,18</i>)

>  [Hdg] Ruckus over Samoa roping in rugby guns. <i>(Jan 26, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [ca 1953 He's a gun at preaching.]

>  <b>HOWITT</b> ... the New Zealand forwards took control and, with Hill one of the guns in the rucks, oulled off a 17-10 victory ... <i>(1975,NZ Rugby Greats,102</i>)

>  <b>TUCKER</b> Having produced my very first stud book in 1948, I was extremely pleased with my performance. I suppose I had begun to regard myself as a sort of `gun guy' in the thoroughbred world. <i>(1978,Thoroughbreds Are My Life,56</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Then he came over and cleaned up all our best `guns' on the pool-table. <i>(1971,Bastards I Have Met,18</i>)

>  I like `Beginnings' because only the `guns at Oakley' [Mental Hospital] suggest to us a setting and explanation for this meandering monologue. <i>(Jun 1984,Landfall,150/131</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> .. a slang language has developed to suit. ... `Crud' (cloud), `bush-bash', a `gunn' tramper (which must have originated from Davy Gunn's famous trek up the Hollyford Valley to help victims of a plane crash in 1936 ...[Not so: HWO]), windfall' (a collapsed pile of trees), `pit' (the tramper's true friend, a sleeping bag), `pog' (porridge), `bastard grass' and `lawyer' (two unpleasant plants), `piker' (one who stayed home), `Huey' (all-powerful god of weather) ... <i>(Mar 1988,Hills, The</i>) [NOTES: [page proofs: Reed Publishing] get cit. if still in pub. work] So to cope with the rituals of tramping .]

>  gun excellent person or thing; eg a gun motor or a gun guitarist; originally a fast shearer. [Acland] NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,53</i>)

### gun hand
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hand that a shearer uses to shear with, to hold blades or handpiece.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> When a shearer called out 'tar-boy' he would run to the stand, grab the stick and make a swipe at the cut. No shearer would pause in his work, so pity help the boy if he missed the cut or put the tar on the shearer's 'gun hand.' <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,44</i>)

### gun shearer
 
 <i>n.</i> an expert shearer [(station and farming)] (See [gun n. & adj](../G#gun-n.-&-adj))

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Gun shearer: A very fast shearer who may be expected to `ring the board', i.e. beat everyone else. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,254</i>) [NOTES: (Gloss)]

>  <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> There I saw some of our greatest gun (fast) shearers in action. <i>(1947,Ways & Byways,39</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Shearing is hard work (probably among the hardest we've got), work that calls for much more than just physical strength and exuberance - rather for balance, grace, rhythm, suppleness, with eye, brain and hand in smart co-ordination. All these are required to make a `gun' shearer. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,1</i>)

>  The organiser of the Golden Fleece championship, the New Zealand fine-wool title event, ... said ... that many of the North Island `gun' shearers would compete in the event at Alexandra on September 27-28. <i>(Sep 9, 1963,Dominion,6</i>)

>  In recognition of this one of the gun shearers received a Queen's Medal. <i>(Jun 16, 1965,Hawke's Bay Herald Tribune,8</i>)

>  He credits his father George, a celebrated "gun" shearer in his youth and later a Southern Hawkes's Bay farm manager, with instilling in him a strong worth ethic. <i>(Jul 11, 2003,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  Gary pere went in for golf after that, but his youngest son became a gun shearer... <i>(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  Trained by his father Mr Hipa Morris of Okautete, Homewood, the 32 year old gun shearer Jim Morris spent most of his earlier years in the atmosphere of the shearing sheds. <i>(Mar 1959,Te Ao Hou,26: 36</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> There's a gun shearer and his wife (she's cook and rousie), two youngsters aiming for their two hundred, two fleecos and a presser. <i>(2001,Ellie and the Shadow Man,88</i>)

>  "Gun shearers" are as a rule good unionists. <i>(Oct 8, 1911,Maoriland Worker,17</i>)

### gun-chain
 
 <i>n.</i> the fast expert chain. [(freezing works) -->ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon A good man is called a gun-man, and the fast chain is called the gun-chain.]

>  [see quot. learners' chain] <i>(Dec 18, 1973,NZ Truth,56/2</i>)

### gun-gun
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [gun](../G#gun))

 <i>n.</i> an expert, fast hand, often on the gun-chain [ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon A good man is called a gun-man, and the fast chain is called the gun-chain 1980 Lynfield College, Auckland (Goldie Brown Collection) gun man: the best in the group]

### gun-shy
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [sp.use ] [of sheepdogs] frightened by gunshot.

>  Whip- or gun-shy dogs are undesirable. <i>(May 1933,NZ Farmer,317</i>)

>  The trouble was that Tim .. was gun shy. <i>(May 10, 1951,NZ Farmer,58</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Quite a few sheep dogs are gun-shy. [gun shy] <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,221</i>)

### gunga
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [gunger](../G#gunger))

>  <b>McGILL</b> gunga vagina or anus, in phrs. `up your gunga' or `stick it up your gunga'; ref. Lyn of Tawa on TVNZ .us Cancer Line, 11 November 1987; possibly from ANZ phr. `bung it in, Gunga Din!', a direction to go ahead, as with pouring concrete. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,53</i>)

### gunger
 
 <i>n.</i> [pron often @/gonger/. ca1946 Nelson Boys' College (J.H. Brownlee) -->ca1955 Wellington (HS Gajadhar) Associated with Gunga Din (`Though I've belted you and flayed you') -->ca 1953 H.S. Gajadhar ? -->1983 Oct 14 C. Corne Letter (Auckland) gunga, gunger [g??g?] - anus, backside probably the same as Part sup: `gonga anus: Services': C20: Origin?'; feetesick wallah (Arabic) or gunga wallah (Hindi); A male prostitute: Services, esp Army C.20; nf W3]

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> Oh, come on. He'd put a knife up your gunga quick as look at you, don't you worry. <i>(1985,Shuriken,34</i>)

### gunn tramper
 
 <i>phr.</i> (see cit.: a mistaken derivation)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> So to cope with the rituals of tramping, its pains and camaraderie, a slang language has developed to suit. Lots of quick short words to fit in the breathless pauses as you pant uphill. `Crud' (cloud), `bush-bash', a `gunn' tramper (which must have originated from Davy Gunn's famous trek up the Hollyford Valley to help victims of a plane crash in 1936; he travelled 40 km by rowing boat, horse and foot in 21 hours), `windfall' (a collapsed pile of trees), `pit' (the tramper's true friend, a sleeping bag), `pog' (porridge), `bastard grass' and `lawyer' (two unpleasant plants), `piker' (one who stayed home), `Huey' (all-powerful god of weather), and many, many more. <i>(1988,Hills, The,8</i>)

### gunny bag
 
 <i>n.</i> A bag made out of gunny; hence a hold-all (?US gunny-sack)

>  A few dozen gunny bags for sale. <i>(Aug 29, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... one [advertisement in Auckland] may be cited for the purpose of piquing the reader's curiosity, `On sale, Three bales of Gunny Bags!' <i>Ibid.</i> 2/563 [List of dutiable goods] ... linen manufactures (except corn and gunny bags and woolpacks.), ... <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/208</i>)

>  A disgraceful and malicious attempt had been made to damage the little river steamer Expert, by tightly stuffing the boiler flues with wet gunny bags, so as to resist the fire for a long time. <i>(Mar 23, 1861,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  <b>CARRICK</b> Laden with gunny bags, the fire gradually gained on them, ... <i>(1892,New Zealand's Lone Lands,39</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... sod walls with flies of gunny-bags sewn together. <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,113</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a nickname for an early Wellington social set.

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Social distinctions here [in Wellington] are rather amusing. They have the `Ice Creams,' the `Jam Tarts,' and the [58] `Gunny Bags.' I have joined the Ice Creams [sic] assemblies, which have dances fairly often in the old provincial council room. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,57,1935 ed.</i>)

### guri

>  The man who wrote such chirpy plain-folks ballads as "Talking Dog" and "Down the Hall on Saturday Night", with their frequent references to blokes, sheilas and tying up the guri, could also describe coming out of the priesthood as a "slow and somewhat bitter reconciliation to secular life in the secular world". <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

### gurnard
 
 <i>n.</i> Any of a group of small, sluggish, usually red fish which have spikes, a bony case around the head and often large, coloured wing-like fins. Also called kumukumu. [(usu. the red gurnard). Early and variant spg also <i>gurnet</i>.]

>  (Queen Charlotte Sound) Sometimes we got a sort of small Salmon, [ed. fn <i>?Arripis trutta</i>.] Gurnards, [ed. fn <i>Chelidonichthys kumu</i>.] Skate [ed. fn <i>Raja nasuta</i>.] and Nurses [ed. fn Three genera are found here, and it would be unwise to plump for one.] ... spg gurnet <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/808,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/808]

>  <b>YATE</b> Those saltwater fish] most plentiful, and of greatest note, are, soles, mackarel, cod-fish, a species of salmon, whiting, snapper, mullet, bream, skate, gurnards, and a few smaller kinds, some not near so large as a sprat; with an abundance of crayfish, oysters, shrimps, prawns, muscles, and cockles. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),71</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other fish are equally numerous, answering to our <i>hakes, tench, bream, snapper, haddock, elephant-fish, pollock, salmon, gurnards, pipe-fish, parrot-fish, leather-jackets, cole-fish, John Dorys, sword-fish, cod; various kinds of skate and cat-fish, sting-ray and dog-fish. </i> <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/322</i>)

>  [see quot THE BAY] <i>(1840,Journal of Ensign Best,222</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The bream, ling, gurnard, herring, sole, rock-cod, and a species of mackarel, are all in abundance, as are also many fish unknown elsewhere [than Wellington]. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),49</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Of the Fam. <i>Cottoideoe</i>, there are six enumerated, of these the <i>Gurnard (kumu-kumu)</i> is most common, and the <i>Trigla Volitans (korama)</i> the most beautiful. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,624</i>)

>  42. GURNARD. ... The Red Gurnard or Kumukumu (<i>Trigla kumu</i>) is very abundant during the summer months in the harbours in the north, ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,113</i>)

>  A few other fishes occasionally come to market, such as the Gurnard (<i>Trigla kumu</i>), a pretty fish ... <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/486</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  [Maori or Settlers' Name] Kumukumu, Gurnard .. [Scientific Name] <i>Trigla kumu</i> <i>(1878,TrNZI,10/326</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  Occasionally a few Gurnard, <i>Trigla kumu</i>, may be seen in the shops,... <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/381</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> GURNARD (<i>Trigla kumu</i>). ... [37] The red gurnard, or kumukumu, is very abundant during the summer months in the harbours of the North, ... <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,36</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kumukumu, n. trigla kumu, gurnard</i> a fish. <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,78</i>)

>  16. <i>Moeraki</i>. - Rock-cod, gurnard, parrot-fish, butter-fish, sprats, conger-eel <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/204</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <i>Gurnet</i> ... [<i>Gurnard</i> cross-referenced to<i> Gurnet</i>] The species of <i>Trigla</i> found in British waters, called <i>Gurnards</i> are of the family of <i>Cottidae.</i> The word <i>Gurnet</i> is an obsolete or provincial form of Gurnard, revived in Australia, and applied to the fish <i>Centropogon scorpaenoides</i>, Guich., family <i>Scorpaenidae</i>. The original word <i>Gurnard</i> is retained in New Zealand, and applied to the new species <i>Trigla kumu (kumu</>being the Maori name), family <i>Cottidae</i>. The <i>Flying Gurnet</i> is <i>Trigla polyommata</i>, Richards., found on all the Australian coasts from New South Wales to Western Australia, family <i>Cottidae</i>. It is a distinct species, not included in the British species. They have large pectoral fins, but are not known to possess the power of supporting themselves in the air like the `flying fish' which belong to other genera. Sir Fredk. McCoy says that <i>Sebastes Percoides</i>, Richards., is called Gurnet, or Garnet-perch, by the fishermen and dealers, as well as the more common <i>Neosebastes scorpaenoides,</i> Guich., and <i>Scorpaena panda</i>, Richards. <i>(1898,Austral English,187</i>)

>  In other parts king-fish in abundance, red cod, soles, and flounders were found, and large numbers of elephant-fish, gurnard, and schnapper, ... <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/574</i>)

>  <i>Chelidonichthys kumu</i>: Gurnard or red gurnard. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kumukumu, Chelidonichthys kumu, gurnard</i>, a fish. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,156</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> GURNARD (a) Scientific name: <i>Chelidonichthys kumu</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: N.Z. Latchett, N.Z. Latchett Fillets; Gurnard. (c) Other common names: Flathead, Shoal or Latchett or N.Z. F.F. for the prepared state of frozen fillets for export. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,27</i>)

>  <i>gurnard</i> ... also <i>kukukumu</i> [sic for kumukumu see p. 274] <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,220</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  [species name] gurnard .. [scientific name] <i>Chelidonichthys kumu</i> .. [other name(s)] red gurnard, latchett [sic], N.Z. latchett <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Gurnards ... They are good at producing sound and may grunt when captured. New Zealand has five species.<i> Red gurnard Chelidonichthys kumu </i>... Often simply called gurnard, being the commonest species seen in New Zealand. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,80</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] gurnard 1201-24 GURNARDS 1199-226 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> ... very fine fish they are [off Whangarei] - schnappers, gurnet and what is called salmon in these seas. <i>(Apr 20, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 20 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 121]

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> Gurnets are plentiful everywhere, ... Whiting, bream, and snappers are abundant, with several kinds of skate. <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,34</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> They had various kinds of fish [in Queen Charlotte Sound], especially fine mullets and gurnets, which they offered for sale, ... <i>(1835,Travels in NZ,1/35</i>)

>  (quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5) English Name Native Name .. ... Gurnet kumukumu New Zealand Salmon kahawai Guardfish Takeke Sprat or Sardine Aua Flounder Patiki Herring Tawa tawa Yellow tail Haku ... ... <i>(1849,NZ Journal,9/125</i>)

>  Of the sea-fishes that are used as food in New Zealand, we have among the constant residents on all parts of our coast the hapuku, tarakihi, trevally, moki, aua, rock-cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butterfish, and red-cod are confined to the south. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Gurnet ... [Gurnard</i> cross-ref'ed here: see a. above.] <i>(1898,Austral English,187</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The warehou, tarakihi, and gurnet are common in the north, and the moki, butterfish, and frost fish in the south, while the kahawai, hapuku (the groper of Dunedin), blue cod, barracouta, the little sea mullet, sometimes called the herring, and the flat fish are found all round the coast. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,71</i>)

>  OYSTER SOUP ... Oysters ... Gurnet or Rock Cod ... Carrot ... Celery ... 1 Anchovy ... Lemon Juice ... DEFIANCE BUTTER ... DEFIANCE DRIED MILK ... Herbs ... Salt and Pepper. <i>(1905,'Defiance' Cookery Recipes,8</i>)

>  <i>gurnet</i>: edible fish. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>GLOVER</b> Sometimes snapper, groper, whiting, crab,/ Trevalli, gurnet, cod, will grace it./ <i>(1974,Wellington Harbour,33</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> fn Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, haddocks, pollock, salmon, gurnets, john dory, hakes, bream, sword-fish, elephant-fish, diodons, pipe-fish, cod, parrot-fish, flounders, skate, cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. - Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called para[as-acute] paraua[a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale, mungu nui (black physeter); right whale, tohora (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis); sun-fish (diodon mola); large-lipped whale (balaena mysticetus); large-lipped whale (balaena musculus); hump-back (balaena gibbosa); shark, mango (squalus); hammer-head (zygaena); pilots, (scomber); flying-fish (exocaetus volitans); and an infinity of others, besides a great variety of testaceous and crustaceous fishes that almost cover the rocks under water, such as clams, muscles, oysters, crays, cuttles, limpets, wilks, gigantic muscles, a foot in length, cockles, sea-ears, sea-eggs, star-fish, mutton-fish, &c.; from the latter the pearl is procured which they insert in the eyes of their carved images and boxes. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> Amongst the sea-fishes fit for food there are ... aua, rock cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butter-fish, and red-cod are confined to the south. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,288</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] gurnet 1203, 1224 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>POATA</b> <i>(1919,Maori as a Fisherman, The,14</i>) [NOTES: TE KUMUKUMU (or Gurnett).]



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Lepidotrigla brachyoptera</i> Hutton, scaly gurnard.

>  <i>Lepidotrigla Brachyoptera</i> ... Gurnard. <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/255</i>)

>  <i>Lepidotrigla brachyoptera</i> Hutton. This gurnard is not uncommon. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/230</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  LEPIDOTRIGLA BRACHYOPTERA (Hutton). <i> Gurnard</i>. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/123</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Lepidotrigla brachyoptera</i> Hutton. GURNARD. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,54,1971 ed.</i>)

>  49. <i>Lepidotrigla brachyoptera</i> Hutton. Gurnard. [distinguished from 48. prec. C. kumu Red Gurnard] <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/149</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <i>Lepidotrigla brachyoptera</i> Hutton. Gurnard. [=SCALY GURNARD] <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/417</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  Gurnard <i>Lepidotrigla brachyoptera</i> Hutton <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,112</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 112]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GURNARD <i>Lepidotrigla brachyoptera</i> Hutton <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,363,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE GURNARD <i>Lepidotrigla brachyoptera</i> (Hutton) <i>Ibid..</i> 168 Usually taken by trawl the Red Gurnard, [?Ch. kumu] together with the Gurnard, [?L. brachyoptera] are sold in the shops under the general name of `Gurnard'. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,165</i>)

### gut
 
 <i>n.</i> a narrow newly-formed watercourse; a steep-sided erosion gully. (The word is general English, and in New Zealand seems confined to hill-country usage: perhaps omit: [(? station;? hill country) -->]

>  <b>PAULIN</b> The flood had cut a gut 20 feet deep and half a chain in width through the bush. <i>(1899,Wild West Coast,105</i>)

>  Gut. (1) To take the paunch out of a rabbit. (C.) (2) Narrow, newly formed watercourse wit very hard, steep sides. A shepherd's word. They sometimes call it a shoot. [Sometimes, a guts.--A[rnold] W[all].] <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> A ten foot gut. <i>(1907,Tracks  We Tread,161</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The lower portion of the ace was scarred with rocky guts. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,83</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Jack ... moved along to the next gut, where he set another two [snares]. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,55</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> The flexible drive-shaft of a shearing machine encased in a downtube [(shearers')]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> At times a gut will break. Every shed should have spare guts on hand so that such a mishap may not cause a long hold-up. New guts can be stored for protection in a piece of pipe, for rats have a special taste for new gut cores. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,100</i>)

>  Gut: Flexible drive shaft of shearing machine which is encased in downtube. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> In NZ, not only of fish (cf OED v1 to take the guts out of (fish); to eviscerate) but more specifically of animals after slaughter.

>  Gut. (1) To take the paunch out of a rabbit.(C.) (2) Narrow, newly formed watercourse wit very hard, steep sides. A shepherd's word. They sometimes call it a shoot. [Sometimes, a guts.--A.W.] <i>(1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the flexible drive-shaft of a shearing machine encased in a downtube. (See [chute; chimney; staircase](../C#chute;-chimney;-staircase))

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> When I started up the motor I checked each of the four stands to make sure all was well when to my horror, all the guts on the shearing arms wound themselves backwards. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,148</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> At times a gut will break. Every shed should have spare guts on hand so that such a mishap may not cause a long hold-up. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,100</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Gut: Flexible drive-shaft of shearing machine which is encased in downtube. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering and hunting] a high steep narrow chute between rocks, where sheep or deer can pass [often at the top of a shingle fan and that can fill with fast-moving water.] (See [chute; chimney; staircase](../C#chute;-chimney;-staircase))

>  <b>PORTER</b> Following up some deer, we had to drop into a small gut, just a step down and another up the other side. <i>(1970,Under the Nor'West Arch,61</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> .. the dog brought in half a dozen sheep from a blind gut just above me. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,51</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> No one knew that a cloudburst the month before had scoured guts down the face depositing rocks large and small through the tussocks. [gutter] <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,112</i>)

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> What the high-country folk call 'guts' or 'gutters' - deep, steeply-inclined gullies - are the most striking form of soil wastage .. Neither sheep nor shepherd can cross the gutters without difficulty and risk of serious mishap. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,73</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> We coaxed and pleaded with Bill to come across the little gut. <i>(1966,Over the River,163</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> They were in a narrow gut from which a small shingle slide descended. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,143</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Gut: steep-sided gully eroded out of the hillside, a chute. Gutter: alternative term for a gut. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,37</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> At Glenrock there's a broken wall of bluffs up from the farm, with guts separating knobs, and behind that a tussocky plateau I've only been told about. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,40</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Out of scrubby guts, from high terraces, out of the bush and from the large snow tussocks on the river bed they came <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,56</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> The hunters came to the head of a very steep and tapering gut, a way down with sheer faces on either side. <i>(1996,Great Hunting Yarns,180</i>)

### gut floor
 
 <i>n.</i> [(freezing works)]

>  Till this season men did this work on the gut floor but it was switched to the viscera table to meet new Agriculture Department regulations. <i>(Nov 2, 1972,Dominion,1/9</i>)

### gut(s)ful
 
 <i>n.</i> have had a g. of: to be fed up with. [Orsman DNZE: [Used elsewhere but esp. freq. in NZ: see OED gutful `dial. and slang '.] a A bellyfull; esp. in the phr. to have a gutsful (of), to have had more than enough (of), to be fed up with (a person, situation, etc.).]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> .... The boys have had a gutful of this. You know what they thought of the last performance. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I'd had a gutsful. I was off home. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,67</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Three months ... saw Jack with a gutful of forestry. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,178</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> Let them go somewhere else if they don't like it here, I've had a gutsful of them. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,91</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Don't talk to me about them bastards. I've had a gutsful of doctors in my time. There's only one thing they want, and that's everyone in the country busy dying on social security. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,74</i>)

>  To put it vulgarly ... I've had a gutsful of the whole horrible business. <i>(Jun 25, 1972,Sunday Times (NZ),36/3</i>)

>  The former Speaker Sir Roy Jack, was not to be mistaken for his meaning when he said he had had a `gutsful' of demonstrations <i>(Sep 11, 1972,Dominion,4/2</i>)

>  He said that he had had a gutsful of his neighbour who was always complaining about his dog. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>) [NOTES: (Sinclair `At the Point')]

>  <b>McGILL</b> gutsful more than enough; phr. had a gutsful; eg `I've had a gutsful of this party. Let's go.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,53</i>)

>  "He sees himself as a law unto himself and even the criminal fraternity have had a gutsful," Mr grimstone said. <i>(Jun 8, 2006,NZ Herald,A1</i>)

### gut-bash
 
 <i>v.</i> to have a good meal [ca1951 Wellington Teachers College (C.D. Orsman) - also as noun]

### gut-shoot
 
 <i>v.</i> [(hunters)]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> He ... shouted ... that he'd gut-shoot the first of us to come near the hut. <i>Ibid.</i> 122 I was lucky to drop the stag and gut-shoot another hind before they reached the bush. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,33</i>)

### gutbuster
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Gurney blasts attitude of 'amateur' gutbusters.... Multi-sport legend Steve Gurney says ill-prepared athletes are causing unnecessary "paranoia" in his sport. <i>(Feb 1, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> gutbuster a mountain likely to bust your gut because so difficult; mostly trampers' term. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,53</i>)

>  Although he will skirt the lurking presence of the lake during the annual gutbuster, he will also in so many ways be dipping his toes into the waters. <i>(Apr 22, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B16</i>)

### gutless wonder
 
 <i>phr.</i> Of a weak or inefficient worker or person; or a person with pretentions to doing a great job. [ca1949 at least Havelock]

### guts buster
 
 <i>n.</i> [(trampers')] (See [gutbuster](../G#gutbuster))

>  <b>TURNER</b> A steep hill on the bush track to the Wellington student's tramping club hut is (or used to be) known as the G. B. or guts buster, and the name is used for other hills by trampers. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,120</i>)

### guts it out
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  The guys gutsed it out, absorbed a lot of pressure and had the presence of mind to take their opportunities and strike when they arose. <i>(May 7, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

>  I thought the guys gutsed it out pretty well and in the last 20 or 30 minutes we started showing we'd done the work. <i>(Jun 1, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

>  You've got to be disciplined and just be able to guts it out when you have to. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Evening Post,27</i>)

### gutser
 
 <i>n.</i> a greedyguts, a hog (? here used figuratively).

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> ... in his own vernacular that judgement was: `A gutzer for women. Flash joker'. <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,52</i>)

>  gutser glutton. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,53</i>)

### gutsful
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (See [worth](../W#worth))

>  <b>SLATTER</b> No-one cares what happens to me. They're not worth a gutsful of cold water. To hell with the lot of them. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,207</i>)

### gutter
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>WALKER</b> We found yesterday our next door neighbours had driven into our wall a good gutter and I believe got a good 9dwt. piece out while we were away on Saturday. <i>(Jan 21, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>) [NOTES: (Central Otago)]

>  ... we arrived at a miry gutter, which we crossed by a rough foot-bridge. <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,13</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  The Alice Fell Company are now working three shifts and hope soon to be on the gutter. <i>(Sep 10, 1890,Evening Post,3</i>) [NOTES: (Linkwater, Mahakipawa)]

>  ~ n. in mining, an ancient buried water-course containing gold-bearing material. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> I was on gold all the time. I had sunk my first prospect hole at the lower end of a shallow depression ... and had struck the deepest part of the lead. It was a narrow gutter, no more than a foot wide at the most, and rich in heavy gold. <i>(1946,There Was Gold,69</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> This claim, with a gutter length of 1200 feet, was taken up in 1888. <i>Ibid..</i> 99 The gold was said to be clearly visible in the gutter as the water rushed in [from a collapsed shaft]. <i>(1962,Linkwater,95</i>)

>  ~ n. lowest portion of a gold lead. (goldminers') <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> J. F. Byrne & Co. showed off their wares by the light of six kerosene chandeliers and advertised, THE REAL HOMEWARD BOUNDER!/An ounce to the Dish can be Saved/ BY BUYING YOUR/CLOTHING AND BOOTS AT THE CORINTHIAN HALL/ NEW RUSH NEW RUSH /Peg out your claims at the Corinthian Hall If you want to strike a good lead/Dead on the gutter/ BUY YOUR CLOTHING AND BOOTS AN THE CORINTHIAN HALL/ <i>Ibid.</i> 527 GUTTER: the lowest portion of a lead which contains the richest auriferous dirt. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,329,1962 ed.</i>)

>  gutter n. lowest portion of a gold lead. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> guttie

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> The gutter or gutty guts, that is , extracts the guts from the carcase, which is hard because he may get maggots under the nails, that is, fat under the nails which affects them. The gutter drops guts and throws the lights down. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> In NZ often informally (?and ignorantly) applied to the downpipe from the eaves to the ground (?as part of the `guttering' complex).



 
 <i>n.</i> [Omit as one of Thatcher's puns on guttering gaslight in the gutter.]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> In London the gas is lit up every night, But the thing that's called gas here don't give any light; The gutter at home, and the gutter out here, Are things widely different, to all is quite clear. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No.3/88</i>) [NOTES: London and the Diggings]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a narrow deep gully or gut in hill and high country.

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> What the high-country folk called 'guts' or 'gutters' - deep steeply-inclined gullies - are the most striking forms of soil wastage ... <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,73</i>)

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> Neither sheep nor shepherd can cross the gutters without difficulty and risk of serious mishap. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,74</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> gut (?from the same root sene). [(station and more general term)]

>  <b>15</b> Gutter. Alternative name for gut (2). <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Gutter.-Alternative name for the gut (2) (q.v.) <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>I DAVEY (Whangarei)</b> <i>(Jul 29, 1966,Letter</i>) [NOTES: GUTTER, trampers' term for a creek, stream, small river of mountain variety. See Pascoe's `West Coast gutters from the main divide']

### guttie
 
 <i>n.</i> freezing-worker [ca1951 Foxton Freezing Works (C. Gordon)]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> GUTTIES (Must also do Pluck) <i>(1975,Log of a Superfluous Son, The,42</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an employee in a freezing works gut-house.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Guttie: Employee in a freezing-works gut-house. There the paunches, intestines etc are separated and sent their various ways. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,38</i>)

### gutting
 
 <i>adj.</i> making one feel gutted

>  "I certainly wasn't expecting it. It was a bit gutting having played all my rugby down here to find I wasn't wanted," Nutbrown said. <i>(Oct 12, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,16</i>)

### gutty
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> gutty a whitebait that has lived in freshwater a while and no longer has a transparent gut. [G. Johnston] <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,40</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a whitebait

>  He explained that he was getting rid of the "gutties" - the whitebait that, due to small tides, had taken a long time to get up the river and in the process developed tiny guts. <i>(Oct 30, 2004,Dominion Post,E8</i>)

### gutzer
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [gutser](../G#gutser))

### guy
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Guy. 7. Whence, in Australia and, by 1920, in New Zealand, `a foolish fellow', C.J. Dennis: from ca. 1910. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,364</i>)

### guyess
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Not only was Matheson the little guyess who, against the odds, took on the big boys of the hospital system, but her life herstory mapped the struggle of many New Zealand women born into an era when they had to subjugate their lives both to their marital partner and the male state. <i>(Mar 31, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [gyver](../G#gyver))

 <i>n.</i> feed

 <i>abbr.</i> Greater Wellington Regional Council

And GWRC Sources privatelt say it is hard to see the LGNZ's submission as favouring the bill. <i>(Jul 22, 2004,Wellingtonian,6</i>)

### gyle
 
 <i>n.</i> a beer vat [in SOED (brewing term), but not in sense 'vat']

>  At the Speight's brewery in Otago, magnificent kauri gyles - or vats - made for Speight's by coopers in 1945 were dusted off after 30 years in storage to create a range of Porter and a new Pilsener, Pale Ale and a brew dubbed Distinction Ale. It's one of only two breweries in the world to use a wooden gyle - the other is in England. <i>(Nov 9, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> [(WW2)]

>  On leave in Cairo the troops sampled the varied delights of the city - the first `Gyppo' beer, the first `Gyppo' tummy, the Muski, ... the wonders of the Cairo Museum with all its golden treasures. <i>(Sep 26, 1979,Evening Post,27</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>gypsophila [s] Gypsophila paniculata</i> L. <i>clammy gypsophila... gypsophila, clammy [s] Gypsophila australis</i> (Schlecht.) A. Gray <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,35</i>)

### gypsy day
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the day that sharemilkers move herds, usually on foot, and belongings from one farm property to another (usually June 1) [DB notes: 'gypsy days' plural is more correct in Waikato and Taranaki - they often start around May 29]

>  Tomorrow is gypsy day for the dairy industry, the first day of the new season, and the day when sharemilkers moving farms will shift their families, belongings, farm equipment and herds to their new properties. <i>(May 31, 2005,Ashburton Guardian,-</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  Tens of thousands of New Zealand dairy cows hit the road last week - 'gypsy day' for the dairy industry, when sharemilkers move their families, belongings and herd from their old farm to their new one. <i>(Jun 2004,Wairarapa Midweek</i>) [NOTES: 8-9 June issue]

>  I expect gypsy day to be a busier time on the roads this year ... <i>(May 31, 2005,Waikato Times,3</i>)

>  In traditional terminology, June 1 is 'Gypsy Day' for the dairy industry ... <i>(Jun 2, 2005,NZ Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>Jochems, Bridgette</b> Many stock truck drivers call 1 June Gypsy Day. <i>(2001,Chewing the Cud,43</i>)

>  Gypsy Day is entrenched in dairy farming culture but discussion has started within the industry about whether there are less disruptive ways to move farms. There was agreement that Gypsy Day was incredibly disruptive for rural communities and schools. <i>(Apr 6, 2015,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

### gypsy kettle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>H.WEEKES</b> ... camp oven and gypsy kettles. <i>(1842,Journal,IV/3</i>)

>  Our cooking, as with most early settlers, was now an open-air affair with camp-oven and gypsy kettles. <i>(1940,Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement,118</i>) [NOTES: c1846 Weekes My Island in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 118]
